To everyone’s surprise, however, the Prince only smiled, stuck the pocket-handkerchief into Monsieur Reverend’s kalpag, cocked it rakishly on the ambassador’s head, and said to him with peculiar bonhomie﻿—

“So you have succeeded in seducing my wife, eh?”

Reverend laughed awkwardly at what was a rather ambiguous jest so far as he was concerned.

“Me, however, you shall not seduce,” added Apafi, smiling.

Reverend bowed deeply; then, throwing back his head, he observed archly﻿—

===Chinese===

"*Parbleu*（哎呀），先生！"阿帕菲好不容易忍住笑，答道，"只要咱们的谈话里把政治放逐出去，我随时乐意见到您。可您没系披巾；马扎尔装束缺了披巾，那模样活脱脱像个忘了穿马裤的法国人。"

说着，亲王拿出一条缀宝石的披巾，亲手围在勒韦朗先生那可敬的腰身上。

"还有这是怎么说的？谁教您把手帕塞在裤袋里的？只有海杜克（heyduke）仆役才那样。搞什么名堂！贵族老爷的手帕向来是插在卡尔帕格（kalpag）帽里的。瞧！咳！您这手帕多漂亮！"

"漂亮得很，是不是？"

"可不是嘛！丝线绣的花环花样，四角还绣着金银线。这样的货色，只有巴黎才产得出来。"

"可它偏偏是特兰西瓦尼亚造的。"

"真的？"

"真的；而且就在这个地方，就在埃贝什法尔瓦。"

阿帕菲惊异地望着勒韦朗先生。

"这样一双能绣出如此妙物的巧手，我竟会不知道！"

"殿下是知道的。那位女艺人的芳名，就绣在手帕的边上，用的是华丽的哥特式花体字母。"

阿帕菲把手帕的四角挨个儿细看。每一角绣着不同的图样——这边是一顶橡叶花环，那边是一组兵器饰徽，第三角是一柄土耳其弯刀、一把匈牙利马刀和一柄法国剑，用一条缎带束在一起。最后他看到第四角：在一顶亲王冠冕下面，绣着*Apafiné*（阿帕菲之妻）26几个字。

亲王把那名字念出了声。周围的人都屏息盯着阿帕菲的脸，仿佛等着他雷霆大发。不料亲王只是笑了笑，把手帕塞进勒韦朗先生的卡尔帕格帽里，把帽子潇洒地往大使头上一歪，带着特别的和蔼对他说——

"这么说，您把我的妻子勾引上手了，嗯？"

这话对勒韦朗来说颇有点双关的意味，他笑得颇为尴尬。

"不过我本人，您可是勾引不动的。"阿帕菲微笑着补了一句。

勒韦朗深深一躬；随后一仰头，狡黠地说——

===Original===

“That will be brought about also, I hope, though by mightier than I.”

At that moment the door opened and a servant announced﻿—“Her Highness, Dame Anna of Bornemissa, his Highness’s consort, desires an audience of the Prince.”

Apafi looked at Teleki.

“This is all your doing.”

Teleki calmly replied﻿—“It is, your Highness.”

“You have besieged us in form?”

“I do not deny it, your Highness.”

“It was you who brought the ambassador to the Princess?”

“Such is indeed the case, your Highness.”

“And it was you who then advised him to present himself in this masquerade in order to lure me hither more easily?”

“I did it all, your Highness.”

“Then you have done a very foolish thing, Master Michael Teleki.”

“That remains to be seen, your Highness,” replied the minister proudly, conscious of his own intellectual superiority.

Meanwhile Dame Apafi had entered the room; her princely robes well became her princely aspect. All the gentlemen present hastened forward to do her homage. But Apafi also advanced quickly towards her, put his arm through hers, and with marked tenderness endeavoured to lead her into his cabinet.

“No; let us remain here,” cried the Princess; “there will be plenty of time later on to look at your Dutch clocks. Far more serious matters claim our attention first. These gentlemen from Hungary desire an audience.”

Apafi exploded at once.

“I know beforehand what they want, and I have declared once for all that I will hear no more of the matter.”

“But you will surely listen to me. I too am an Hungarian woman, and in the name of my fatherland I implore the Prince of Transylvania for help. None shall say that I rule the Prince in secret. Look now, I advance openly before his throne, and I beg of him protection for Hungary, whose sons are called strangers in Transylvania, though I, her daughter, am the Princess.”

From Apafi’s looks it was clear that he would much rather have listened to the Hungarian gentlemen than to his own consort. But he was caught in a trap. She stood before him as a petitioner. There was no escape.

===Chinese===

"这件事也总会成的，我希望——尽管得靠比我更有本事的人。"

正在这时，门开了，一个仆人通报——"殿下，安娜·博尔内米莎夫人、殿下的元妃，请求觐见。"

阿帕菲看向泰莱基。

"这全是你的手笔。"

泰莱基从容答道——"正是，殿下。"

"你这是把我们正式围了城？"

"臣不否认，殿下。"

"把大使引见给亲王夫人的，也是你？"

"确是如此，殿下。"

"后来劝他穿上这身化装行头来、好更容易把我诱到这儿来的，也是你？"

"全是臣做的，殿下。"

"那你可干了件天大的蠢事，米哈伊·泰莱基老爷。"

"成败尚待分晓，殿下。"首相答道，对自己的智谋高出众人一筹满怀自觉。

这时阿帕菲夫人已走进厅来；亲王的华服正配她亲王的气度。在场的老爷们全都趋前行礼。可阿帕菲也快步迎上去，挽住她的手臂，明显带着万般温存，想把她引进自己的私室。

"不；我们就留在这儿。"亲王夫人喊道，"要看你的荷兰钟，往后有的是工夫。有正经得多的事，得先劳我们的神。这些匈牙利来的老爷们请求觐见。"

阿帕菲当场就炸了。

"他们要什么我早就知道，我已经断言过，这件事我再也一个字不听了。"

"可你总得听我说。我也是匈牙利女子，我以父亲之邦的名义，恳求特兰西瓦尼亚亲王施以援手。谁也不许说我暗地里支配亲王。瞧吧，我光明正大地站到他的座前，为匈牙利乞求庇护——她的儿子们在特兰西瓦尼亚被唤作外人，而我，她的女儿，却是这里的亲王夫人。"

从阿帕菲的神色看得出，他宁可去听匈牙利老爷们的，也不想听自己夫人的。可他落进了圈套。她以请愿人的身份立在他面前。无处可逃。

===Original===

Teleki bade the pages in waiting at the door admit no one else. Apafi, with a gesture of impatience, sat down in an armchair, and resigned himself to listen to his consort; but Anna had scarcely commenced to speak, when the rattling of a coach was heard in the courtyard, and shortly afterwards heavy footsteps resounded in the corridors, and a stern, dictatorial voice, with which everyone appeared to be familiar, asked if the Prince was in. The pages said *No*, and tried to stop the intruder, but exclaiming, “Out of my way, you brats!” he burst open the door and forced his way into the room. It was none other than Denis Banfi.

He had just descended from his carriage. His cheeks were much redder than usual, and his eyes sparkled. He went straight towards the Prince and cried, without the slightest preamble﻿—

“Do not listen to these gentlemen, your Highness! Do not listen to a single word.”

The Prince smiled and greeted Banfi.

“God preserve you, my cousin,” said he.

“Pardon me, your Highness, if in my great haste I neglected to salute you; but when I heard that the Hungarian gentlemen were here in audience, I was quite beside myself with rage. What do you want?” continued he, turning towards the Hungarians; “not satisfied, I suppose, with ruining your own country with your unruliness, you must needs come hither to disturb us likewise?”

“You speak of us,” remarked Teleki, with quiet sarcasm, “as if we belonged to some outlandish Tartar stock, and as if we had been cast hither from heaven only knows what sort of savage, distant land.”

“On the contrary, I know you only too well, ye Hungarian lords. I speak of you as men whose turbulence has, time out of mind, been ruinous to Transylvania. The people of Hungary are idiots one and all.”

“I beg you not to lose sight of the fact that I too am one of them,” said the Princess.

“I know it; and it is with anything but satisfaction that I see the will of your Highness predominant here.”

Dame Apafi, with an expression of wounded dignity, turned towards her brother-in-law.

“Whatever you may say, I will not cease to be your good kinswoman and well-wisher,” and with these words she quitted the room.

“You might at least have addressed the Prince more becomingly,” remarked Teleki, sharply.

===Chinese===

泰莱基吩咐守在门口的侍童再放任何人进来。阿帕菲不耐烦地摆摆手，坐进一张扶手椅，认命地听夫人说下去；可安娜刚开了个头，院里就响起辚辚车声，不多时走廊里回荡起沉重的脚步声，一个听来人人耳熟的、专断严厉的嗓音问亲王在不在。侍童说*不在*，想拦住这位不速之客，他却喝一声"滚开，你们这群小崽子！"撞开房门，硬闯进屋来。来者不是别人，正是德尼斯·班菲（Denis Banfi）。

他刚从车上下来的。两颊比平时红得多，双眼放光。他径直奔向亲王，开门见山地嚷道——

"别听这些老爷们的，殿下！一个字也别听。"

亲王微笑着向班菲见礼。

"上帝保佑你，我的表亲。"他说。

"恕罪，殿下，我来得匆忙，失了礼数；可一听匈牙利老爷们在这儿觐见，我简直气昏了头。你们要干什么？"他转向匈牙利人接着说，"闹得自己的国家败了还不够，想必还要上这儿来搅扰我们不成？"

"您说起我们来，"泰莱基冷冷地讥讽道，"倒像我们是哪门子外邦鞑靼种，像我们是从天知道什么蛮荒远地给抛到这儿来的。"

"恰恰相反，你们这些匈牙利老爷，我认识得太透了。我说你们这号人，无法无天，自古至今都是特兰西瓦尼亚的祸根。匈牙利人个个都是傻子。"

"请您别忘了，我也是他们中的一个。"亲王夫人说。

"我知道；眼见殿下的意志在这儿说一不二，我心里可一点不痛快。"

阿帕菲夫人带着尊严受了伤害的神情，转向这位妹夫。

"随您怎么说，我还是您的好亲戚、好心人，这一点我不会变。"说罢，她离开了房间。

"您对亲王说话，至少该有个体统。"泰莱基尖刻地说。

===Original===

“Have I then spoken one word to the Prince?” asked Banfi, shrugging his shoulders. “How can I even reach his Highness when you are always standing in the way? I am and always will be the enemy of those who have no right whatever to stand on the steps of the throne, and you are one of them, Master Michael Teleki. Oh, don’t imagine that the reasons which make you so enthusiastic in the Hungarian cause are hidden from me. You are not content with being the first in Transylvania after the Prince; you would fain become Palatine of Hungary27 as well. Ha! ha! how you all befool one another. The French promise aid to the Hungarians; the Hungarians promise Teleki the dignity of Palatine; Teleki promises Apafi a kingly crown, and ye lie, the whole lot of you; ye deceive and are deceived.”

“Sir,” replied Teleki, bitterly, “is that the way to speak to guests, to exiled, unhappy fellow-countrymen?”

“Don’t teach me how to be generous,” retorted Banfi, proudly. “At my house the poor and the persecuted have ever found an asylum, and if these fugitive gentlemen wish us to share house and home with them, I’m ready to do so. Here’s my hand upon it. But just as I should be out of my senses to burn my own house down, so now too I protest against the conflagration of my country; and if you do not cease from troubling a peaceful land, I’ll leave no stone unturned till I have driven you all out.”

“We ought not to be surprised at this tone, my friends,” said Teleki, with bitter scorn, turning towards the Hungarians. “His Excellency here has been so very recently amnestied by the Prince, that he imagines he is still at war with us.”

Apafi, who had been sitting on burning coals, now interposed.

“Cease this bickering. We dismiss you all. You see that sundry of our councillors are against the matter, and without their consent I can do nothing.”

“Then,” cried Teleki, with solemn emphasis, “we appeal to the Diet.”

“I too will be there,” said Banfi.

The Prince, very much offended, withdrew to his cabinet. The Hungarian nobles, much excited, went out by the other door. Teleki remained behind. Banfi, adjusting his marten-skin cap, haughtily measured his opponent from head to foot, and exclaimed ironically as he went out﻿—“I leave my reputation behind me!” Teleki returned his gaze with the most nonchalant sangfroid.

===Chinese===

"我对亲王说话了吗？"班菲耸耸肩问。"您老堵在道儿上，我哪还够得着殿下？对那些根本没资格站上御阶的人，我从来是、永远是敌人，而您，米哈伊·泰莱基老爷，正是其中一个。哦，别以为让您为匈牙利事业这样起劲的种种缘由，能瞒得过我。您不满足于在特兰西瓦尼亚做亲王之下的第一人；您还想再当上匈牙利的帕拉丁27呢。哈！哈！你们这一伙，互相愚弄得可真妙。法国人许诺援助匈牙利人；匈牙利人许诺泰莱基当帕拉丁；泰莱基许诺阿帕菲一顶王冠——你们全体都在撒谎；你们骗人，又受人骗。"

"先生，"泰莱基痛苦地答道，"对宾客，对流亡的不幸同胞，就是这样讲话的吗？"

"用不着您来教我怎么大方。"班菲骄傲地反驳，"在我家门下，穷人和受逼迫的人向来找得到避难所；这些流亡的老爷们若想与我们同住屋檐下，我乐意奉陪。我的手就伸在这儿。可正如我若亲手烧掉自家房子便是发了疯，如今我也抗议烧掉我的国家；你们若不停止搅扰这片安宁的土地，我必千方百计，把你们统统撵出去。"

"对这种腔调，朋友们不必惊奇。"泰莱基带着辛酸的轻蔑转向匈牙利人说，"这位大人新近才蒙亲王赦免，还只当自己在同我们打仗呢。"

如坐针毡的阿帕菲此刻插话了。

"别吵了。你们全都退下吧。你们也看见了，我们的几位顾问反对此事，没有他们的首肯，我什么也做不成。"

"那么，"泰莱基庄严而郑重地喊道，"我们诉诸议会。"

"我也会到场。"班菲说。

亲王深感受辱，退回私室。匈牙利贵族们群情激奟，从另一扇门出去了。泰莱基留了下来。班菲正了正貂皮帽，倨傲地把对手从头到脚打量了一番，出门时讥诮地甩下一句——"我把我的名声留在这儿了！"泰莱基以最漫不经心的镇定回敬了他的目光。

===Original===

When everyone had disappeared, Teleki whispered some words to a page, who went out and returned in a few moments with a florid, curly-headed young man. Methinks we have seen this youth somewhere or other before, though only for an instant which we cannot call to mind. A beggar’s sack hangs down over his ragged clothing, his hand holds a knobby stick.

“So you permit me at last to approach the Prince?” said he, in a somewhat dictatorial tone.

“Sit down here by the door,” replied the minister; “the Prince goes to dinner shortly, and will pass by this way. You can then speak to him.”

The young man with the beggar’s sack sat for a long time at the Prince’s door, till Apafi came out of his room on his way to dinner. The beggar with the knapsack planted himself right in his Highness’s way.

“Who are you?” asked the Prince, much surprised.

“I am that renowned warrior, Emerich Balassa, who once was one of the chief men of Hungary, and now stands before your Highness with the beggar’s staff.”

“You were involved, I understand, in that conspiracy against us?” said Apafi, disagreeably flurried.

“That I was not, your Highness. If you would deign to listen to my tale, then﻿—”

“Speak!”

“There was once in Hungary a famous Turkish freebooter, named Corsar Beg, who for a long time ravaged the mountain regions. The banded might of six counties was insufficient to besiege him in his fortress. This man I captured by subtlety. By promises and flatteries I won over his favourite slave, who enticed him out of his stronghold by night and alone. I, duly advertised thereof, fell upon him with horsemen ambushed in the woods, and took captive both him and his slave, who is the most beautiful and the most abandoned of her sex in the whole world.”

“I have heard of you, Master Balassa. It was a daring deed.”

“Listen further, your Highness. No sooner had the news of my capture spread abroad, than the Palatine of Hungary, very emphatically, insisted upon my handing over the prisoners to him. The Turks had already offered me a ransom of sixteen thousand ducats for the pair, but I would not part with the girl at any price. I therefore sent word to the Palatine that if he wanted a Beg of his own he must catch one, for I had not captured mine on his account.”

Apafi laughed heartily. “That was one for him!”

===Chinese===

人都散尽后，泰莱基对一名侍童耳语了几句，侍童出去，不多时领来一个面色红润、长着鬈发的年轻人。我仿佛记得这少年我们在哪里见过一面，只是那一瞬实在太短，想不起来了。一身破衣烂衫上垂着讨饭袋，手里拄一根疙疙瘩瘩的棍子。

"这么说，您总算许我接近亲王了？"他说，口气竟有点发号施令的派头。

"就坐在门边这儿。"首相答道，"亲王一会儿就去用膳，必打这条路经过。到时你再跟他说话。"

背讨饭袋的年轻人在亲王门口坐了许久，直到阿帕菲出屋去用膳。背行囊的叫花子一横身，正好挡住殿下的去路。

"你是谁？"亲王大为惊讶地问。

"本人便是那位赫赫有名的武士埃梅里希·巴拉沙（Emerich Balassa），曾是匈牙利的头面人物之一，如今拄着讨饭棍站在殿下面前。"

"我记得，你卷进过那桩反我们的阴谋？"阿帕菲说，心里不痛快地慌了一下。

"那倒没有，殿下。您若肯屈尊听我讲完缘由，那么——"

"讲！"

"匈牙利曾有个出了名的土耳其巨盗，叫科尔萨尔贝伊（Corsar Beg），长期在山区烧杀劫掠。六个州合力围攻他的巢穴，都拿他没办法。这个人，我用计擒住了。我许愿奉承，收买了他最宠爱的女奴，她夜里只身把他诱出了城堡。我得了准信，带着埋伏在林中的骑兵突袭，把他连同他那个女奴一并活捉——那女子是天下第一美人，也是天下第一荡妇。"

"你的事我听说过，巴拉沙老爷。那是桩胆大包天的壮举。"

"再往下听，殿下。我擒人的消息刚传开，匈牙利的帕拉丁就斩钉截铁地非要我把俘虏交给他。土耳其人已出价一万六千克特要赎这一对儿，可那姑娘多少银子我也不卖。于是我给帕拉丁带话：他若想要贝伊（beg），自己逮一个去，我擒的这个可不是替他擒的。"

阿帕菲放声大笑。"这话噘得他够呛！"

===Original===

“Thereupon the Palatine waxed wroth, and by the Emperor’s command sent out troops against me to rob me of my captives. Now just at this very time, your Highness’s brother-in-law, Denis Banfi, had taken refuge in my castle, and to him I entrusted the slave, of whom I was madly enamoured. He was to fly with her to my castle of Ecsed, and as I saw that the Palatine was bent upon securing Corsar Beg for himself in order to cut off his head at Buda as a warning to all malefactors, I gave the Turk poison, which he, to escape the scaffold, thankfully accepted. When, therefore, the troops of the Palatine arrived at my house, all that they found there was the cold corpse, which the Turks afterwards purchased from me for a thousand ducats.”

“The Palatine was naturally very angry, I suppose?” remarked Apafi.

“ ’Twas I who had cause to be angry, for all through him I lost fifteen thousand ducats, and yet he succeeded in obtaining an order for my apprehension from the Emperor. I scented the danger in time, and got together my valuables in order to fly into Transylvania, and remain there till the affair had blown over. First of all, then, I hastened to my castle at Ecsed, whither, as I have said, I had sent Banfi on beforehand with the Turkish slave. While still on the way, I learnt that Banfi had been restored by your Highness’s amnesty to his former position. I rejoiced greatly thereat, supposing that I now had in him a powerful protector. Nevertheless, on reaching Ecsed, I found no sign or trace of the girl. My castellan there informed me that Banfi had carried her off with him, and left a letter behind for me, which contained the following words﻿—‘Learn from this, my friend, that there are three things you should never entrust to another﻿—your horse, your watch, and your mistress!’ ”

“What!” cried Apafi; “is this really true?”

“Pray let your Highness look at his own writing,” and he drew the letter in question out of his leather knapsack. “He is said to have concealed the girl somewhere in his forests at Banfi-Hunyad.”

Apafi turned scarlet with rage.

“ ’Tis monstrous!” cried he. “This fellow possesses a virtuous and lovely wife of his own﻿—my consort’s own sister﻿—and yet he can so far forget his duty as a husband! I’ll not put up with it!”

===Chinese===

"帕拉丁于是大怒，奉皇帝之命派兵来夺我的俘虏。恰在这当口，殿下的连襟德尼斯·班菲正在我城堡里避难，我便把那个女奴——我为她神魂颠倒——托付给了他，让他带她飞逃到我在埃切德（Ecsed）的城堡去。我又看出帕拉丁一心要亲自拿到科尔萨尔贝伊，好在布达（Buda）砍他的头，儆戒天下匪徒，便给了那土耳其人毒药；他为了不上绞架，感恩戴德地领受了。于是帕拉丁的兵开到我家时，找到的只有一具冰冷的尸首——土耳其人后来出一千克特把它从我手里买了回去。"

"帕拉丁自然气得要命，我猜？"阿帕菲说。

"该生气的是我：为了他，我损失了一万五千克特，他倒还从皇帝那儿讨来了拘捕我的敕令。我及时嗅出风声，收拾了细软，准备逃进特兰西瓦尼亚避避风头。于是我首先赶去埃切德城堡——如我所说，我先前已把班菲和那土耳其女奴打发去了那里。半路上，我就听说班菲蒙殿下大赦，官复原职了。我大为欢喜，心想这下有了个靠山。谁知到了埃切德，那姑娘连影子带气味都没有了。我的城堡总管告诉我，班菲把她随身带走了，还给我留下一封信，信上写着这么几句——'我的朋友，由此可知：有三样东西万万不可托付他人——你的马、你的表、还有你的情人！'"

"什么！"阿帕菲喊道，"这可是真的？"

"请殿下过目他的亲笔。"说着他从皮囊里掏出那封信。"据说他把那姑娘藏在他班菲-胡尼亚德（Banfi-Hunyad）的林子里的什么地方了。"

阿帕菲气得满脸通红。

"简直无法无天！"他喊道，"这家伙自己分明有一位贤淑可爱的妻子——我夫人嫡亲的妹妹——竟会这般忘掉做丈夫的本分！我绝不容忍！"

===Original===

“Pardon me, your Highness; I have nothing more to do with Banfi now. My complaint is against one Kapi, who had the usufruct of my Transylvanian property. Not wishing, then, to have anything more to do with Banfi, I took up my quarters with Kapi at Aranyosi Castle. Your Highness, the pomp which that man displays exceeds anything that I have ever seen, and I have seen many princely and palatinal courts in my day. His wife never uses her feet at all. Even if she wants to get to the door, she is carried thither in a gilded sedan-chair, and she never wears a dress more than once!”

“But what have I to do with the frippery of Dame Kapi?”

“I’m coming to that. Her love of display costs money, and has compelled her husband to resort to fraudulent practices. And besides, such extravagance concerns your Highness also, as tending to emphasize the contrast already apparent between the frugal simplicity of your Highness’s court and the dazzling pomp of these petty kings﻿—a contrast which has already made a pretty deep impression upon our foreign visitors. Thus, quite recently, the Bavarian minister, who had come from a banquet at Ebesfalva to Aranyosi, remarked in a flattering tone to Dame Kapi, in my hearing, that she was the real Princess of Transylvania.”

“He said that, did he?” cried the Prince, becoming much interested. “Go on with your narrative. So he said that Kapi’s wife was the real Princess, eh?”

“Yet strip from off her her costly pearls and diamonds, and you will see that in regard to beauty and majesty she is not fit to lace the shoes of her Highness the Princess Apafi.”

“Go on! go on!”

“Well, one fine day this same Kapi came to me, and told me that your Highness had been commanded by the Palatine to arrest and deliver me over to him.”

“I receive a command! I know absolutely nothing about it.”

===Chinese===

"殿下恕罪；班菲的事，如今与我再无干系。我要控告的是一个叫卡皮（Kapi）的人，他享有我特兰西瓦尼亚产业的用益权。我既不愿再与班菲有什么瓜葛，便去阿拉尼奥什（Aranyosi）城堡投卡皮住下。殿下，那人摆的排场，超出了我此生所见的一切——我这辈子也见过不少亲王的、帕拉丁的宫廷了。他的老婆压根儿不兴自己走路。哪怕只想挪到门口，也得坐镀金轿子抬过去，而且一身衣裳从不穿第二回！"

"可卡皮夫人的浮华，与我何干？"

"这就说到正题了。她这爱炫耀是要花钱的，逼得她丈夫干起了欺诈的勾当。再说，这般穷奢极欲也与殿下有关：它越发衬出殿下宫廷的俭朴与这班土皇帝的豪奢之间的对照——这对照已给我们的外国来客留下了相当深的印象。就说新近吧，那位从埃贝什法尔瓦的宴席来到阿拉尼奥什的巴伐利亚公使，当着我的面奉承卡皮夫人说，她才是特兰西瓦尼亚真正的亲王夫人。"

"他这么说了，是吗？"亲王喊道，顿时来了兴致。"接着讲。他说卡皮的老婆才是真正的亲王夫人，嗯？"

"可是剥掉她那身贵重的珍珠钻石，您就看见了：论美貌、论威仪，她连给阿帕菲亲王夫人殿下系鞋带都不配。"

"讲下去！讲下去！"

"好。有一天，这位卡皮来找我，说殿下已奉帕拉丁之命，要将我逮捕并引渡给他。"

"我奉命！我怎么一点都不知道。"

===Original===

“Unfortunately I believed his words, and imagining myself caught between two fires, I made over my Transylvanian property to Kapi to save it from confiscation, he at the same time delivering to me an undertaking to re-transfer the estates as soon as possible. Meanwhile I resolved to fly to Poland, and stay there till the storm blew over. Kapi gave me two guides, who were to conduct me through the mountain-passes to the frontier; but at the same time he secretly informed the frontier sentinels that I was a spy sent by the Emperor to explore Transylvania, and was now desirous of returning unobserved. So the rogues waylaid me, robbed me of all my money and papers, and dragged me to Fehervár, where my innocence came to light, but my money and papers were of course hopelessly lost. And now this Kapi actually maintains that I sold him all my property, and I’ve nothing in the world but this leather knapsack round my neck, with which I must now beg my way about.”

“Be of good cheer. I will give you the most exemplary satisfaction,” returned the enraged Prince.

“It is a matter which also concerns your Highness’s own dignity,” replied Balassa. “These great lords behave in as high-handed a fashion as if they had absolutely no superior.”

“Be easy. I will very soon show them who is the real Prince of Transylvania.”

Apafi, full of indignation, then left the audience-chamber.

A storm was gathering over the heads of two great men who stood in Teleki’s way.

===Chinese===

"不幸我真的信了他的话，自以为腹背受敌，便把特兰西瓦尼亚的产业转让给卡皮，以免遭抄没；他同时给了我一张字据，答应尽快把产业归还。这期间我决意逃往波兰，在那儿待到风头过去。卡皮拨给我两个向导，领我翻山越岭到边界去；可他同时又密报边卡哨兵，说我是皇帝派来刺探特兰西瓦尼亚的奸细，正想悄悄溜回去。于是那两个坏蛋半路设伏，抢光了我的钱财文书，把我拖到费海尔瓦尔（Fehervár）；到了那儿，我的清白虽然水落石出，钱财文书却自然是再也要不回来了。如今这卡皮居然一口咬定，说我把全部家产卖给了他；我落到身无长物，只剩脖子上这只皮囊，只好拄着它一路讨饭了。"

"宽心吧。我定给你一个最教人长记性的交代。"怒不可遈的亲王答道。

"这事也关系殿下自己的尊严。"巴拉沙答道，"这班大人物的做派，专横得好象头顶上压根儿没有君主。"

"放心。我很快就让他们瞧瞧，谁才是特兰西瓦尼亚真正的亲王。"

阿帕菲满怀义愤，离开接见厅。

一场风暴，正朝着两位挡在泰莱基路上的大人物头上聚拢。

## Book / 卷
===Original===

II

The Devil’s Garden

===Chinese===

二

魔鬼的花园

## I / 一
===Original===

The Patrol

Clement the Clerk stuck his pen behind his ear and recited to himself the elegant verses which he had just composed, two hundred strophes in all, almost every line of which ended in *fuerat*, with a sporadic *fuisset* in between.

Michael Apafi used regularly to repent whenever he had offended anyone, and he therefore could not rest till he had compensated the itinerant scholar Clement for the snub he had administered to him, and this he did by making the unsophisticated poet his﻿—Patrol-officer.

In those days many agreeable duties were connected with this office﻿—duties which Clement simply left alone, devoting himself instead to the composition of epics and chronicles, which he manufactured in great abundance.

At that moment he was casting his eyes over a great epic, in which he recorded how his Highness, Prince Michael Apafi, had gone out against Érsekújvár to besiege it; how with splendid valour he had arrived there; how, on beholding the foe, he had drawn his sword; how, after mature deliberation, he had turned back again; and how, finally, he and all his heroes had returned home again safe and sound.

Poetic distraction had so completely absorbed the faculties of Clement the Clerk, that a week had already elapsed since his servant had made off with his master’s spurred jackboots, without the latter, in his capacity of Patrol-officer, thinking of pursuing the runaway; but in fact he was confined within a vicious circle, inasmuch as every time he thought of inquiring for his boots, it occurred to him that his servant had stolen them; and every time he thought of going out and inquiring for his servant, it occurred to him that he had no boots. What could he do then under such circumstances but sit down again, and write poems in absolutely endless quantities?

His room had not been swept out for weeks, naturally therefore there was no lack of dust and cobwebs; but, by way of contrast, the deal floor all around the solitary table was mottled with inkblots. The table itself had only two legs, the place of the others being supplied by layers of bricks.

===Chinese===

巡察官

文书克莱门特把笔别到耳朵后面，独自吟诵起他刚刚写成的雅致诗篇——足足两百节，几乎每一行都以拉丁文的*fuerat*（"曾是"）收尾，中间偶尔穿插一个*fuisset*（"若曾是"）。

米哈伊·阿帕菲（Michael Apafi）有个习惯：什么时候得罪了人，事后必定要补过。所以直到用某种方式补偿了游方学士克莱门特所受的那场抢白，他才安下心来——他补偿的法子，是让这位不谙世故的诗人当上了他的巡察官。

在那个年代，这个官职连带许多美差——这些差事克莱门特一概撂下不管，一心扑在史诗和编年史的写作上，而且产量极丰。

此刻他正在通览一部大型史诗，诗中记载：米哈伊·阿帕菲亲王殿下如何挥师进讨埃尔谢克乌伊瓦尔；如何以赫赫声威抵达城下；如何一望见敌军便拔剑出鞘；如何深思熟虑之后又回转马头；以及最后，他和他麾下的众英雄如何全军安然无恙、全须全尾地返回了家园。

诗兴出神到了极点，把文书克莱门特的心智整个吞没了：他的仆人卷了他的带刺长筒靴逃跑，已经过去一个星期，身为巡察官的他竟还没想起来去追；其实他是被困在了一个怪圈里——每当想起要打听靴子，他就想起仆人把靴子偷了；每当想出门去打听仆人，他又想起自己没了靴子。这样的处境下他还有什么可干的？只有重新坐下，写出绝对没完没了的诗来。

他的房间几个星期没有打扫，灰尘蛛网自然不缺；不过相映成趣的是，那张孤零零的桌子四周的松木地板上，墨水渍斑斑点点。桌子本身只剩两条腿，缺腿的位置拿几层砖垫着。

===Original===

The poet scribbles, erases, and nibbles at his pen; on the windowsill lies a piece of bread and some cheese; it occurs to the poet that it has been put there for him to eat; but first he must use up the ink still remaining in his pen, and in doing so another idea occurs to him, and after that a third, and then a fourth; meanwhile mice come skipping out of a hole beneath the windowsill, frisk about the bread and cheese, nibble away at it till not a morsel remains, and then skip back into their holes again. The poet having wearied out his Pegasus, starts up, looks for his bread and cheese, and perceiving that only the crumbs remain on the windowsill, concludes that he has already eaten his fill, so sits him down again and goes on writing.

While he is thus plaguing himself for the benefit of posterity, somebody begins scratching at the door, and after groping about the door-hinge in search of the door-latch, finds it at last, and shakes it to and fro as if he does not know what to do with it. This disturbance disagreeably awakens Clement the Clerk out of his poetic reveries, who, after vainly exclaiming in a loud and angry voice that the door is not bolted, finds himself at last obliged to rise from his seat and open the door himself, lest the importunate visitor should break off the latch or lift the door bodily from its hinges.

Before him, with a sealed letter in his hand, stands a gaping Wallach peasant, who appears extraordinarily terrified to see the door open, though that was the very thing he had been aiming at all along.

“Well, what is it?” snapped Clement the Clerk, horribly angry. “Why don’t you speak?”

The Wallach raised his round eyebrows, which looked, for all the world, like a charcoal smear extending from his nostrils to his temples, and which also served him as a kind of propeller for shoving backwards and forwards the lamb’s-wool cap that he wore half over his face, looked at the poet with wide-open eyes, and asked him﻿—

“Are you he whom they pay to tell lies?”

The Wallach meant no offence by this terminology. It was only his roundabout way of describing Clement the Clerk’s sphere of activity.

The poet was almost choking with rage.

“And whose ox are you?” he exclaimed furiously.

“The ox of his Excellency who sent this letter,” he answered with perfect simplicity.

===Chinese===

诗人涂了写，写了涂，啃着笔杆；窗台上搁着一块面包和一点干酪；诗人想起那是放给他吃的；可他得先把笔里剩的墨水用完，用着用着又来了一个新念头，接着第三个、第四个；这当儿，老鼠们从窗台底下的洞里蹦出来，绕着面包干酪撒欢，一点点啃了个精光，又蹦回洞里去了。诗人的飞马跑乏了，他一跃而起，找他的面包干酪，一见窗台上只剩些渣子，便断定自己早已吃饱，于是重新坐下，继续写。

他正这样为造福后世而自讨苦吃，门外有人抓挠起来，又在门轴旁边摸索门闩，摸了半天摸着了，把它摇来晃去，仿佛不知道该拿它怎么办。这搅扰把文书克莱门特不痛快地从诗境里唤醒，他白白发了一通火，大声嚷门没有上闩，最后只好自己起身开门，免得这位不依不饶的来客把门闩掰断，或干脆连门带轴把门卸下来。

他面前站着个目瞪口呆的瓦拉几亚（Wallachian）庄稼汉，手里拿一封火漆封信；门开了，他反倒显得骇异万分——虽然开门正是他一直以来的目标。

"喂，什么事？"文书克莱门特厉声喝道，气得要命，"怎么不说话？"

那瓦拉几亚人扬起他的圆眉毛——那两道眉毛活脱脱像两抹从鼻孔抹到太阳穴的锅灰，还兼作推进器，把他那顶半罩着脸的羊绒帽前后推移——他睁大眼睛望着诗人，问——

"你就是那个拿了工钱专门撒谎的人吗？"

瓦拉几亚人用这个称呼并没有冒犯之意。那不过是他绕着弯儿描述文书克莱门特的职守范围罢了。

诗人气得几乎背过气去。

"你又是谁家的牛？"他怒喝道。

"派送这封信的大人老爷家的牛。"他答得再老实不过。

===Original===

“What is your master’s name?” cried Clement, angrily snatching the letter out of the Wallach’s hand.

“They call him Excellency.”

Clement tore open the letter and read as follows﻿—“I want a word or two with you; follow the bearer whithersoever he leads you.”

Clement was wroth enough already, but the reflection that he was summoned away on important business, and had no boots to go in, was the last straw. He was quite beside himself.

“Go,” cried he to the Wallach, “and tell your master, whoever he may be, that he is as near to me as I am to him; if he wants to speak to me, let him take the trouble to come hither. Do you understand?”

“I understand, Dumni Macska” (Mister Pussy), returned the Wallach, involuntarily using in his fright the nickname secretly given by the Romanian peasants to the Patrol-officer when he is making his rounds; and with that he slouched out of the room.

Meanwhile Clement, with a great muscular effort, had climbed on to his high-backed chair again, and placed two huge folios upright on the floor in front of him, so that his coming visitor might not see that he was barefooted.

In a short time strident, energetic footsteps were audible outside, and Clement the Clerk, peeping out of the window, perceived to his no small confusion that his visitor was none other than his Excellency, Count Ladislaus Csaky, accompanied by two gold-laced heydukes.

“Clement,” thought the clerk to himself, “now’s the time to assert your dignity! No doubt his lordship is a great man and a high; but, on the other hand, he is in the Prince’s bad books, while you, my boy, are in high favour at court, and a public officer to boot.” So he hid his feet behind his books, stuck his pen between his lips, and when Csaky came in did not so much as offer him a seat.

Csaky seemed much put out by this reception.

“You have a very high opinion of your official dignity,” said he to Clement.

“I am what I am thanks to the favour of the Prince,” returned Clement haughtily, crossing his arms with an air of importance.

“I too have come hither by the Prince’s command. His Highness has just entrusted me with a very delicate errand, in which I need your help; but the affair must be managed with the utmost secrecy, and that was why I wanted you to come out to me.”

At this explanation Clement the Clerk forgot his dignity altogether.

===Chinese===

"你家老爷尊姓大名？"克莱门特喊道，怒冲冲地从瓦拉几亚人手里夺过信。

"人家都叫他大人老爷。"

克莱门特撕开信，读到如下字句——"我有两句话要同你说；送信人领你去哪里，你便跟到哪里。"

克莱门特本来已经够恼的了，再一想自己竟被唤去办要差，却连一双赴差的靴子都没有，这便是压垮他的最后一根稻草。他简直气疯了。

"去，"他朝瓦拉几亚人喊道，"回去告诉你的主子——不管他是哪路神仙——他离我多远，我离他就多远；他要跟我说话，就劳他的大驾自己跑一趟。听懂了吗？"

"明白，猫咪大人（Dumni Macska）。"瓦拉几亚人答道——他吓昏了头，脱口说出了罗马尼亚庄稼人私下给巡游四乡的巡察官起的外号——说完便溜出了屋子。

与此同时，克莱门特费了九牛二虎之力重新爬上他那把高背椅，又在椅子前立起两大本对开本，好叫即将到来的客人看不见他光着脚。

不多时，外头响起铿锵有力的脚步声，文书克莱门特往窗外一窥，大惊失色：来客不是别人，竟是拉斯洛·恰基（Ladislaus Csaky）伯爵大人，身后还跟着两个镶金绦的海杜克。

"克莱门特，"文书暗想，"现在正是摆你身份的时候！这位老爷无疑是个大人物、高人物；可话说回来，他正失宠于亲王，而你，我的小子，正是宫里走红的人，还是个在编的官员呢。"

于是他把双脚藏在书后，把笔往嘴上一横，恰基进来时，他连让座都没让。

这般接待，恰基显得大为光火。

"您可真把自己这官身看得高贵。"他对克莱门特说。

"我之所以有今日，全赖亲王的恩典。"克莱门特傲然答道，抱起双臂，一副要人的派头。

"我也是奉亲王之命而来。殿下刚交给我一件极微妙的差事，需要你帮把手；只是此事必须绝对保密，所以我才想让你出来见我。"

一听这话，文书克莱门特把体统忘了个一干二净。

===Original===

“I beg you a thousand pardons,” stammered he in great confusion, and with meekly-bowed head. “I did not know﻿—pray be seated!” As however there was no other chair in the room but that on which he sat, he sprang down from it to give place to the Count, thereby revealing the fact that his feet were minus their legitimate coverings, at which Csaky laughed till his jaws ached.

“Why, deuce take it, Mr. Officer, is it from a feeling of excessive reverence that you take off your boots like the Turks do?”

“I beg your pardon! I have not taken them off; but my servant ran away with them while I slept, and that was the sole reason why I was forced to send your lordship that churlish message, which I hope your lordship has long since forgotten.”

At this Csaky’s mirth became downright uproarious.

“Well, if that is all, we will soon find a remedy,” said he to Clement; and calling the heydukes, bade them fetch at once his own parade boots out of his carriage.

Clement instantly began to raise objections: he could not think of it; the honour was too great. But when his eyes fell upon the boots, they took his fancy immediately, for they were made of the finest green morocco, sewn with gold thread, trimmed on both sides with galloon, and provided with enamelled spurs.

“Quick! on with them!” cried Csaky to the Patrol-officer; “for you must set out upon your journey without delay.”

So Clement the Clerk seized one of the boots by the tags, and after bestowing a smile upon it, proceeded to pull it on. But this of itself was no light labour, for Csaky wore very small, tight-fitting, gentlemanlike boots, whereas Clement the Clerk was a very large-footed animal; so that it was not till after three desperate struggles had completely exhausted him that he managed to get one foot halfway down the leg of the first boot, and all the time he made such grimaces that Ladislaus Csaky had to put his head out of the window to hide his merriment. When he got as far as the heel, he stuck fast again, so that he had to seize the straps with both hands and stamp his way down, hopping round the room all the while, with his body forming a complete curve, and groaning aloud at every forward shove; so that by the time he had wriggled into one boot, the eyes of the poor poet were almost starting from their sockets, and the sweat trickled from his cheeks.

===Chinese===

"千万请您恕罪。"他极为狼狈地结巴道，恭顺地垂着头。"我不知道——请上坐！"可屋里除了他自己坐的那把，再没有第二把椅子，他便从椅子上蹦下来让给伯爵，这一蹦，两只脚没穿本该穿的鞋袜这一事实便暴露无遗，恰基笑得腮帮子都疼了。

"哎呀，见鬼，长官先生，您脱靴子脱得跟土耳其人似的，莫非是出于过度的敬意？"

"恕罪恕罪！我不是脱掉的；是我睡着的时候仆人卷着跑了，这也是我万不得已才给老爷送上那句粗话的唯一缘由，还望老爷早已把它忘到脑后。"

一听这个，恰基索性开怀大笑起来。

"好吧，既然只是这么点事，很快就有法子补救。"他对克莱门特说，随即唤来海杜克，吩咐他们立刻从他车子里把自己的礼靴取来。

克莱门特马上开始推辞：这如何使得；这恩宠太重了。可等他的眼睛落到那双靴子上，立刻就动了心：那是上等绿摩洛哥皮的，金线缝制，两侧镶着金银绦子，还配着珐琅马刺。

"快！穿上！"恰基朝巡察官喊道，"因为你必须立刻启程上路。"

于是文书克莱门特抓住一只靴筒上的提耳，先对它报以一笑，然后动手往脚上套。可这本身就是一桩重活：恰基穿的是极小巧、极紧俏的绅士靴，而文书克莱门特却是只大脚板的动物；直折腾了三个筋疲力尽的回合，他才把一只脚塞进第一只靴筒的一半，这一路他挤眉弄眼的模样，害得拉斯洛·恰基只好把头探出窗外去藏他的笑。塞到脚跟处又卡住了，他只好双手抓住靴釺，连蹬带踹往下踩，满屋子单脚蹦，整个身子弯成一张弓，每往下一杵就大声呻吟一回；等他终于把一只靴子折腾上脚，可怜的诗人的眼珠几乎夺眶而出，汗珠顺着脸颊直淌。

===Original===

Similar difficulties awaited the good Patrol-officer with the second foot; but after working with six-horse power to force his foot into a receptacle never intended for it, he was at last able, with the ruddiness of satisfaction on his cheeks, to take a smiling survey of his gorgeous, tight-fitting boots, which harmonized so delightfully with the other dusty, greasy, ink-bespattered constituent parts of his dress.

“Now, mark what I say!” said Csaky, sitting down with a lordly air on the solitary chair, whilst the clerk, standing before him, raised first one and then the other leg aloft, at the same time uttering a peculiar hissing sound, and turning a livid green and blue in his agony, for the boots had now begun to play havoc with his corns. “When did you last go your rounds?”

“I really don’t know.”

“But you ought to know. Why don’t you make a note of it? The Prince wishes you to go your rounds at once, and you must look particularly sharp after all the places between Toroczko, Banfi-Hunyad, and Bonczhida. Besides the usual questions, you must ask the people whether they have seen any foreign wild beast in the surrounding woods.”

“Foreign wild beast?” mechanically repeated the wretched Patrol-officer.

“And if at any place they tell you they have seen such beast, you must go personally into the districts indicated, and search till you come upon its track.”

“I cry your Excellency’s pardon! but what manner of beast may it be?” asked the student timidly.

“Come, come! don’t be afraid! It is neither a seven-headed dragon nor yet a minotaur, but only a young panther.”

“A panther!” stammered the terrified Clement.

“You are not expected to catch it,” said Csaky cheerily. “You have only to discover its hiding-place and let me know.”

“And if this wild beast﻿—whose existence indeed in Transylvania I very much doubt﻿—should stray into the territory of Denis Banfi,” asked Clement, “what am I to do then?”

“You must go after it.”

“I cry your Excellency’s pardon, but his property is a *liber baronatus*, where my jurisdiction ceases.”

===Chinese===

第二只脚又给这位好巡察官备下了同样的难关；可他拿出六马之力，硬把脚塞进了一个从不曾为它预备的容器，最后总算能带着满脸心满意足的红光，笑吟吟地欣赏起他那双华美紧俏的靴子来——它们与他浑身上下其余那些落满灰尘、油光锃亮、墨渍斑斑的部件，配得何等相得益彰。

"现在，听我说！"恰基说着，以贵族的气派在那张孤零零的椅子上坐下；文书立在他跟前，先把一条腿抬高，再把另一条腿抬高，嘴里发出一种奇异的嘶嘶声，脸憋得青一阵绿一阵——靴子已经开始祸害他的鸡眼了。"你上回巡察是什么时候？"

"我实在不记得了。"

"可你应该记得。为什么不记上一笔？亲王要你立刻出巡，托罗茨科（Toroczko）、班菲-胡尼亚德（Banfi-Hunyad）和邦奇达（Bonczhida）之间的所有地方，你都要格外细心查访。除了例问的那几项，你还得问百姓们：在周围林子里见过外邦野兽没有。"

"外邦野兽？"倒霉的巡察官机械地重复。

"无论哪个地方，只要有人告诉你见过这么一只兽，你就得亲自到所指的地界里去，一路寻到踩上它的踪迹为止。"

"恳请大人恕罪！那可能是怎样一只兽呢？"学生怯生生地问。

"得了，得了！别怕！它既不是七头龙，也不是米诺陶，不过是一头小豹子。"

"豹子！"吓坏了的克莱门特结巴道。

"没指望你去捉它。"恰基轻松地说。"你只消查明它的藏身处，报给我便是。"

"可这头野兽——它在特兰西瓦尼亚（Transylvania）存在与否，我实在深表怀疑——倘若窜进了德尼斯·班菲（Denis Banfi）的地界，"克莱门特问，"那时我怎么办？"

"你就跟着追进去。"

"恳请大人恕罪，可他的产业是*自由男爵领*（liber baronatus），我的管辖权到那里就断了。"

===Original===

“Don’t be so stupid, Clement,” said Csaky. “I never said you were to repair thither *vi et armis*: the whole expedition must remain a secret. You have only to follow the wild beast’s track. We have it, on the best authority, that the beast is somewhere in the neighbourhood, and we trust to your dexterity to spot it. The rest will be done by more enterprising people than yourself.”

Clement regarded the mission as altogether odd and risky, but he dared not raise any objection, so he simply bowed low and sighed deeply.

“Above all things we must have dexterity, expedition, and secrecy. Keep that constantly in mind.”

“I will go at once,” cried Clement desperately; “but first I must borrow me a horse from someone or other, for I should not like to utterly ruin these beautiful boots by walking in them.”

“That too would be a little too slow for our purpose. But don’t bother your head about a horse. One of my heydukes will give you his, which you must mount at once. Remember however to give him oats occasionally, as I don’t want him to come back all skin and bone.”

Clement the Clerk, quite confounded by so much graciousness, hastily shouldered his shabby knapsack, fastened his rusty sword to his side, and after placing in his knapsack a roll of parchment, a goose-quill, and a wooden inkhorn, declared himself ready to depart.

“You have a very light equipment,” remarked Csaky.

“*Integer vitae, scelerisque purus, non eget Mauri jaculis neque arcu*,” returned the philosopher with a classical flourish, and when the reins had been placed in his hands, he prepared to mount. But the aristocratic charger, as soon as he perceived that the clerk had one foot in the stirrups, began to plunge, buck, and run round and round, thereby compelling the aspiring poet to hop along with him on one foot, till the laughing heydukes seized the horse by the bridle, and helped the unpractised horseman into the saddle. As however he had very long legs, and the wicked heydukes had lashed the stirrups up very high, he was obliged to squat upon the horse as if it had been a camel.

===Chinese===

"别这么死心眼，克莱门特。"恰基说，"我从没叫你*大张旗鼓地*（vi et armis）开进去：这整趟差事必须保密。你只消循着野兽的踪迹走。我们有最可靠的消息，那畜生就在这一带的什么地方，全指望你的机灵把它寻出来。余下的事，自有比你更敢作敢为的人去办。"

克莱门特觉得这差事实在古怪又凶险，可他不敢有半句异议，只好深深一躬，长叹一声。

"最要紧的是机敏、迅速、保密。把这话时时记在心上。"

"我这就去。"克莱门特豁出去喊道，"可我得先跟谁借匹马，我不忍心穿着走路，把这双漂亮的靴子彻底毁掉。"

"那对我们的目的来说也太慢了。马的事用不着你操心。我的一个海杜克会把他的马让给你，你必须立刻上马。只是记住时不时喂它点燕麦，我可不想它回来时只剩一把骨头。"

文书克莱门特被这许多恩宠彻底弄晕了，匆匆背上他那只破行囊，把生锈的剑挂上腰，又在行囊里装进一卷羊皮纸、一支鹅毛笔和一只木墨角，宣布自己可以动身了。

"你这行头可真轻省。"恰基说。

"*Integer vitae, scelerisque purus, non eget Mauri jaculis neque arcu*（品行无瑕、不染罪愆之人，无需摩尔人的标枪与弓）。"这位哲人回敬了一句典雅的拉丁文；缰绳一交到他手里，他便准备上马。

可那匹贵族战马一见文书把一只脚踩进马镫，便开始尥蹶子、蹦跳、打转，逼得这位胸怀大志的诗人单脚跟着它满场蹦，直到笑作一团的众海杜克抓住笼头，才把这位生手上扶进马鞍。又因为他两条腿极长，而促狭的海杜克们把马镫皮带勒得极高，他只好像蹲骆驼似的蹲在马上。

===Original===

Ladislaus Csaky bawled after him once more not to forget what he had told him, whereupon the poet, quite unintentionally, gave his horse the spur, and dashed madly off at full tilt over stock and stone. Mantle, sabre, and knapsack flew about the ears of the unfortunate horseman, who held on to his saddle with both hands in mortal agony, to the intense delight of the whole population of Toroczko, who were sitting in groups outside their houses on their *beard-driers*, as the benches used to be called in those days.

---

First of all the Patrol-officer took the road to Abrudbánya. Formerly, while he still had a servant, Clement used to leave all the pioneering to him; but now he was forced to find his way from village to village himself, with the occasional assistance of the country magistrates.

He had just quitted the narrow mountain path, and was ambling slowly over a dilapidated bridge, which spanned a brawling stream, when he perceived in the thicket a group of dirty-looking men crouching over a large fire. At first he took them for gypsies, but, approaching nearer, was horrified to discover that they were Tartars, who had dismounted from their horses, and were sitting round an ox which they had roasted whole.

To turn back was scarcely advisable; but the road he was following went straight past the diners. Clement was in a fix; but he determined at last to put a bold face on the matter, so he trotted by the gaping group with affected nonchalance, pretending to be intent all the while on calculating the exact number of acorns on the wayside oaks, and merely raised his hat to the Tartars with a brief “*Salem aleikum!*” when he came close up to them, as if he only then perceived them for the first time, passing quickly on without looking once behind him.

So far all was well, but at that very moment two of the Tartars sprang up from the fire and called to the rider to stop. Clement, perceiving that they were both unarmed, argued therefrom that they had no murderous designs upon him, and therefore halted and awaited them.

No sooner had the two dog-headed figures come up to him, one on each side, than they caught hold of his legs and displayed no less an intention than to rob him of his beautiful boots.

===Chinese===

拉斯洛·恰基又在他背后大喊了一遍，叫他别忘了嘱咐的话；诗人闻声，全然无意地刺了一下马，那马便不管不顾，跨沟过石没命狂奔。斗篷、马刀、行囊在这位倒霉骑手的耳朵边乱飞，他双手死死抠住马鞍，苦不堪言——托罗茨科全体的居民则大饱眼福：他们正一群群坐在自家门前的"晾须凳"上（当年的人们管那种长凳就这么叫）。

---

巡察官首先取道去阿鲁德巴尼亚（Abrudbánya）。从前，仆人还在的时候，探路问径的活克莱门特全都交给他；如今他只得自己一个村一个村地摸索着走，偶尔靠乡间官吏帮个忙。

他刚出了狭窄的山径，正缓步走过一座横跨湍溪的破桥，忽见树丛里有一群灰头土脸的人，围着一堆大火蹲着。起初他当是吉卜赛人，走近一看，惊恐地发现竟是鞑靼人（Tartar）：他们已下了马，正围着一头整烤的牛坐着。

往回走是不妥的；可他走的这条路偏偏直穿用餐者们的身边。克莱门特进退两难；最后他决定索性壮起胆子装无事，便小跑着从那群张着嘴的人前面经过，一路假装专心数着路边橡树上橡子的确切数目，直到挨近鞑靼人才短促地喊一声"*Salem aleikum*（愿你好运）！"抬一抬帽子，仿佛这时才头一回瞧见他们，随即匆匆而过，一次也不回头。

到此为止一切尚好，可就在这当口，两个鞑靼人从火堆边跳起来，喝令骑手站住。克莱门特见两人都赤手空拳，便推断他们没有害命之意，于是勒马等候。

那两个狗头人身的家伙一左一右赶到他跟前，便一把抓住他的双腿，摆明了是打算抢他脚下那双漂亮的靴子。

===Original===

“Would you? ye sons of Belial!” cried Clement, beside himself with rage, and grasping the hilt of his sword he tried to pull it from its leather sheath, in order to cut off the ears of his assailants forthwith. But the good blade, which had not quitted its sheath for ten years, had grown so rusty that Clement, despite all his endeavours, could not pluck it forth, and in the meantime the two Tartars pulled the wriggling rider hither and thither by the legs, naturally without succeeding in loosening the tight-fitting boots in the least. The Tartars reviled Clement, and Clement reviled the Tartars: their language was perfectly horrible.

The noise brought the Aga to the spot﻿—an orangutan-like object whose mahogany features were framed by a white beard﻿—and he asked in a hoarse whisper what was the matter.

Clement the Clerk at once drew his credentials from the pocket of his mente, and shook it in the Aga’s face﻿—he was too wrathful to speak﻿—while the Tartars, pointing with frantic gestures at the boots, jabbered something to the Aga.

“Who art thou, O bowlegged unbeliever!” asked the Aga, “that thou dost presume to wear on thy lowest extremities, on thy mud-wading feet, forsooth! the sacred colour of the Prophet, that radiant green which the faithful may only behold on the arches of their mosques and on the turban of the Padishah? Thou shalt be burned alive, thou godless Giaour!”

“I am the Patrol-officer of his Highness Prince Michael Apafi!” declaimed the ex-student, with terrified pathos. “My person is sacred and inviolable. I am he who provides the host of the sublime Sultan with meat and drink; I proclaim and collect the taxes, so let me go, for I am a very important personage.”

This mode of defence pleased the Tartars. The Aga exchanged glances with his subalterns, as much as to say﻿—“This is the very man we want!” and addressed him again in a more friendly tone.

“Dost thou indeed collect the taxes? Look now! my master, Ali Pasha of Grosswardein, has sent me hither to notify to the people a fresh imposition. Allah hath clearly brought us together. Thou wilt act discreetly then by proclaiming the new tax at once. It is no more than thy duty.”

“I’ll do so gladly,” replied Clement, who made as if he were going.

===Chinese===

"你们敢！彼列的崽子们！"克莱门特怒不可遏地喊道，抓住剑柄想把它从皮鞘里拔出来，好当场削掉这两个家伙的耳朵。可这把十年不曾出鞘的好剑早已锈死，克莱门特使出浑身解数也拔它不动；这当儿，两个鞑靼人拽着他那双乱蹬的腿拖来拖去，自然也丝毫没能松动那双紧巴巴的靴子。鞑靼人骂克莱门特，克莱门特骂鞑靼人：双方的语言都难听极了。

喧闹声把阿迦引了过来——那是个猩猩似的家伙，红木色的面孔四周镶着一圈白胡须——他哑着嗓子低声问是怎么回事。

文书克莱门特立刻从门特（mente）口袋里掏出他的委任状，冲着阿迦的脸摇晃——他气得说不出话——鞑靼人则指着靴子，手势乱舞，朝阿迦哇啦哇啦嚷着什么。

"你是什么人，罗圈腿的不信主的家伙！"阿迦喝问，"竟敢把你那最卑贱的肢体、你那蹚泥的脚——好哇！——裹上先知的圣色，那璀璨的绿色？那颜色，虔诚信士只许在清真寺的拱顶上和帕迪沙（Padishah）的头巾上瞻仰！你该活活烧死，无法无神的异教徒（Giaour）！"

"我乃米哈伊·阿帕菲亲王殿下的巡察官！"前大学生悲壮地朗诵道，吓出一身戏。"我的人身神圣不可侵犯。是我为至高苏丹（Sultan）的大军供应饮食；是我宣令收税，所以放我走，我是非常非常重要的人物。"

这个辩解的路数让鞑靼人们很满意。阿迦与部下们交换了一下眼色，那意思分明是——"我们要的正是这个人！"随即换上友好的口气对他说话。

"你当真管收税？瞧！我家主人、大瓦尔代（Grosswardein）的阿里帕夏（Ali Pasha），派我到此向百姓宣示一项新税。真主显然把你我凑到一处了。那么你立刻宣示新税，便是识相之举。这本是你的分内之事。"

"乐意效劳。"克莱门特答道，做出要走的样子。

===Original===

“Stay, my son,” said the Aga, beckoning to him. “Thou dost not even know yet the amount of the new tax. ’Tis a mere trifle, and only imposed by way of showing that we are the masters here. ’Tis only a farthing per head. That’s not much, I’m sure.”

“Nothing at all!” assented Clement, eager to be off.

“Not so fast! not so fast!” remonstrated the Aga. “I shall not be best pleased if thou dost disobey my orders; but as I know that thou dost not regard it as perjury to break promises made to us, I’ll tell off one of my brave fellows here to accompany thee from village to village, and take care that thou dost duly proclaim the new tax whithersoever thou goest.”

“It is well, gracious sir,” said Clement meekly, with the mental reservation of ridding himself of the brave fellow at the very next village.

“Mount your horse, Zülfikar,” cried the Aga to one of his servants.

The person addressed was an evil-looking fellow with a malignant squint. Although just as dirty as the others, it was clear from his physiognomy that he was not made of the same stuff, and if we condescended to bestow any thought at all upon such low people, it might even occur to us that we had seen him somewhere else before.

“As for thee,” said the Aga to Clement, who was anxious to be off at any price, “take off thy boots as soon as thou gettest home, and if ever I meet thee with them on again, thou shalt receive from me five hundred strokes on the soles of thy feet, which thou wilt have cause to recollect even on thy wedding-day.”

Clement the Clerk said “Yes” to everything, rejoiced that he had got off at last, and trotted off towards Abrudbánya. His Tartar escort rode faithfully by his side.

From time to time the Patrol-officer cast a sidelong glance at his companion, only quickly to avert his eyes again, for as the Tartar squinted horribly, Clement could never exactly make out which way he was looking. Clement was thinking all the while how easily he would give the Tartar the slip, smiled to himself at the thought, winked with both eyes, and nodded his head with a self-satisfied air.

“Mr. Patrol-officer, don’t fancy you will circumvent me as you go your rounds!” exclaimed the Tartar suddenly, in the purest Hungarian, as if he could read Clement’s thought from his face.

===Chinese===

"且慢，我的孩子。"阿迦朝他招手道，"你还不知道新税的数目呢。不过区区小数，加征它只为表明：这里是我们当家。每人不过一法新。这不算多吧，我担保。"

"根本不算什么！"克莱门特附和道，一心想脱身。

"别急！别急！"阿迦拦道，"你若违抗我的命令，我可是要不高兴的；不过我知道，你们不把对我们失信当作背誓，所以我要点我一名勇士跟着你，一个村一个村地陪你去，看着你走到哪里，就把新税照章宣到哪里。"

"就这么办，仁慈的老爷。"克莱门特温顺地说，心里却盘算着到了下一个村子就把那位勇士甩掉。

"上马，祖尔菲卡尔（Zülfikar）。"阿迦朝他的一名仆从喊道。

被点到的那家伙面目不善，斜眼透着狠劲。虽然跟旁人一样脏，可从面相上看得出他不是同一块料子做的；倘若我们肯屈尊为这等下等人费一点神，甚至可能会想起：这人我们在别处见过。

"至于你，"阿迦对不惜一切代价只想开溜的克莱门特说，"一回到家就脱掉这靴子；日后若再叫我撞见你穿着它们，我必赏你脚掌五百杖，管保你到成亲那天还记着这份情。"

文书克莱门特什么都答应"是"，庆幸总算脱了身，小跑着朝阿鲁德巴尼亚去了。他的鞑靼护卫忠实地骑行在旁。

巡察官不时斜眼瞟一瞟同伴，又赶紧把目光收回来，因为那鞑靼人斜视得厉害，克莱门特永远闹不清他正瞧着哪边。克莱门特一路盘算着甩掉这鞑靼人该有多容易，想到得意处独自微笑，双眼眨巴，脑袋自鸣得意地点着。

"巡察官先生，别做梦以为出巡路上能绕得过我！"鞑靼人突然开口，一口最纯正的匈牙利话，仿佛能从克莱门特脸上读出他的心事。

===Original===

Clement was so aghast that he almost fell from his horse. How the deuce could the fellow snap up his very thoughts, and speak Hungarian despite his Tartardom?

“Don’t bother your head about me any more,” continued the Turk calmly. “I am an Hungarian renegade who was once in the service of Emerich Balassa. I had a hand in the capture and poisoning of Corsar Beg, and when the Hungarians began to persecute me on that account, I turned Turk. If the Prophet befriend me, I may yet rise to be Kapudan Pasha. Pray don’t imagine you can bamboozle a wily old fox like me.”

Clement, completely disconcerted, could only scratch his head, proceeded with his escort from village to village, and after accomplishing his regular official business, proclaimed the fresh imposition of a farthing per head, which the people everywhere received most favourably, in many cases even paying it down at once to his Tartar comrade.

But no one knew anything about the panther. Indeed, but for the respect inspired by his gallooned green boots, the Patrol-officer would have been laughed out of countenance.

Only one little Wallachian village up in the mountains, called Marisel, was yet to be visited, and beyond that place began the domains of Baron Banfi, where the jurisdiction of the Patrol-officer terminated.

Thither also the renegade followed him.

===Chinese===

克莱门特惊得险些栽下马来。这家伙怎么见鬼的能一把叼住他的念头，又怎么一身鞑靼气竟说得一口匈牙利话？

"别再为我费脑子了。"那土耳其人不慌不忙地接着说，"我是个匈牙利叛教者，从前在埃梅里希·巴拉沙手下当差。擒拿、毒杀科尔萨尔贝伊（Corsar Beg）的事，我都插过一手；后来匈牙利人为这事开始收拾我，我便归了土耳其教。若得先知眷顾，我兴许还能当上卡普丹帕夏呢。请你别妄想糊弄我这只老奸巨猾的狐狸。"

克莱门特彻底乱了方寸，只会挠头；他由护卫陪着走村串乡，办完例行的公务，便宣示了每人一法新的新征，所到之处百姓无不欣然接受，许多地方甚至当场把税钱付到了他那鞑靼同伴手里。

只是谁也没有听说过什么豹子。说实话，若不是那双镶绦的绿靴子震慑住了众人，巡察官早被人笑话得下不来台了。

只剩山里一个叫玛丽谢尔（Marisel）的小瓦拉几亚村庄还没查访，过了那地方，便是班菲男爵的领地，巡察官的管辖权到那里便告终止。

那叛教者也一路跟着他到了那里。

## II / 二
===Original===

Sange Moarte28

The Patrol-officer and his companion had already been travelling for half the day across the Batrina moor on their way to Marisel. Clement kept on asking every living soul he met where the village was, and always received the same answer﻿—“Further on!”

From time to time they met a Wallachian peasant reviling the team of sluggish oxen spanned to his huge wagon, and vainly endeavouring to make them quicken their pace; then there were ponds to be waded, where half-naked gipsy bands, in picturesque rags, were washing gold-dust out of the sand, and stared at the Tartar as if he were a wild beast; here and there, in the mossy hollow of a wayside tree, stood an icon, the pale, weatherworn gilding of which being all that remained of its once gorgeous colouring; in the worm-eaten niche stood the *pomana*,29 a pitcher of pure spring water which the traditional piety of the young Wallachian maidens had placed there for the refreshment of thirsty travellers.

The road now went up hill and down dale; for the greater part of the way they had to lead their horses. All around stood the ever-changing wilderness; lofty, perpendicular beeches, terebinthine oaks, with an occasional dark-green pine. At last they reached a point where the road divided. One branch of it ran right down into the valley, the other wound obliquely up to the summit of a bald bleak hill, from which a projecting rock hung down so precipitately that it seemed ready to fall every moment.

“Well, whither shall we turn now?” asked Clement, hesitating. “I have never come so far as this before.”

“Let us follow the road,” returned Zülfikar; “none but a fool would risk his neck up that steep cliff.”

Clement looked about him in great perplexity, and suddenly perceived a man sitting on the rock which so precipitately overhung the path. It was a young Wallach with a pale face and long, flowing curls; his sheepskin jacket was open at the breast, his cap lay beside him on the ground. There he sat in a reverie, on the very edge of the lofty rock, with his feet dangling in empty space, his stony countenance resting on his hands, and his eyes staring glassily into the remote distance.

“Hi! you up there! *ungye méra ista via?*”30 cried Clement, in a jargon which was half Latin and half Wallachian.

===Chinese===

桑格·莫阿尔特（注28）

巡官和他的同伴前往马里谢尔（Marisel），已经赶了半天路，一路穿行在巴特里纳（Batrina）荒原上。克莱门特逢人便问村子在哪儿，得到的回答总是那一个——“往前走！”

他们不时遇上个瓦拉几亚（Wallachian）农夫，正对着套在大车前的那队慢牛破口大骂，白费力气地想催它们快走；接着又要蹚过一片片池沼，半裸的吉卜赛人群穿着花花绿绿的破衣裳，正在沙里淘金砂，见了那鞑靼人（Tartar）就跟见了野兽似的直瞪；路旁树上生苔的树洞里，东一处西一处供着圣像，昔日金碧辉煌的颜色只剩下风吹雨打后的一层淡金；虫蛀的壁龛里摆着波马纳（pomana）（注29）——一罐清洌的泉水，是瓦拉几亚年轻姑娘们按祖辈传下的虔诚摆在那儿，给过路口渴的旅人解渴的。

道路忽上忽下；大半路程他们只得牵着马走。四下里是千变万化的荒野；笔直高耸的山毛榉、脂香扑鼻的橡树，间或夹着一棵墨绿的松。终于他们到了一个岔路口。一条道直落谷底，另一条斜着盘上一座光秃秃的荒山顶，山顶探出一块岩石，悬得那么陡，仿佛随时要掉下来。

“咱们现在往哪边走？”克莱门特迟疑地问，“我从来没到过这么远的地方。”

“顺着大路走呗，”祖尔菲卡尔（Zülfikar）答道，“只有傻子才肯拿脖子去冒那个险，爬那么陡的崖。”

克莱门特大为茫然地四下张望，忽然看见那块悬在路面上方的岩石上坐着一个人。那是个年轻的瓦拉几亚人，脸色苍白，垂着长长的鬈发；皮袄敞着怀，帽子扔在手边的地上。他就那样坐在高崖的最边上出神，双脚悬在半空里晃荡，石头一样的脸支在双手上，眼睛直勾勾地瞪着极远处。

“嗨！上头那位！ungye méra ista via?（注30）”克莱门特用半拉丁半瓦拉几亚的黑话喊道。

===Original===

The Wallach did not appear to hear the question; he remained in just the same position, blankly staring and immovable.

“He is either deaf or dead,” said Zülfikar, after they had both bawled themselves hoarse at him in vain. “The best thing we can do is to follow the beaten track,” and off they set at a trot. The Wallach did not so much as look after them.

Evening was drawing nigh, and the road to Marisel seemed absolutely endless. It went out of one valley into another, without passing a single human habitation, and the huge boulders and fierce mountain torrents, which they came upon at frequent intervals, made it almost impassable. At last they perceived, somewhere in the wood, a fire burning, and a monotonous chant struck upon their ears. On approaching nearer, they saw an immense pyre, made of the trunks of trees, burning in a forest glade, and shaded by oaks, the foliage of which was singed red by the long tongues of flame which flickered up to their very summits.

Not far from the pyre, a band of Wallachs were dancing with savage gesticulations, striking the ground at the same time with their massive clubs. Their twirling feet seemed to be writing mystic characters in the soil, and all the while they brandished their arms and howled forth metrical curses as if they were exorcising some evil spirit.

Around the men twined a wreath of young girls, holding one another by the hand, and twirling in a contrary direction. These young and charming forms, with their black, plaited tresses interwoven with pearls and ribbons; their flowered petticoats, cambric smocks, and broad, striped aprons; their tinkling gold spangles, or strings of silver coins about their round necks and their tiny, high-heeled shoes, formed a pleasant contrast to the wild, ferocious figures of the men, with their high sheepskin hats perched upon their shaggy, unkempt hair, their sunburnt, naked necks, greasy *köduröns*,31 broad brass buckles, and large ox-hide sandals.

===Chinese===

那瓦拉几亚人好像没听见问话；依旧保持着原来的姿势，呆望着，一动不动。

“他不是聋子就是死人，”两人朝他喊得嗓子都哑了也没用，祖尔菲卡尔说，“咱们顶好还是走现成的大路。”说罢便策马小跑而去。那瓦拉几亚人连头也不回一下。

暮色渐近，通往马里谢尔的路仿佛没有尽头。它从一个山谷钻进另一个山谷，沿途不见一个人家；动辄横在面前的大圆石和汹涌的山涧，几乎把路堵死。终于他们望见林子里什么地方燃着一堆火，一阵单调的歌吟传进耳中。走近一看，只见林间空地上烧着一座巨大的柴堆，全是整根的树干搭成，上方橡树掩覆，长长的火舌一路舐到树梢，把树叶燎得通红。

离柴堆不远，一伙瓦拉几亚人正狂野地手舞足蹈地跳着，同时用粗大的木棍咚咚砸地。他们旋转的双脚仿佛在泥土上写着神秘的符文；一面抡着胳膊，一面拖着调子高声咒骂，像是在驱除什么恶灵。

男人们外圈环着一串手拉手的姑娘，朝相反的方向旋转。这些年轻娇美的身影——乌黑的发辫里编着珍珠和缎带；花裙子、细麻布衬衣、宽大的条纹围裙；浑圆的脖颈上叮当作响的金亮片或银币串，脚上是小巧的高跟鞋——同男人们粗野凶悍的形象恰成有趣对照：高高的羊皮帽子扣在蓬乱的头发上，晒得黝黑的光脖子，油腻腻的库杜龙（ködurön）（注31）皮袄，宽大的黄铜扣子，牛皮凉鞋。

===Original===

Both dance and song were peculiar. The girls, all hand in hand, flew round the men, singing a plaintive, dreamy sort of dirge, while the men stamped fiercely on the ground and uttered an intermittent wail. The fire blazing beside them cast a red glare, intermingled with dark flitting shadows, on the wild group. Some distance behind, on the stump of a tree, sat an old bagpiper with his pipes under his arm. The tortured goatskin’s monotonous discord blended with the savage harmony of the song.

When the pyre had nearly burnt itself out, the dancers suddenly dispersed, dragged forward a female effigy stuffed with straw and clothed in rags, placed it on two poles, and with loud cries of “Marcze Záre! Marcze Záre!”32 held it over the fire; then, exclaiming in chorus﻿—“Burn to ashes, accursed Wednesday-evening witch!” they threw it into the glowing embers. The girls then danced round the fire with cries of joy till the witch was burned, when the men, with a wild yell, rushed among the embers and trod them out.

“Who are ye, and what are you doing here?” cried Clement the Clerk to the Wallachs, who hitherto had not taken the slightest notice of him.

“We are they of Marisel who have burned Marcze Záre,” answered the peasants unanimously, with the grave faces of men who had just done something uncommonly wise.

“Well, be quick about it, and then come back to the village, for I am here by command of the Prince, my master, to put the usual questions to you.”

“And I,” put in Zülfikar, “am here by command of the mighty Ali Pasha of Grosswardein to levy a new tax.”

The Wallachs watched the Patrol-officer till he was quite out of sight without uttering a word; but they shook their fists after him and exclaimed﻿—“May Marcze Záre take him!”

Then, with the bagpiper in front, they formed into a long procession and marched, loudly singing, down towards the distant village.

---

===Chinese===

舞也好，歌也好，都别有一样。姑娘们手拉着手，绕着男人飞旋，唱着一支哀婉如梦的挽歌；男人们狠命跺着地，发出一阵阵断续的嚎哭。身旁的烈火把红光投在这群狂野的人身上，光里搅动着暗影幢幢。后面不远处的树桩上，坐着个抱着风笛的老乐师。那面受罪的羊皮袋发出的单调噪音，同歌咏的野性曲调搅在一处。

柴堆快烧尽时，跳舞的人忽然散开，拖出一个塞满稻草、穿着破衣裳的女人草像，架在两根杆子上，高喊着“马尔采·扎雷！马尔采·扎雷！”（注32）把它举到火上；接着齐声呐喊——“烧成灰吧，该咒的星期三晚上女巫！”——把它扔进了通红的炭火里。随后姑娘们围着火堆欢呼跳跃，直到女巫烧完；男人们狂呼一声，冲进余烬，把火踏灭。

“你们是什么人，在这儿干什么？”书记官克莱门特朝瓦拉几亚人们喊道——直到这时他们还没把他放在眼里。

“我们是马里谢尔烧马尔采·扎雷的人。”农夫们异口同声地答道，个个板着脸，一副刚干完一件顶顶聪明大事的神气。

“好了，快点忙完，都回村里去，我奉我家亲王之命，来照例向你们问话。”

“还有我，”祖尔菲卡尔插嘴道，“奉大瓦尔德因（Grosswardein）威武的阿里帕夏（Ali Pasha）之命，来征收新税。”

瓦拉几亚人一言不发，目送巡官走得没了影；却朝他背后挥舞拳头，嚷道——“叫马尔采·扎雷把他抓去吧！”

随后，风笛师领头，他们排成长长一列，高唱着朝远处的村庄走去。

---

===Original===

It was a long, straggling, Wallachian hamlet at which the patrollers now arrived﻿—one house exactly like another; low clay huts with lofty roofs and projecting eaves, surrounded by quick-set hedges, the doors so low that one had to stoop in order to enter. Every house consisted of a single room, in which the whole family, parents and children, goats and poultry, lived together. At the entrance of the village stood a gigantic triumphal arch made of marble blocks; over the principal portal was the torso of a Minerva; on the façade were battle-pieces in high relief, and beneath them this Latin inscription in large Roman letters﻿—“This town has been built by the unconquerable Trajan as a memorial of his triumph!” And behind the arch a heap of wretched clay huts!

On the capitol of a fallen Corinthian column, in front of the village dead-house, sits the *prefika*, the oldest old woman in the place, lamenting with meretricious tears over the dead young maiden who lies within. On the side of a grass-grown hill close at hand one sees a round stone building, raised once upon a time, no doubt, in memory of some Roman hero; but the Wallachian population has turned it into a church, covered it with a pointed roof, and daubed the interior with hideous paintings.

The Patrol-officer called the people together into the church, which was the only public building in the place. The crowd stood around him, the old men leaning on their crutches. The bloodred rays of sunset pierced through the round windowpanes, giving a peculiar appearance to the interior of the venerable edifice, whose walls were daubed all over with figures of grotesque saints, whom the monstrous fancy of the rustic artist had provided with scarlet mantles and spurred jackboots. Amongst so many pictures of the marvellous, that well-known allegory which represents Death as a skeleton, dragging off with him a king, a beggar, and a priest, was not lacking, and scattered among the icons were a few bandy-legged fiends derisively stretching out their tongues at poor damned sinners whom they clutched tightly by the hair.

===Chinese===

巡查二人组此刻到达的，是一长条散散落落的瓦拉几亚村落——家家的房子一模一样：低矮的土屋，高高的屋顶，出挑的屋檐，围着一圈绿篱，门矮得进门必得弯腰。每户人家只有一间屋，全家老小连同鸡羊，都住在一起。

村口立着一座大理石砌成的巨型凯旋门；正门楣上是密涅瓦（Minerva）的躯像；门面饰着高浮雕的战争场面，下面是罗马大字母的拉丁铭文——“此城乃不可战胜之图拉真（Trajan）所建，以志其凯旋！”而凯旋门后面，却是一堆破烂土屋！

村中停尸房前，一根倒伏的科林斯式石柱的柱头上，坐着普雷菲卡（prefika）——全村最老的老妇——正挤着做作的泪水，哭悼屋里停放的死去的少女。近旁一座青草覆盖的山坡上，看得见一座圆形石建筑，想必是从前为纪念哪位罗马英雄而建；可瓦拉几亚人把它改成了教堂，戴上个尖顶，又在里头涂满了丑陋不堪的画。

巡官把民众召集到教堂里——那是村里唯一像样的公共建筑。人群围着他站定，老头们拄着拐杖。血红的落日透过圆窗照进来，给这座古老建筑的内部添上一种奇特的景象：四壁涂满了奇形怪状的圣徒像，乡下画师的荒唐想象力还给这些圣徒穿上了猩红大氅、蹬上了带马刺的长统靴。这么多神神怪怪的画里，自然少不了那幅著名的寓意画——死神是个髅髅，拖着一个国王、一个乞丐和一个教士；圣像之间还散布着几个罗圈腿的小鬼，朝他们紧揪着头发的该下地狱的可怜罪人们伸着舌头嘲笑。

===Original===

Behind the iconastasis the priest and the Patrol-officer took their stand, surrounded by gilded icons and consecrated candles. When Clement had read his credentials to the people, he called to the village elder, a tall man with large projecting teeth, to come in front of the altar-rail, and addressed the following questions to him﻿—

“Are there amongst you any sorcerers and magicians who can summon the devil to their aid?”

The crowd received this question with an awful whisper, and after a long pause the magistrate replied﻿—

“There was one last year, your worship, a godless villain with blotches on his neck and body, which were patches stitched on to him by the devil, for even when we singed them with red-hot irons he did not feel it. We sent him to the Sanhedrim at Fehervár, where, failing to stand the water test, he was burnt alive.”

“Are there among you any hags or vampires which injure other people’s children, make knots in men’s bowels, ride through the air, colour milk red, hatch serpents’ eggs, or seek for grasses which can make them invisible, and open barred and bolted doors?”

This question called forth a hundred different answers. Everyone tried to communicate his own personal experiences to the interrogator; the younger women in particular pressed upon the Patrol-officer with furious importunity.

“One at a time, please,” cried Clement, with great dignity. “Let the magistrate say what he knows.”

===Chinese===

神父和巡官在圣像壁后站定，四周是镀金圣像和祝圣过的蜡烛。克莱门特向民众宣读了委任状，便把村长——一个龅牙凸出的高个子——叫到圣坛栏杆前，向他提出下列问题——

“你们中间可有会念咒召魔鬼来帮忙的巫师术士？”

人群听了这问题，发出一阵骇人的窃窃私语；半晌，村长才答道——

“回大人的话，去年倒有一个，是个脖子上身上长斑的无法无天的恶棍，那些斑是魔鬼给他缝上去的补丁——我们用烧红的烙铁去烫，他都觉不出疼。我们把他送到了费海尔瓦尔（Fehervár）的公议会，他在那儿没通过水审，就被活活烧死了。”

“你们中间可有妖婆、吸血鬼——害人家的孩子、在人肚肠里打结、夜里腾空飞行、把牛奶变红、孵蛇蛋、寻隐身草、开得上闩上锁的门的那种？”

这个问题引出了百种回答。人人都争着向问话人讲述自己的亲历；年轻的媳妇姑娘们尤其缠着巡官，缠得他不得安宁。

“一个一个来，”克莱门特极有威仪地喊道，“让村长说他知道的。”

===Original===

“Yes, there used to be an old witch here, worshipful sir,” said the village elder obsequiously. “We called her Dainitsa.33 She had long molested mankind, for her eyes were red. She could, when she chose, bring down such storms upon the village that the wind would take off the roofs of the houses. Once she brought a hailstorm upon us, and God’s thunderbolt smote the village in three places. Thereupon the women here grew furious, seized her, and threw her into the pond. But even there the witch railed upon them and said﻿—‘Take heed! You will live to beg of me the water which you now give me to drink!’ Then the women fished up her dead body from the bottom of the pond, thrust a dart through her heart, buried her in the valley, and rolled a large stone over her grave. But the very next year the witch’s curse came upon us. Throughout the summer not a drop of rain fell in our district. Everything was withered up, and our cattle carried off by the murrain. Dainitsa had drunk up all the rain and dew. So we went to her grave, bored a large hole therein, and filled the grave with water till it ran over, shouting at the same time﻿—‘Drink thy fill, accursed hag! but lap not up all our rain and dew!’ And so at last the great drought came to an end.”

The priest gravely vouched for the accuracy of this narrative, and Clement made a note of it in his parchment roll.

Now came the third question.

“Are there any among you who dare to smoke tobacco, either by cutting up the leaves into small fragments and putting them in his pipe, or by roasting them on the fire and inhaling the ascending steam?”

“There are none, sir!” returned the elder. “We do not know that dish.”

“And do not try to, for whoever is caught in the act will, in accordance with the law of the land, have the stem of his pipe thrust through his nose, and be led in that guise all round the marketplace.”

The fourth question was this﻿—

“Do any of the peasants wear cloth coats, marten-skin kalpags, or morocco shoes?”

“Pshaw!” cried the village elder. “Why, our poverty is such that we never look beyond sheepskin jackets and leather sandals. What do we want with coloured cloth and morocco shoes?”

“Nor must you, for the Estates of the Realm have forbidden the peasantry to wear the clothes of the gentry.”

Now came the fifth question.

===Chinese===

“有，回大老爷的话，这儿从前是有一个老妖婆，”村长谄媚地说，“我们叫她达伊尼察（注33）。她祸害人可有些年头了，不信看她那双红眼睛。她高兴起来，能给村里招来那种狂风，把屋顶都掠跑。有一回她给我们降了场冰雹，上帝的雷霆把村子劈了三处。村里的女人们火了，把她抓住，扔进了池塘。可到了水里那妖婆还骂不绝口，说——‘记住！往后你们会有来求我的时候，求我喝你们如今灌我的这水！’女人们便从塘底捞上她的尸首，用木桩穿透她的心，把她埋在谷里，坟上还滚了块大石头。可第二年妖婆的咒就应验了。整整一个夏天，我们这地方没落一滴雨。庄稼全枯了，牲口也遭了瘟疫。是达伊尼察把雨水露水全喝干了。于是我们到她坟上，钻了个大洞，往坟里灌水，一直灌到外溢，一边灌一边喊——‘喝个够吧，该咒的老妖婆！可别把我们全国的雨露都舐了去！’这才终于结束了那场大旱。”

神父郑重担保这段叙述句句属实，克莱门特把它记在了羊皮卷上。

接着是第三个问题。

“你们中间可有人敢抽烟——把烟叶切碎了装进烟斗抽，或是搁在火上烤着吸那冒上来的烟气的？”

“没有，老爷！”村长答道，“那道菜我们没吃过。”

“也别去尝试，谁要当场被拿了，按国法，烟斗杆就要从他鼻子里穿过去，再叫他顶着那副模样游遍整个集市。”

第四个问题是——

“可有哪个农夫穿呢子外套、貂皮高帽、摩洛哥皮鞋的？”

“呸！”村长嚷道，“瞧您说的，我们穷得除了羊皮袄、皮凉鞋，什么都不想。花呢子、摩洛哥皮鞋，我们要它做什么？”

“也不许要：邦国各等级早已明令，禁止农家穿绅士的衣裳。”

接着是第五个问题。

===Original===

“Which of you not only acted contrary to the decree of the Diet, that the peasants should extirpate the sparrows, but even mocked the officers charged to collect sparrows’ heads?”

The magistrate humbly approached the Patrol-officer.

“Believe me, worshipful sir; by reason of the great drought and the bad season, the sparrows have all departed from our district. Tell his Highness that we have been unable to lay our hands upon a single one all through the summer.”

“That’s a lie!” cried Clement the Clerk fiercely.

“I speak the truth,” persisted the magistrate, seizing Clement by the hand, and dexterously insinuating two silver *marias* into his clenched fist.

“Well, it is not impossible,” said the Patrol-officer, somewhat mollified.

Last of all came the question﻿—

“Has any among you seen foreign beasts of prey, or other strange animals, straying about in these regions?”

“Of a truth, sir, we have seen lots of them.”

“And what sort of beasts were they?” asked Clement, with joyful curiosity.

“Well, dog-headed Tartars!”

“You fool, I don’t mean that sort of beast. I want to know whether anyone, in strolling through these woods, has come upon a four-footed beast of prey, a creature with a spotted skin? You know very well you have left no hole or corner unexplored, for even now you are hunting after the hidden treasures of Decebalus.”

The magistrate shook his head incredulously, glanced at the crowd, and said, with a shrug of his shoulders﻿—

“We have seen no such wondrous beast; but haply Sange Moarte has seen it, for he in his mad moods roams incessantly through woods and hollows.”

“And where then is this Sange Moarte? You must call him hither.”

“Alas! sir, he is difficult to catch; he seldom comes to the village. But perhaps his mother is here.”

“Here she is! here she is!” cried several peasants at once, pushing forward an old woman with sunken cheeks, whose head was wrapped round in a white cloth.

“What mad name is this you have given to your son?” cried the Patrol-officer; “whoever heard of calling a man ‘Dead blood’!”

===Chinese===

“议会有令，叫农民剿灭麻雀。你们中间有谁不但抗旨不遵，还敢嘲笑奉命收缴麻雀头的官员？”

村长卑微地凑近巡官。

“您千万相信小的，大老爷；因为大旱加坏年成，麻雀都离开我们这地界了。禀告殿下，一夏天我们连一只麻雀都没能逮着。”

“胡说！”书记官克莱门特厉声喝道。

“小的说的句句实话，”村长坚持着，一把抓住克莱门特的手，麻利地把两枚玛丽亚银币塞进他攥紧的拳头里。

“唔，那倒也不是没可能。”巡官的口气软了些。

最后是这个问题——

“你们中间可有人见过外地的猛兽，或是别的怪兽，在这一带游荡？”

“说真的，老爷，见得多了。”

“都是些什么兽？”克莱门特喜滋滋地问。

“嗨，狗头鞑靼人呗！”

“你这蠢货，我不是说那种兽。我要知道的是：有没有人在林子里转悠的时候，撞见过四条腿的猛兽，一身花斑皮的？你们心里清楚得很，这一带哪个洞哪个旮旯你们没翻遍——你们不是到这会儿还在挖德切巴鲁斯（Decebalus）藏的宝贝么。”

村长不信地摇摇头，扫了一眼人群，耸耸肩说——

“这样的怪兽我们没见过；没准儿桑格·莫阿尔特见过，他那疯劲儿上来，整天在林子里沟壑里转悠。”

“那么这个桑格·莫阿尔特在哪儿？你们得把他叫来。”

“唉，老爷，他难抓得很，轻易不回村。不过他娘倒兴许在。”

“在这儿！在这儿！”几个农夫齐声喊，把一个两颊深陷的老妇人推上前，她头上缠着白布。

“你们给你儿子起的这是什么疯名字？”巡官喝道，“谁听说过管人叫‘死血’的！”

===Original===

“ ’Twas not I, sir, who gave him this name,” said the old Wallachian woman with a broken voice. “The villagers call him so because he is never seen to laugh or speak to anyone, or answer when he is spoken to. He did not even weep for his father when he died; nor has he ever visited the girls in the spinning-rooms, but wanders about incessantly in the woods.”

“All right, all right, old lady; but that has nothing to do with me.”

“I know it, sir, I know that it does not concern you; but I must tell you that the pretty Floriza, the belle of the village, was in love with my son. There was not a lovelier maiden in all Wallachia. Such black eyes, such locks reaching down to her feet, such rosy cheeks, such a slim waist were not to be found anywhere else. And then she was so diligent, and she loved my son so dearly! In her chest she had sixteen embroidered chemises which she herself had woven and spun, and round her neck she wore a string of two hundred silver and twenty gold pieces. Sange Moarte never so much as looked at the girl. Vainly did Floriza make him posies, he would not put them in his hat; vainly did she give him kerchiefs, he would not wear them in his breast. Whenever he passed by, the girl would sing such beautiful songs as she sat by the hearth; but Sange Moarte for all that did not linger at her threshold, and yet she loved him so dearly. Often she said to him, when they met together in the lane﻿—‘Thou dost never come to see me; perchance thou wouldst not even look at me if I were dead?’ Sange Moarte replied﻿—‘Then indeed I would look at thee.’﻿—‘Then I will soon die,’ said the maiden sorrowfully. ‘And then will I also visit thee,’ said Sange Moarte, and went his way. Does all this weary you, good sir? I shall soon have done. Pretty Floriza lies dead. Her heart broke for grief. There she lies on her bier; the funereal *armindenu*34 stands in front of the house. When Sange Moarte sees it he will know that Floriza is dead, and will come forth from the woods to look upon his dead sweetheart, as he promised her, for he always keeps his word. Then you can speak with him.”

“Very well, old lady,” said Clement, who had suddenly become serious, and was almost angry to find something very like poetry among rude peasants, who had certainly never read Horace’s *Ars Poetica*. “You must watch for the lad’s return, and let me know.”

===Chinese===

“老爷，这名字不是我起的，”瓦拉几亚老妇声音发颤地说，“村里人这么叫他，是因为从没见他笑过，没见他跟谁说过话，别人跟他说话他也不答。他爹死的时候，他连滴眼泪都没掉；纺线房里的姑娘们那里，他也从没去过，就知道一天到晚在林子里转。”

“行了行了，老太太；这跟我没关系。”

“我知道，老爷，我知道这不干您的事；可我一定得说给您听：村里那俊俏的弗洛丽扎（Floriza），一村的花魁，她爱我的儿子。整个瓦拉几亚再找不出比她更好看的姑娘了。那样的黑眼睛，那样垂到脚面的长发，那样的红脸蛋，那样的细腰，别处哪儿都没有。而且她那么勤快，又那么真心爱我的儿子！她的箱子里存着十六件绣花衬衣，全是她自己纺自己织的；脖子上挂着一大串钱——二百枚银的，二十枚金的。可桑格·莫阿尔特连正眼也不瞧那姑娘。弗洛丽扎白给他扎花束，他不往帽子上插；白送他手帕，他不往怀里揣。他打门前过的时候，坐在炉边的姑娘就唱起那么好听的歌；可桑格·莫阿尔特照样不在她门槛边停留，而她还是那么爱他。两人在巷子里碰见，她常说——‘你从来不来看我；我要是死了，怕你连看都不看我一眼吧？’桑格·莫阿尔特答——‘那我一定会看你。’——‘那我很快就死。’姑娘伤心地说。‘到那时我也会去看你。’桑格·莫阿尔特说完就走了。这些话絮叨得您心烦了吧，好老爷？我快说完了。俊俏的弗洛丽扎死了。她的心愁碎了。她就停在那儿的灵床上；丧树阿尔明登（armindenu）（注34）立在房前。桑格·莫阿尔特一见那树，就知道弗洛丽扎死了，就会从林子里出来，来看他死去的情人——他答应过她的，他说话从来算数。到那时您就能同他说话了。”

“很好，老太太，”克莱门特说，忽然正经起来，在粗人中间发现近乎诗意的东西，他简直有点恼火——这些人分明连贺拉斯的《诗艺》都没读过。“你守着小伙子回来，来告诉我。”

===Original===

“ ’Twere better you went yourself, sir,” said the old woman, “for I scarcely think he will answer a single question put to him by anyone else.”

“Be it so! Lead me thither!” cried the Patrol-officer; and the whole assembly proceeded towards the mortuary, which stood at the extreme end of the village.

This end of Marisel is so far distant from the church, that night had fallen before the crowd had reached it.

The moon came from behind the mountains. Round about the house stood pine trees, through the sombre foliage of which the evening star shimmered faintly. In the distance sounded the melancholy notes of some pastoral flute. In front of the little white house the hired mourner was sobbing loudly. The wind agitated the crape-hung branches of the *armindenu*. Inside the house lay the corpse of the beautiful young maiden awaiting her truant lover. The moonbeams fell upon her pale countenance.

---

The mob surrounded the mortuary, crept stealthily on tiptoe into the courtyard, peeped through the window, and whispered﻿—

“Look! There he is! there he is!”

The Patrol-officer, the priest, the magistrate, and Sange Moarte’s mother entered the room.

Right across the threshold lay the girl’s father dead drunk; he got so tipsy yesterday from sheer sorrow that he will need all today and all tomorrow to sleep it off. In the middle of the room stood the pinewood coffin, bedaubed with glaring roses fresh from the brush of a rural artist; within it lay the girl (she was only sixteen), her beautiful forehead encircled by a funereal wreath. A wax taper had been placed in one of her hands, in the other she held a small coin. At the head of the coffin burned two handsome wax candles stuck into a jar containing gingerbreads; at the foot of the coffin, in a gaudily-painted, high-backed chair, staring blankly at the girl’s face, sat Sange Moarte.

The pious superstition of the priest and the magistrate would not let them cross the threshold; but Clement stepped up to the lad, and immediately recognized in him the man on the rock who would not tell him the way.

“Hi, young man! So you are he who has the bad habit of never replying to people when they address you, eh?”

The person thus addressed justified the question by not answering it.

“Now hearken and answer my question. I am the Patrol-officer. D’ye hear?”

===Chinese===

“老爷，还是您亲自去的好，”老妇人说，“换了别人问他，只怕他一个问题也不答。”

“就这样！给我带路！”巡官喊道；于是全体人众朝村尾的停尸房走去。

马里谢尔的村尾离教堂那么远，人群走到的时候，夜已经落下来了。

月亮从山后升起来。房子四周长着松树，昏暗的枝叶间，昏星微微闪烁。远处传来牧笛的忧郁调子。小白房子前，雇来的哭丧妇正放声呜咽。风吹动着丧树上缠黑纱的枝条。屋里躺着美丽少女的遗体，等候着她负心的情人。月光落在她苍白的脸上。

---

众人围住停尸房，踮着脚偷偷溜进院子，扒着窗往里看，悄声说——

“瞧！他在那儿！他在那儿！”

巡官、神父、村长和桑格·莫阿尔特的母亲走进屋去。

门槛上横躺着姑娘的父亲，烂醉如泥；昨天他纯粹是伤心才喝成那样，得今天睡一整天、明天再睡一整天才能缓过来。屋子正中立着松木棺材，上面涂抹着乡下画师手笔新画的俗艳玫瑰；棺中躺着姑娘（她才十六岁），好看的前额绕着一圈丧事花环。一只手里放着细蜡烛，另一只手里攥着一枚小钱。棺材头上，两支漂亮的蜡扦插在盛着姜饼的罐子里燃着；棺材脚头，一把彩漆高背椅上，坐着桑格·莫阿尔特，直勾勾地望着姑娘的脸。

神父和村长的虔诚迷信不让他们跨进门槛；克莱门特却径直走到小伙子跟前，立刻认出他就是岩上那个不肯指路的人。

“嗨，年轻人！原来你就是那个有人搭话从来不肯回的坏家伙，嗯？”

被问到的人用不回答证实了这一问。

“听着，回答我的问题。我是巡官。听见没有？”

===Original===

Sange Moarte remained speechless, with his eyes fixed all the time on Floriza. He was as motionless as the corpse itself, and scarcely seemed to breathe. His good old mother tenderly took him by the hand and called him by his proper name.

“Jova, my son! answer the gentleman. Look at me, I am your mother.”

“In the name of my master, the Prince, I command you to answer me!” cried the Patrol-officer, raising his voice.

The Wallach still remained silent.

“I ask you if, in the course of your sylvan ramblings, you have seen any sort of foreign wild beast, to wit a yellow, speckled monster, which the learned call a panther?”

Sange Moarte gave a start, as if suddenly aroused out of a deep sleep. His glassy eyes flashed and sparkled as he looked at his interrogator, a feverish scarlet flushed his cheeks, and he stammered tremulously﻿—

“I have seen it, seen it, seen it.”

And with that he covered his eyes, so as not to look upon the dead body.

“Where have you seen it?” asked the Patrol-officer.

“Far, far away,” whispered the Wallach; then he became dumb once more and buried his forehead in his hands.

“Name the place. Where is it?”

The Wallach looked timidly around. A cold shudder ran through him, and with fearful, rolling eyes he whispered to the Patrol-officer﻿—

“In the Gradina Dracului.”35

The priest and the magistrate immediately crossed themselves thrice, and the latter gazed devoutly on a mural St. Peter, as if to invoke his help on this occasion.

“You seem to me a plucky lad, to venture to approach the Devil’s Garden,” said the Patrol-officer. “Will you guide me thither?”

The Wallach nodded, with a joyful look.

“In the name of St. Michael and all the Archangels I implore you, sir, not to go,” interrupted the priest. “Of all who have visited the Devil’s Garden, not one has ever been known to come back. A truly devout person would turn his back upon it. It is only this man’s sinfulness that has led him thither.”

Clement scratched his head.

“I don’t go there for the pleasure of the thing,” said he. “Not that I fear the name of the place, but because I object to scaling mountains. In my official capacity, however, I have no choice.”

“Then at least stick a consecrated willow-twig in your cap,” urged the anxious pastor, “or take with you a picture of St. Michael, that the devil may not come near you.”

===Chinese===

桑格·莫阿尔特依旧一言不发，眼睛始终盯着弗洛丽扎。他和死人一样纹丝不动，几乎看不出呼吸。他善良的老母亲温柔地拉起他的手，叫着他的本名。

“约瓦（Jova），我的儿！答应这位老爷。看着我，我是你娘。”

“奉我家主人亲王之名，我命令你回答！”巡官提高了嗓子喊道。

那瓦拉几亚人还是不作声。

“我问你：你在林子里转悠的时候，可见过什么外来的野兽，就是说，黄色的、带花斑的怪物——读书人管它叫豹子的？”

桑格·莫阿尔特猛地一惊，仿佛从沉睡中忽然被人唤醒。他望着问话人，呆滞的眼睛骤然放出光来，狂热的猩红涌上双颊，他哆嗦着结结巴巴地说——

“我见过，见过，见过。”

说完他捂住眼睛，不忍再看那遗体。

“在哪儿见的？”巡官问。

“远，远得很，”瓦拉几亚人低声说；随后又哑了，把额头埋进手里。

“说出地名。在哪儿？”

瓦拉几亚人怯怯地四下看了看。一阵寒战穿过他全身，他眼珠乱转，恐惧万分地对着巡官耳语——

“在格拉迪纳·德拉库卢伊（Gradina Dracului）（注35）。”

神父和村长立刻画了三次十字；村长还虔诚地望着壁上的圣彼得像，仿佛此刻要请他搭救。

“你小子胆子不小，敢闯魔鬼花园，”巡官说，“你肯给我带路吗？”

瓦拉几亚人点点头，一脸欢喜。

“凭圣米迦勒和众天使之名，我求您别去，”神父插嘴道，“凡是进过魔鬼花园的，从没听说有一个回来。真正虔敬的人早掉头走了。是这人身上的罪孽才引他去了那地方。”

克莱门特挠了挠头。

“我去那儿又不是图乐子，”他说，“倒不是我怕那地方的名头，实在是不爱爬山。不过公事在身，由不得我。”

“那至少插一枝祝圣过的柳条在帽子上，”焦急的神父劝道，“或者带上一张圣米迦勒的像，魔鬼就不敢近你的身。”

===Original===

“Thank you, my brothers; but it would be much more to the point if you provided me with a pair of sandals, for I cannot go clambering over the mountains in these spurred boots. I regret too that your amulets are thrown away upon me, for I am a Unitarian.”

The priest crossed himself once more, and said with a sigh﻿—

“I fancied you were orthodox, because you were so zealous about the hags and witches.”

“I only did that officially. Send my Turk hither.”

As he went out the priest murmured to himself﻿—

“Birds of a feather! A nice pair of heretics!”

“Comrade Zülfikar,” cried Clement to the Turk, as he tied on his sandals, “you can find the rest of your way by yourself, for I must take a side spring into the mountains.”

“If you spring, I will spring too,” replied the distrustful renegade. “Whithersoever you go, thither will I go also.”

“My dear fellow, there is nothing to be pocketed on the road that I am about to take, except perhaps the devil, for man has never set his foot there.”

“What do I care! My orders are to go along with you till I return to the point from whence I started.”

“So much the better, then; I shall have the pleasure of your company. But pray help me to draw my sword, so that I may be able to defend myself in case of need.”

“So you carry a sword which requires two men to draw it! Well, let’s look at it,” and with that the two men planted their legs one against the other, grasped the sword with both hands, and tugged away at it for a long time, till at last it flew out of its sheath so suddenly that Clement the Clerk nearly fell sprawling.

Clement then called for a jar of honey, rubbed the rusty blade all over with the viscid stuff, and stuck it back into its sheath.

“And now let us be off, young man,” said he to the Wallach, who hastily took his cap and a small axe from the ground, and went out without once looking behind him.

His mother seized him by the hand﻿—

“Wilt thou not first kiss thy dead sweetheart?”

Sange Moarte did not even turn his head round, but drew his hand out of his mother’s and went with the two strange men towards the darkening woods.

All that night the adventurers were traversing a deep dell. Gigantic perpendicular rocks rose up on each side of them, only above their heads shimmered a narrow streak of starry sky.

Towards morning they found themselves among the Carpathian Alps.

===Chinese===

“多谢了，弟兄们；不过你们要是能给我弄双凉鞋，那才真是雪中送炭——我总不能穿着这双带马刺的靴子去爬山。还可惜你们的护身符在我身上要白费，因为我是一位论派。”

神父又画了个十字，叹道——

“我原以为你是正教徒呢，你对妖婆巫婆那么上心。”

“那是官样文章。叫我的土耳其人来。”

神父出去时自言自语地咕哝——

“一丘之貉！好一对异端！”

“祖尔菲卡尔伙计，”克莱门特一面绑凉鞋一面对土耳其人喊道，“剩下的路你自己认得，我得往山里岔一趟。”

“你岔我也岔，”这满腹疑心的叛教者答道，“你上哪儿，我上哪儿。”

“亲爱的伙计，我要走的这条路上，除了魔鬼，什么也捞不着装进口袋——人还从来没踏过那块地方呢。”

“管他呢！我的命令就是跟着你，一直跟到我出发的地方。”

“那更好；有劳你做伴了。不过请帮我拔拔剑，万一有事我也好自卫。”

“原来你佩的剑得两个人才拔得动！行，来吧。”于是两人腿抵着腿站定，双手攥住剑柄，拔了老半天，剑终于“铮”地一声脱鞘而出，书记官克莱门特差点摔个仰面朝天。

克莱门特随后要来一罐蜂蜜，把锈刃从头到尾抹了个遍，重新插回鞘里。

“那就走吧，年轻人。”他对瓦拉几亚人说。后者急忙从地上拿起帽子和一把小斧头，头也不回地出去了。

他母亲一把拉住他的手——

“你不先亲亲你死去的情人么？”

桑格·莫阿尔特连头也没回，把手从母亲手里抽出来，跟着两个生人朝渐渐暗下去的树林走去。

整整一夜，这几个冒险人在一道深谷里穿行。两边是巨大的直立岩壁，只有头顶上闪着一线星空。

近拂晓时，他们已身处喀尔巴阡高山之中。

===Original===

It was a dazzling spectacle. In the distance diamond-peaked crystal mountains covered with white snowfields, striped here and there by dark-green lines of pine forest. Close beside them is a basalt rock, consisting of angular columns as large as towers, standing side by side like the pipes of a gigantic organ, with their summits crowned by wreaths of round trees.
