“As for Sobieski,” continued Banfi, “depend upon it he will not attack his present ally the Emperor for our sweet sakes; nor will the Sultan break his oath as lightly as Master Michael Teleki seems to imagine. What then remains for us to do? Call the nomadic Tartars into Hungary, I suppose! The poor Hungarian population would certainly express their gratitude for such assistance as that! Your ideal Hungarian, Nicolas Zrinyi, used to tell a tale which deserves to be handed down to our latest posterity. The devil was carrying a Szekler away on his back. The Szekler’s neighbour met and thus accosted him: ‘Whither away, gossip?’ ‘I am being carried to hell,’ said he. ‘Eh! but that is a very bad job,’ returned the other. ‘Yes, but it might be much worse,’ replied the rogue. ‘Just fancy if he were to sit on my back, dig his spurs into me, and compel me to carry him instead!’﻿—Let everyone apply this fable as he thinks best. For my part, I cannot quite decide which I fear the most, the enmity of the Emperor or the amity of the Sultan. For, tell me, what will be the end of this war? If we conquer with the aid of the Sultan, Transylvania will become a Turkish Pachalic; if we are conquered, we shall sink into an Austrian province, while now we are a free and independent State by the grace of God! In any case Hungary’s fate is bound to improve, and that fate touches my heart quite as much as theirs who fancy they can heal the sick man with the sword. But nothing is to be won in that way. How much blood has not already been shed without the slightest result? Let us try some other way. Surely the Magyar has sense enough to subdue by his intellectual superiority those whom he cannot overcome by force of arms? Subdue your conquerors, I say. You who are second to none in sense, energy, wealth, and the beauty of manliness, why do you not take the highest posts which belong to you of right? If you were to sit where the Pázmáns49 and the Esterhazys50 have sat, there would be no room left for a Lobkovich.51 If instead of fighting petty, fruitless battles now and then, you were to use your intellects and your influence, you might make your land happy without costing her a drop of blood.

===Chinese===

“至于索别斯基，”班菲接着说，“放心吧，他绝不会为了疼我们就去攻打他如今的盟友皇帝；苏丹也不会像米哈伊·泰莱基老爷想的那样，轻飘飘地毁弃誓约。那么我们还剩什么可做？我想是请游牧的鞑靼人进匈牙利吧！可怜的匈牙利百姓对这样的‘援助’，定会感恩戴德！你们那位理想中的匈牙利人尼古拉·兹林尼，生前爱讲一个故事，值得一直传给最末的子孙。魔鬼驮着一个塞克勒人赶路。塞克勒人的邻人迎面撞见，招呼道：‘上哪儿去呀，老哥？’‘我正被驮去地狱呢。’他说。‘诶！那可遭透了。’那位答道。‘是啊，可兴许还糟得多呢。’这滑头回道，‘你想呀，要是他骑上我的背，拿马刺扎我，逼我驮着他走——那才叫糟呢！’——这则寓言，各人爱怎么用就怎么用。至于我，实在断不定更怕哪个：是皇帝的敌意，还是苏丹的友谊。因为告诉我，这场战争到头来是什么下场？我们若靠苏丹打赢了，特兰西瓦尼亚就成一个土耳其帕夏领地；我们若打输了，就沦成奥地利的一个行省——而如今，靠上帝的恩典，我们是一个自由独立的国家！无论哪样，匈牙利的命运都会好转；那份命运牵动我的心，丝毫不亚于那些自以为能用剑给病人治病的人。可那样什么也赢不来。已经流了多少血，连一点成效都没有？换条路试试吧。马扎尔人凭武力降伏不了的人，难道凭智力还降伏不了吗？我说，去降伏你们的征服者。你们在心智、精力、财富和男儿之美上样样不输于人，为什么不占据那本属你们名下的最高位置？你们若坐上帕兹马尼（Pázmán）49们和埃斯特哈齐（Esterhazy）50们坐过的位置，就再没有洛布科维奇（Lobkovich）51们的空子了。与其隔三差五打些小而无功的仗，不如动用你们的头脑和影响，那才不必让国土流一滴血，就能使她幸福。

===Original===

It rests with you to restore once more the age of Louis the Great,52 that foreign prince who became enamoured of his adopted people, turned Magyar, and made the nation as great and as powerful as the nation made him. The Estates of Transylvania will undertake to mediate between Hungary and the Emperor, and so get you back your privileges and your possessions. I will be the first to stretch out a helping hand, and assuredly Master Michael Teleki will be the second. If, however, you do not accept this offer, then, I say, beware of what you do. As to the prophecy﻿—Our turn today, yours tomorrow! I’ll only say, Fear nothing for Transylvania. I’ll be bold to say, that whoever invades her by force of arms, will always find a host of equal strength ready to meet him; but let me tell you, that that same host will never be so foolhardy as to invade a foreign land.”

“Then Hungary is to you a foreign land?” cried a mocking voice from the crowd.

This interruption was too much for Banfi’s composure. He turned furiously towards the quarter whence the question came, and meeting the cold, contemptuous looks of the Hungarians assembled there, he quite forgot himself; everything around him seemed to be in a whirl, and dashing his kalpag to the ground, he cried﻿—

“Right, right﻿—indeed! A foreign land﻿—nay more, a stepmother you have always been to us. We have always had to suffer for your sins. We have won victories, and you have frittered away the fruits of our victories. Your discords have thrice brought Hungary low, and thrice have we raised her from the dust. We have given you heroes; you have given us traitors!”

These last words Banfi was obliged to roar out at the top of his voice to make himself heard above the ever-increasing din. The uproar was general. Everyone tried to shout down his neighbour. The Hungarian gentlemen sprang from their seats and reviled Banfi. The graver members of the peace party shook their heads when they saw how Banfi’s indiscretion had let loose the passions of the Assembly.

Beldi now arose. All lovers of order cried at once﻿—“Let us hear Beldi!”

Then a young man suddenly leaped over the barrier, and placing his hand on Teleki’s armchair, planted himself in front of Banfi with a flushed and defiant face. It was Emerich Tököli.

===Chinese===

重振路易大王（Louis the Great）52时代的伟业，全在你们自己——那位外邦君王爱上了他归化的子民，做了马扎尔人，民族使他多伟大，他便使民族多强大。特兰西瓦尼亚的各等级愿出面调停匈牙利与皇帝，替你们把特权和产业都要回来。我愿第一个伸出手来，米哈伊·泰莱基老爷必然是第二个。你们若不受这份好意，那我说，就小心你们的作为。至于那句预言——今天轮到我们，明天轮到你们！——我只回一句：特兰西瓦尼亚什么也不用怕。我斗胆放言：无论谁兴兵来犯，总会碰上一支旗鼓相当的大军迎他；可我也告诉你们，那支大军永远不至于糊涂到去侵犯他人的国土。”

“这么说，匈牙利在你们眼里竟是外国了？”人群里一个嘲弄的声音喊道。

这一插话终于搅破了班菲的镇定。他狂怒地转向发问的角落，迎面撞上聚在那里的匈牙利人冷冷轻蔑的目光，登时把持不住；四周的一切仿佛都在打旋，他把卡尔帕格（kalpag）帽狠狠掼在地上，吼道——

“对，对——一点不错！外国——不，更糟，你们对我们向来是个后娘。你们的罪孽，从来都由我们来受。我们打了胜仗，你们把我们胜利的果实挥霍精光。你们的内讧三回把匈牙利掀翻在地，三回都是我们把她从尘土里扶起。我们给了你们英雄；你们给了我们叛徒！”

最后这几句，班菲只能扯开最大的嗓门吼出来，好压过那越来越响的喧嚣。全场哗然。人人都想吼过自己的邻座。匈牙利老爷们从座位上跳起来，破口大骂班菲。主和派里持重的成员们直摇头——班菲这一失言，把整个议会的激情全放了出来。

这时贝尔迪站了起来。所有爱秩序的人齐声喊——“让贝尔迪说！”

就在这时，一个年轻人忽然越过栏杆，一只手按住泰莱基的扶手椅，涨红着脸、一脸挑衅地立在班菲面前。正是埃梅里希·托克利（Emerich Tököli）。

===Original===

“I too have got a word to say,” cried he, in a voice audible above the tumult. “I also have the right to say a word or two within this barrier. If you will deny your mother, Hungary, and draw boundaries between her and you, it is time for me to speak. I am just as good a territorial noble here in Transylvania as that proud and petty demigod, whose father before him was just such another reviler of his mother country!”

Beldi was making his way towards Tököli to stop him from speaking, when someone from behind seized his hand, and turning round, he was astonished to see his own son-in-law, Paul Wesselenyi, who begged him to step outside for a moment.

Beldi retired into the lobby, while Tököli’s voice thundered through the hall above the never-ending din.

A veiled lady awaited Beldi in the lobby, whom, when she had unveiled her face, he had some difficulty in recognizing as his daughter Sophia, so much had grief and care changed and broken her. Her beautiful eyes were red with weeping.

“We are homeless fugitives,” sobbed Sophia, sinking on her father’s breast. “They have taken from us our Hungarian possessions; my husband has been driven from his castle, and a price set on his head.”

Beldi became very serious. This unexpected ill-tidings pricked him to the heart. Within, Tököli’s thundering voice was raising a perfect tempest of indignation, but Beldi no longer made haste back to quell it.

“Remain with me,” said he, with a troubled countenance; “here you can dwell in peace till things improve.”

“Too late!” said Wesselenyi. “I have already enlisted under the flag of the French General, Count Boham, as a common soldier.”

“You a common soldier! You, the descendant of the Palatine Wesselenyi! And what in the meantime is to become of my daughter?”

“She will remain behind with you﻿—till Hungary has been won back again!” and with these words he placed his wife in Beldi’s arms, kissed her on the forehead, and departed with dry eyes.

===Chinese===

“我也有一句话要说。”他喊道，那嗓门压过了鼎沸的人声。“在这道栏杆里面，我同样有权说一两句。你们若要否认你们的母亲匈牙利，要在她同你们之间划界，那就轮到我说话了。在特兰西瓦尼亚这里，我是同那位骄傲的小半神一样货真价实的领地贵族——他那位先父，当年也就是这样一位咒骂自己母邦的人！”

贝尔迪正朝托克利挤过去要拦他发言，冷不防有人从背后抓住了他的手；回头一看，竟是自己的女婿保罗·韦谢莱尼（Paul Wesselenyi），求他到外面说话。

贝尔迪退进前厅，托克利的声音则在上头没完没了的喧嚣中，如雷般滚过大厅。

前厅里候着一位蒙纱的夫人；待她揭开面纱，他费了好大劲才认出是自己的女儿索菲娅——忧伤和操劳把她折磨得变了样、损了形。她那双美丽的眼睛哭得通红。

“我们成了无家可归的逃亡者。”索菲娅扑在父亲胸前呜咽道，“他们夺走了我们在匈牙利的产业；我丈夫被赶出了城堡，头上还挂了赏格。”

贝尔迪的脸色严峻起来。这意外的噩耗直扎进他的心。厅内，托克利雷鸣般的声音正掀动一场义愤的风暴；可贝尔迪不再急着回去平息它了。

“留下跟我住吧。”他神色不安地说，“在这里你们能安安稳稳住到时局好转。”

“太迟了！”韦谢莱尼说，“我已经投到法兰西将军博哈姆（Boham）伯爵的旗下，当了名普通士兵。”

“你当普通士兵！你，帕拉丁韦谢莱尼的后人！那这期间我的女儿怎么办？”

“她留下来陪你——直到匈牙利被夺回来！”说着，他把妻子放进贝尔迪的怀抱，吻了吻她的额头，眼睛干干地走了。

===Original===

Within raged the tumult. Beldi heard his daughter sobbing, and a bitter feeling began to fill his breast, a feeling not unlike a nascent desire of vengeance. He felt almost pleased that war was being demanded within there; and he, the leader of the peace party, was also just about to draw his sword, rush into the Diet, and exclaim﻿—“War! war! and retribution!” when the pages led into the lobby an old man as pale as death, who, recognizing Beldi, staggered up to him and addressed him in a trembling voice﻿—

“My lord, are you not the Captain-General of the Szeklers, Paul Beldi of Uzoni?”

“Yes. What do you want with me?”

“I am the last inhabitant of Benfalva!” stammered the dying man. “War, famine, and pestilence have carried off all the others. I alone remain, and feeling that I too am on the point of death, I have brought you the official seal of the place and the church bell. Give them to the Diet. Preserve them in the archives, and write over them﻿—‘These are the bell and the seal of what was once Benfalva, the inhabitants of which utterly perished.’ ”

Beldi’s nerveless arm dropped the hilt of his sword, and he tore himself from his daughter’s embrace.

“Go to your mother at Bodola, and learn to bear your fate with a stout heart!”

Then he took the seal and the bell from the dying man, and hastened back to the hall of the Diet, where Tököli had just finished his speech, which had produced a terrible effect on the Assembly. The French ministers were shaking hands with him.

Beldi stepped up to the president’s table, and placed upon it the seal which had just been handed to him.

Everyone looked at him, and seeing that he was about to speak, became silent.

“Look!” cried he, with a voice broken by emotion. “A desolated town sends its official seal to the Diet by its last inhabitant. There are already enough of such towns in Transylvania, and in time there may be more. War and famine have wasted the fairest portions of our land. You should not forget, gentlemen, to place this seal among your other﻿—trophies!”

At these last words Beldi’s voice sank almost to a whisper, yet so deep was the silence, that he was heard distinctly in every part of the hall. A thrill of horror passed through everyone present.

===Chinese===

厅内风暴正酣。贝尔迪听着女儿的呜咽，一股苦涩开始灌满胸膛——那滋味，同初萌的复仇欲已相去不远。他几乎要庆幸厅里正有人叫嚷开战；这位主和派的领袖，自己也险些拔剑冲进议会，大喊——“战争！战争！报仇！”——恰在这时，侍童们搀进前厅一个面如死灰的老人；老人认出贝尔迪，踉跄着挪到他跟前，声音发颤地说——

“大人，您可是塞克勒人的总兵、乌佐尼的保罗·贝尔迪？”

“正是。你有何贵干？”

“我是本法尔瓦（Benfalva）最后一个活人！”垂死的人结结巴巴地说，“战争、饥荒和瘟疫，把其余的人都带走了。只剩我一个；我觉得自己也快死了，就给您送来了本地的官印和教堂的钟。把它们交给议会。存进档案库，在上面写一行字——‘此钟此印，属曾经的本法尔瓦；其居民已全部死绝。’”

贝尔迪那再也使不上劲的手从剑柄上垂落下来，他从女儿怀里挣脱出身。

“去波多拉找你母亲，学着硬起心肠担起自己的命！”

随后他从垂死的人手里接过印和钟，急急赶回议会大厅；托克利刚好讲完——他的演说在议会上激起了可怕的效果。法国的公使们正同他握手。

贝尔迪走上议长席，把方才接到的官印放在桌上。

人人都望着他，见他要开口，便都静了下来。

“看哪！”他喊道，声音激动得发碎。“一座死绝的城，托它最后的居民，把官印送给了议会。这样的城，特兰西瓦尼亚已经够多了，往后兴许还会更多。战争和饥荒，已把我们国土最美好的部分蹂躏殆尽。诸位老爷，别忘了把这枚印，同你们其余的——战利品摆在一处！”

说到最后几个字，贝尔迪的声音低得近乎耳语；然而全场静得那样深，大厅每个角落都听得清清楚楚。一阵战栗掠过在场每一个人。

===Original===

“Outside that door I hear someone weeping,” continued Beldi, with quivering lips. “It is my own dear daughter, the wife of Paul Wesselenyi, who, driven from her fatherland, on her knees implored me, as I loved her, to let the *lex talionis* assert its rights. But I say, let my child weep, let her perish, may I also perish with my whole family if need be, but let not the curse of war fall on Transylvania! May no one in Transylvania have cause to weep because I suffer. No! I would declare against war though everyone here present were for it.﻿ ﻿… Gentlemen!﻿ ﻿… this seal﻿ ﻿… and the other relic too﻿ ﻿… forget not to preserve them among your trophies!”

Beldi sat down. Long after his words had ceased to sound, a deathlike silence continued to prevail.

Teleki, ascribing this silence to indignation against Beldi, very confidently arose, and bade the Estates give their votes. But for once he had wrongly felt the pulse of public opinion, for the majority of the Diet, deeply touched by the foregoing scene, voted for peace. So great was still the influence of Banfi and Beldi in the land.

Teleki looked with some confusion at his future son-in-law, who clenched his fists, and murmured bitterly with tears in his eyes﻿—

“*Flectere si nequeo Superos, Acheronta movebo!*”

---

As the Assembly broke up, Forval and Nicholas Bethlen again met together.

“So our hope that Transylvania will take up arms has been dashed,” observed the crestfallen Frenchman.

“On the contrary, our hope only now begins,” returned Bethlen, tapping his friend on the shoulder. “Did you hear that young man Tököli speak?”

“Yes; he spoke very prettily.”

“Prettily or not, it strikes me that he is just the man you seek.”

“A King of Hungary, eh?”53

“Either that or an outlaw. Fate will decide.”

===Chinese===

“门外，我听得见有人在哭。”贝尔迪嘴唇颤抖着继续说，“那是我亲爱的女儿，保罗·韦谢莱尼的妻子；她被赶出了祖国，跪着求我——若我还疼她——就让*同态复仇法*（lex talionis）讨还它的公道。可我说：让我的孩子哭吧，让她毁灭吧，必要时我拼上一门老小同归于尽，也决不让战争的诅咒落到特兰西瓦尼亚头上！愿特兰西瓦尼亚没有一个人因为我受苦而流泪。不！哪怕今天在场的人个个主战，我也断然主和。……诸位老爷！……这枚印……还有那件遗物……别忘了把它们保存在你们的战利品中间！”

贝尔迪坐下了。话音消歇之后许久，死一般的寂静依然笼罩全场。

泰莱基把这寂静归为对贝尔迪的义愤，满有把握地站起身，命各等级投票。可这一回他把民情的脉摸错了：议会的大多数被方才那一幕深深打动，投了主和。班菲和贝尔迪在国中的影响力，仍旧有这般大。

泰莱基有些发窘地望向他未来的女婿；后者攥紧双拳，含着眼泪痛苦地低吟——

“*Flectere si nequeo Superos, Acheronta movebo!*（感天不动，我便撼动冥河！）”

---

散会时，福尔瓦尔和尼古拉·贝特伦又聚到了一处。

“这么说，我们指望特兰西瓦尼亚揭竿而起的美梦破了。”垂头丧气的法国人说。

“恰恰相反，我们的指望现在才开始呢。”贝特伦拍着朋友的肩膀答道，“你听见那位托克利少年的演说了吗？”

“听见了；讲得很漂亮。”

“漂亮不漂亮且不论，我看，他正是你们要找的那个人。”

“一位匈牙利国王，嗯？53”

“不是国王，就是绿林。命运自会裁夺。”

## VII / 七
===Original===

The *Jus Ligatum*54

’Tis a good old custom which requires that every ceremony should end with a feast, and so the boisterous Diet was succeeded by a still more boisterous banquet, whereat Michael Apafi also presided; and here he was in his proper place, for the chronicles tell us that a skin of wine at a sitting was a mere nothing to his Highness.

Wine inflames hate as well as love. When ladies are at table, we must look to our hearts; but when only men sit down together, our heads are often in danger.

After dinner, according to Transylvanian custom, the guests stood up to drink. Conversation flows more easily thus, and the Prince, going the round of his guests, presented to them an overflowing beaker with his own hand, challenging them one by one to drain it﻿—“Come, a toast﻿—my health, the welfare of the realm, and whatever else you like!”

The gentlemen were in high good-humour, and kept falling out with each other and making it up again from sheer lightness of heart. Only one man was quite sober﻿—Michael Teleki, who never drank at all.

Beware of the man who keeps sober while everyone else is in his cups.

Teleki went about among the wrangling roysterers, and lingered for a long time round Banfi’s chair. When the magnate caught sight of him, creeping about like a cat, he turned sharply round upon him.

“Why, how sad you look!” he cried, with a mocking laugh; “just like a man whose coveted palatinate falls into the dust before his eyes.”

That was all Teleki wanted.

With a smile, beneath which there lurked a deadly sting, he replied﻿—

“That is no merit of yours. If Paul Beldi had not been present, you would have been left all alone with your vote. But I must confess that we all bow before such a distinguished man as Paul Beldi. The whole nation cries Amen! to whatever he says.”

Teleki then bowed low, with a semblance of deep respect, well aware that he had sent a venomous shaft into the proud magnate’s heart, for nothing wounded Banfi so much as to see someone honoured above himself, especially someone who really deserved it.

Teleki next turned to Beldi, drew him into a window-niche, and thus began in his suavest manner﻿—

===Chinese===

密盟之法（Jus Ligatum）（注54）

好古老的好规矩：凡有典礼，必以盛宴收场。于是喧腾的议会之后，又接了一场更喧腾的宴会，由米哈伊·阿帕菲（Michael Apafi）亲自做东；在这儿他可算得其所哉——史册告诉我们，殿下一气喝下一皮囊酒，算不得一回事。

酒既旺爱火，也旺恨火。席上有女士，须当心我们的心；清一色男人同席，则往往要当心脑袋。

按特兰西瓦尼亚的规矩，正餐过后宾客们要站着饮酒。这样谈话更畅快；亲王在宾客间绕行，亲手把斟得满满的大杯递到各人面前，一个个地叫阵，要他们一饮而尽——“来，干一杯——祝我龙体康健，祝国运昌隆，再添上你们爱添的一切！”

绅士们兴致极高，一会儿互相拌嘴，一会儿又言归于好，全凭一腔轻狂快活。只有一个人滴酒未沾——米哈伊·泰莱基（Michael Teleki），他向来根本不喝酒。

人人都在酒里泡着，偏偏他一个清醒——这种人，须防。

泰莱基在吵吵嚷嚷的酒客中间转来转去，在班菲的座位四近逗留了许久。大贵族（magnate）瞥见他猫一般蹑手蹑脚地绕来绕去，猛地朝他转过身来。

“哟，瞧您这愁眉苦脸！”他讥笑地嚷道，“活像一个眼看觊觎的帕拉丁高位当着自己的面掼进尘土里的人。”

泰莱基等的正是这一句。

他面带微笑答道——笑底下藏着一根致命的毒刺——

“这可算不得您的功劳。倘若保罗·贝尔迪（Paul Beldi）不在场，您就剩自己孤零零一票了。不过我得承认，面对保罗·贝尔迪这样德高望重的人物，我们大家全都俯首帖耳。他说什么，全国都跟着喊‘阿门’！”

泰莱基随即深深一躬，装出万分敬重的样子；他心里明白，一支毒箭已经射进了这位傲慢大贵族的心窝——再没有比看见别人受尊崇压过自己更刺痛班菲的了，尤其那人是真配受尊崇的。

接着泰莱基转向贝尔迪，把他拉到一处窗龛里，用他最温文尔雅的口吻开了腔——

===Original===

“I had always held your Excellency for a very magnanimous man, but today I learnt to recognize you as doubly such, though it was to my own detriment. The Diet only knows that in voting for peace you sacrificed your fatherly affection; but *I* know that at the same time you sacrificed your hatred of Banfi.”

“I?﻿—I have never hated Banfi.”

“I know why you conceal your hatred. You fancy that no one knows your secret reasons for it. My friend, we men know well that a sword-thrust may be forgiven, but a *kiss* never.”

Beldi started. He knew not what reply to make to this man, who, after planting the sting of jealousy in his heart, quitted him with a smiling countenance, leaving the wound to rankle.

At that moment Banfi appeared behind Beldi’s back with his haughtiest air. He was burning to make Beldi feel his haughtiness, and was thinking how he could best pick a quarrel with him.

Beldi at first did not perceive him, and when the Prince, chancing to stray into that part of the room, holding a costly pocal set with turquoises, which he affably extended, saying familiarly﻿—“Drink, my cousin!” Beldi, fancying that the invitation was meant for him, and never suspecting that anyone was behind him, took the cup out of the Prince’s hand, and drained it to his Highness’s health, at the very moment when Banfi also held out his hand towards it.

Banfi, purple with rage, turned furiously upon Beldi, and said in his most insulting tone﻿—

“Not so fast, Szekler. You might, I think, have a little more respect for the Marshal of the Diet, and not snatch away the cup from beneath my very nose. Let me tell you, sir, that if you persist in such courses, you and I shall fall out!”

Beldi was anything but a quarrelsome man. Had he been in another frame of mind, he would simply have apologized for his mistake. But now he too was in a pugnacious mood, so, calmly measuring Banfi from head to foot, he replied with suppressed rage﻿—

“Yes, Denis, I am a Szekler, as you say, and a tough one too; and if it came to a bout between us, and I fell uppermost, I’d give you such a squeeze that you’d never raise your head again in this world.”

===Chinese===

“我一向把大人您看作胸襟极阔的人；今天我更认出您阔上加阔——尽管这于我不利。议会只知道，您投和平票时牺牲了慈父之情；可*我*还知道，您同时也牺牲了对班菲的恨。”

“我？——我从没恨过班菲。”

“我知道您为什么把恨藏起来。您以为没人知道您心底的缘由。我的朋友，我们男人都懂：一剑刺来，尚可饶恕；一个*吻*，永不可赦。”

贝尔迪浑身一震。他不知该怎样回答这个人——此人在他心里种下嫉妒的毒刺，随即满面春风地扬长而去，任那伤口在原地溃烂。

就在这时，班菲摆出他最倨傲的架势，出现在贝尔迪背后。他正惈着要叫贝尔迪尝尝他的傲气，盘算着怎样寻衅最妙。

贝尔迪起先没察觉他。恰巧亲王信步走到大厅这一角，手里端着一只镶绿松石的贵重酒杯，和颜悦色地递上前去，亲热地说——“喝吧，我的表亲！”贝尔迪只当这一请是冲自己来的，做梦也没想到身后有人，便从亲王手里接过杯，为殿下健康一饮而尽——恰在此刻，班菲的手也朝那杯酒伸了过去。

班菲气得脸色发紫，狂怒地扑向贝尔迪，用最刻毒的腔调说——

“别这么急，塞克勒人（Szekler）。依我看，你对议会元帅该多一分敬意，不该从我鼻子底下把杯子抢走。告诉你，先生，你再这么干下去，你我可要翻脸！”

贝尔迪绝不是好斗的人。换个别的心境，他会为这误会道个歉了事。可此刻他也正惈着火，于是不动声色地把班菲从头到脚打量了一遍，压着怒气回答——

“不错，德尼斯，我是塞克勒人，你说对了，还是个硬茬儿的塞克勒人；真要同你较量一场，只要我压在你上头摔下去，我就叫你尝尝那么一抱——叫你这辈子再抬不起头来。”

===Original===

“Come, come! What’s all this nonsense about?” cried the Prince, intervening. “I’m surprised at you, gentlemen! *Inter pocula non sunt seria tractanda.*” And, with that, Apafi compelled the two magnates to shake hands with each other, and then passed on, thinking that the whole affair was a mere drunken brawl, and that he had put it right.

But it did not escape Teleki that, immediately after this scene, both the magnates quitted the room, and he learnt soon afterwards that they had suddenly left Fehervár, thus leaving the field clear for him.

Teleki and his satellites remained alone with the half-besotted Prince.

“Drink, gentlemen! drink! be merry!” cried Apafi. “Don’t drop off one by one! Who last went out there?”

“Beldi!” cried several voices.

“Ah, I understand! The poor fellow has not seen his wife for a long time. Let him go. And who else has gone?”

“Banfi!”

“What? Banfi too? What’s the meaning of that?”

“He has gone to lord it at home?” sneered Szekely, one of Teleki’s creatures.

“He can’t endure to be anywhere where there is a greater than he,” put in Nalaczi.

“I certainly shall not resign the princely diadem to please his Excellency!” cried Apafi.

“That is not necessary!” insinuated Teleki. “He knows how to rule in Transylvania without an *athname*. When he commands the country must obey, and what the country commands he contemptuously rejects.”

“I should like to see him do it!” murmured Apafi angrily.

“But is it not so? We want war, he doesn’t, and we must give way. We want peace, and he is immediately up and waging war against our allies on his own account. The throne is ours, the realm is his!”

“Don’t say that, Master Michael Teleki!”

“I appeal to you, Nalaczi! What answer did he give in the Zolyomi affair?”

“He said that if the country wished him to surrender the Gyulai property to Zolyomi, it must give him in exchange the domain of Szamos-Ujvar.”

“What!” cried the Prince, “the property which the Estates gave to me for my maintenance! My princely domains! The man must be mad!”

“So he said, adding that he would not surrender the property even if Zolyomi saddled us with the Turks in consequence.”

“Well, now we’ve had enough of him. Not a word more about it, gentlemen.”

===Chinese===

“行了，行了！这都是哪里的话？”亲王插进来打圆场，“二位真叫我吃惊！杯盏之间，不当谈正事。（Inter pocula non seria tractanda）”说着，阿帕菲强按着两位大贵族握了握手，便走开了，只当这不过是一场酒后的口角，已经被他摆平了。

可这一幕刚过，两位大贵族便双双离席，泰莱基全都看在眼里；他随后很快打听到，两人已突然离开了费海尔瓦尔（Fehervár）——场地，就此为他腾清了。

只剩泰莱基和他的党羽们，陪着半醉的亲王。

“喝呀，诸位！喝呀！快活些！”阿帕菲喊道，“别一个一个溜号！刚才是谁出去了？”

“贝尔迪！”好几条嗓子喊。

“啊，我懂了！这可怜的家伙好久没见老婆了。让他去吧。还有谁走了？”

“班菲！”

“什么？班菲也走了？这是怎么说？”

“他回家当他的土皇帝去了呗。”泰莱基的一名走卒塞凯伊（Szekely）冷笑道。

“哪儿有比他大的，他在哪儿就待不住。”纳拉齐插嘴道。

“为了讨好他大人，我断不会把亲王冠冕让出去！”阿帕菲喊道。

“那倒不必！”泰莱基话里有话地说，“他不用那顶王冠，也知道怎么统治特兰西瓦尼亚。他一声令下，全国必须服从；全国议决的事，他嗤之以鼻。”

“我倒要看看他怎么统治！”阿帕菲愤愤地咕哝。

“难道不是这样吗？我们要打仗，他不要，我们就得让步。我们要和平，他立刻自作主张，向我们的盟邦开战。宝座是我们的，国家却是他的！”

“别这么说，米哈伊·泰莱基先生！”

“纳拉齐，我请问你！佐约米（Zolyomi）那桩案子，他是怎么答复的？”

“他说，国家若要他把久洛（Gyulai）产业让给佐约米，就得拿索莫什-乌伊瓦尔（Szamos-Ujvar）的领地同他交换。”

“什么！”亲王喊道，“那是各等级拨给我作赡养费的产业！我的亲王领地！这人准是疯了！”

“他就是这么说来着，还添了一句：就算佐约米因此引来土耳其人缠住我们，他也不交。”

“好了，我们受够他了。这事再莫提了，诸位。”

===Original===

“The insult to the Turks your Highness might overlook,” persisted Teleki, “but we really cannot look through our fingers any longer at the way in which he treats the gentry. The latest victim of his tyranny is Lady Saint Pauli. The poor widow’s ancestral dwelling was an eyesore to the great lord, because it spoiled the prospect from his palace windows; so he had the house appraised at his own valuation, and turned the poor lady out of doors. The magistrate gave her a letter of indemnity, but my Lord-Marshal tore the letter to pieces, and pulled down the poor widow’s sole possession, her ancestral dwelling-place. The Diet, he said, might build it up again if it felt so disposed. Such an act, sir, in ordinary times has been known to cost the doer thereof his head!”

Apafi was silent, but his bloodshot eyes began to glow savagely.

“But that is not all,” continued Teleki; “outrages on individuals are of small account when the security of the whole realm is at stake. This great lord can speak very prettily about the blessings of peace, let us see now how he labours to uphold it. He takes the sword out of our hands and closes our mouths, while he himself collects an army and goads the Turk against us, well knowing that we have no money wherewith to buy the gifts necessary to counteract his vagaries. Now, three letters have reached us simultaneously﻿—one from the Pasha of Grosswardein, another from the Pasha of Buda, and a third from the Sultan himself﻿—demanding instant satisfaction, or an indemnity of three hundred purses of gold, for the defeat which the Pasha of Grosswardein has suffered at Banfi’s hands. As, however, we cannot expect Banfi to pay the indemnity, will it please your Highness to consider from whence such a large sum of money is to be procured?”

“From nowhither!” cried Apafi furiously, smashing his glass to pieces on the table. “I’ll show the world that I’m able to exact satisfaction from whomsoever I will, let him be even as mighty again as Denis Banfi.”

===Chinese===

“对土耳其人的冒犯，殿下或许可以宽宏大量，”泰莱基坚持道，“可他那样作践缙绅，我们实在无法再睁一只眼闭一只眼了。他暴虐的最新受害者，就是圣保利夫人（Lady Saint Pauli）。那可怜寡妇的祖宅碍了这位大老爷的眼——只因坏了他府上窗外的景致；于是他让人按自己开的价把宅子折了价，把可怜的夫人扫地出门。地方官给了她一纸赔偿凭据，我们的元帅大人却把凭据斯得粉碎，把可怜寡妇唯一的家当——祖传宅邸——拆了个平。他说，议会若是高兴，大可再给它盖起来。殿下，这等行径，在寻常年月，是掉脑袋的罪！”

阿帕菲不作声了，可他布满血丝的眼睛开始凶狠地发亮。

“这还不是全部，”泰莱基接着说，“整个国家的安危攸关之时，个人受些作践还算小事。这位大老爷谈起和平的恩惠来，口若悬河；且看他如今怎样维护和平吧。他缴了我们的剑，堵了我们的嘴，自己却在招兵买马，唆使土耳其人来打我们，而且他心里清楚，我们拿不出钱来买通门路，破解他的胡作非为。眼下，三封书信同时送到——一封来自大瓦尔德因的帕夏，一封来自布达（Buda）的帕夏，第三封来自苏丹（Sultan）本人——要求立刻给出交代，否则赔偿三百袋黄金，为的是大瓦尔德因帕夏在班菲手下吃的败仗。可是，赔偿既指望不上班菲掏，就请殿下思量思量：这么大一笔钱，从何处筹措？”

“哪儿也没处筹！”阿帕菲狂怒地喊，把杯子在桌上砸了个粉碎，“我要让世人看看，我想找谁讨交代，就找得成——哪怕他比德尼斯·班菲（Denis Banfi）还强横一倍！”

===Original===

“Then I wish your Highness would tell us how, for we know that Banfi will not appear to our summons, and we cannot compel him, for he has shown himself stronger than the whole realm. If we attempted to use force he would call out the banderia and the garrison troops, and then it might fare with us as it fared with Ladislaus Csaky﻿—he would arrest the officers sent to arrest him, and expose us to universal derision.”

“As our first counsellor, it is your province to give us good counsel in such cases,” cried Apafi wrathfully.

“I only know of one remedy capable of curing the realm thoroughly of this disease.”

“Then prescribe it. In what does your remedy consist?”

“In the *jus ligatum*.”

Apafi, despite his semi-besotted state, instinctively shrunk back from such an expedient, and throwing himself into his armchair, looked blankly at Teleki.

“Are you not ashamed of yourself,” he murmured in broken sentences, as tipsy people usually do, “to propose a secret conspiracy against a free nobleman? To privily conspire against him is contrary to the law of the land.”

“It is not my fault if the expedient is shameful,” returned Teleki calmly and steadfastly; “but it is shameful that the law should not possess sufficient power to bring a rebel to book, and that one of our own subjects should be able to openly defy justice and laugh at the decrees of the Prince. If in such a state of things the *jus ligatum* is our only means of defence, the shame falls not upon me but upon the Prince.”

Apafi rose angrily from his seat and paced to and fro. The lords remained perfectly silent.

At last the Prince stopped short in front of Teleki, and, leaning on the back of his armchair, asked him﻿—

“And how then do you propose to bring about this league?”

Nalaczi and Szekely exchanged a smile. It was plain that the idea had caught the Prince’s fancy. Teleki beckoned to Szekely to fetch him writing materials and a strip of parchment.

“We will quickly draw up the necessary articles of impeachment; your Highness will subscribe them, and we’ll secretly persuade the great men of the land to consent to Banfi’s arrest and join the league before any legal steps have been taken.”

At these words many of the gentlemen present began to bite their moustaches and move uneasily in their chairs.

Teleki observed the movement, and added emphatically﻿—

===Chinese===

“那就请殿下指教我们怎么办。因为我们清楚，班菲不会应召而来；我们也强迫不了他——他已经摆明了自己比整个国家还硬。我们若动武，他就会调出旗军和守备兵，那时我们的下场，只怕同拉斯洛·恰基（Ladislaus Csaky）一样——他会把奉命去抓他的人反倒抓起来，叫我们在天下人面前出尽洋相。”

“你是我们的首席枢臣，这种时候拿出良策来，正是你的职分。”阿帕菲怒气冲冲地喊。

“我只知道一剂药，能除掉国家这块病根。”

“那就开出来。你这剂药是什么方子？”

“密盟之法（jus ligatum）。”

阿帕菲虽已半醉，对这等招数还是本能地打了个寒噤；他一屁股跌进扶手椅，直愣愣地望着泰莱基。

“你不害臊吗，”他像醉汉惯常的那样，断断续续地咕哝，“竟提出对一个自由贵族搞秘密阴谋？私底下密谋算计他，是违背国法的。”

“这法子丢人，可不是我的过错，”泰莱基从容而坚定地答道，“丢人的是：法律竟没有足够的力量惩一个逆臣，我们自己的臣子竟能公然蔑视法度、讥笑亲王的敕令。事已至此，倘若密盟之法是我们唯一的自卫手段，那么耻辱不该落在我头上，而该落在亲王头上。”

阿帕菲愤然起身，来回踱步。众贵族鸦雀无声。

最后，亲王在泰莱基面前猛地站住，扶着他扶手椅的椅背，问道——

“那么，这个盟，你打算怎么结？”

纳拉齐和塞凯伊相视一笑。显然，这个主意把亲王勾住了。泰莱基招手叫塞凯伊取来笔墨和一条羊皮纸。

“我们即刻草拟必要的弹劾条款；殿下签署，再赶在任何法律程序启动之前，秘密说动国中巨头们首肯拘捕班菲，加入此盟。”

这话一出，在场的许多绅士开始咬起髭须，在椅子里坐立不安。

泰莱基看在眼里，加重语气补了一句——

===Original===

“I perceive that no one here has the courage to put down his name first on the list. Nevertheless I have already found a man, who in dignity and power is every whit Banfi’s equal, and when once he has subscribed the list, the other signatures will follow as a matter of course.”

“And who may that be?” asked Apafi.

“Paul Beldi!”

The Prince shook his head.

“He won’t do it. He is much too honourable a man for that.”

Wine-inspired as this sentence was, it completely ruffled Teleki’s equanimity. Turning vehemently upon the Prince he cried﻿—

“Then you mean to imply that *we* are acting dishonourably?”

“I meant to say that Beldi is never very willing to pick a quarrel with anybody. He is a peace-abiding man.”

“But I know his sore point, and if you but touch it with the tip of your finger, he’ll answer with his clenched fist, and the lamb will become a lion. I’ll get him to﻿—”

At that moment the door opened, and, to everyone’s astonishment, the Princess entered the room.

Nevertheless, her appearance at this time was no freak of chance. You could see by her agitation that she was well aware of what was going on. The lords were confused, and Apafi, despite his tipsy wrath, became so frightened when he beheld the pale face of his consort that he whispered to Teleki﻿—

“For heaven’s sake put that document out of sight.”

Only Teleki kept his countenance, and instead of hiding the parchment, ostentatiously spread it out before him.

“What are you doing?” asked the Princess. She was very pale, and her bosom heaved tempestuously.

“We are holding a council,” replied Teleki grimly.

“A council?” repeated Anna, approaching nearer and nearer to the table.

“Yes; and we venture to ask your Highness by what right you intrude here, while we are deliberating over the most momentous affairs of state?” continued Teleki in a hard, dry tone.

“Deliberating over the most momentous affairs of state, eh?” repeated the lady, measuring Teleki with a searching look. Then with a loud, vibrating voice she exclaimed﻿—“What mean these wine-cups then? You are holding a council of state when the head of the state is drunk, that you may sow discord and confusion.”

Teleki sprang from his seat and turned towards the Prince﻿—

“May it please your Highness to dismiss us. We perceive that a domestic scene is about to begin.”

===Chinese===

“我看出来了，在座没有一位敢头一个在单子上签名。不过我已经找着一个人——论尊贵论权势，处处不输班菲；只要他签下第一笔，旁的签名自然接踵而来。”

“那会是哪一位？”阿帕菲问。

“保罗·贝尔迪！”

亲王直摇头。

“他不会干的。要论人品端方，他可太端方了，干不出这个。”

这句话虽是酒话，却把泰莱基的镇定搅了个稀碎。他猛地转向亲王，喊道——

“这么说，您的意思是*我们*干得不光彩了？”

“我是说，贝尔迪从不轻易同人结怨。他是个安分守己的人。”

“可我知道他的痛处；只要拿指尖轻轻一碰，他就会抡起拳头作答——绵羊就变成了狮子。我会让他——”

就在这时，门开了，王妃走进屋来——众人无不愕然。

然而她此刻现身，绝非偶然的心血来潮。看她的激动神情便知，这里在干什么，她一清二楚。贵族们慌了神；阿帕菲尽管带着酒怒，一见王后半白的脸，也吓得朝泰莱基耳语——

“看在老天爷份上，把那文书藏起来。”

唯独泰莱基面不改色；他非但不藏羊皮纸，反而大张旗鼓地把它摊在自己面前。

“你们在干什么？”王妃问。她脸色惨白，胸口剧烈起伏。

“我们在开枢机会议。”泰莱基阴沉沉地答。

“枢机会议？”安娜（Anna）重复着，一步一步逼向桌前。

“正是；我们还斗胆请问殿下：我们正在商讨国家最重大的要务，您凭什么闯进来？”泰莱基接着说，语调又硬又冷。

“商讨国家最重大的要务，嗯？”夫人重复道，用审视的目光把泰莱基从头到脚量了一遍。随即她扬起洪亮震颤的声音喝道——“那这些酒杯作何解释？国之元首酩酊大醉，你们却开什么国政会议——好播弄是非、搅乱朝纲！”

泰莱基霍然起身，转向亲王——

“恭请殿下准臣等退下。臣等看得出，一场家事风波要开场了。”

===Original===

“Anna!” cried Apafi, scarlet with shame and wine, “leave the room this instant. We command it﻿—and for a week to come do not presume to appear in our presence.”

“Be it so, Apafi. I have nothing more to say to you, for you are not yourself; but to you, Mr. Chief-Counsellor, to you who are always sober, I have a word to say. I raised you from the dust; I helped you into the place where now you stand; you requite me by thrusting yourself between me and the Prince’s heart, for I find you in my way every time I approach my husband. You have taken the sceptre out of the Prince’s hand, and have substituted for it the headsman’s sword; but let me tell you that if I cannot reach the Prince’s heart, I can, at least, step in the way of the sword, and as often as it descends, you will find me between the stroke and the victim!﻿—And ye! Nalaczi and Szekely, ennobled lackeys as you are, who cannot explain to yourselves how you became great lords, reflect that the wheel of Fortune debases as often as it exalts, and that as you treat others today so may others treat you tomorrow. And I say to you all, ye noble cavaliers, who seek your courage in your cups, bethink you and tremble at the thought, that not wine but innocent blood is foaming in the beakers that you hold in your hands! Shame, shame upon you all! who give wine to the Prince in order to ask blood of him. And now your Highness may add a couple of weeks to my term of banishment.”

With these words, the Princess rapidly left the room. The lords were dumb, and dared not look at each other. But Teleki got up, closed the door, dipped his pen in the inkhorn, and said﻿—

“And now we will go on where we left off.”

===Chinese===

“安娜！”阿帕菲喊道，羞和酒一齐涌上脸，涨得通红，“立刻出去。这是敕命——往后一礼拜之内，不得来见驾。”

“由它去吧，阿帕菲。对你我无话可说，因为你此刻不是你自己；可你，首席枢臣先生，你这个永远清醒的人，我有话要说。我把你从尘埃里扶起来；是我扶你登上你如今站的这个位置；你报答我的，是把自己楔进我和亲王的心之间——我每回走近我丈夫，都撞见你挡在道旁。你从亲王手里夺走了权杖，换给他一把刽子手的刀；可我告诉你，我的心纵然够不着亲王，至少还拦得住那把刀——它每回落下，你都会看见我横在刀锋与受难者之间！——还有你们！纳拉齐、塞凯伊，你们这帮封了爵的听差，连自己怎么成了达官贵人都说不明白的，想想吧：命运之轮升人也降人；你们今天怎样待人，明天人就怎样待你们。我对你们大家说——你们这些到酒杯里找勇气的贵族骑士们——好好想想，抖一抖吧：你们手里杯中翻腾起沫的，不是酒，是无辜者的血！可耻，你们统统可耻！灌亲王的酒，向他讨的却是血。现在，殿下可以再给我那放逐期添上两个礼拜了。”

说完这些话，王妃快步出了屋。贵族们哑口无言，彼此都不敢对视。泰莱基却站起身，关上门，笔往墨壶里一蘸，说道——

“现在，咱们接着方才的往下办。”

## VIII / 八
===Original===

Death for a Kiss

Paul Beldi went straight from Fehervár to Bodola: all the way he was tortured by the thought which Teleki’s words had revived.

In itself, a kiss is a very harmless thing. But what if another knows of it or has perceived it? Then indeed it becomes the pole of our suspicion, round which the mind weaves a whole pandemonium of doubts and guesses. We begin to think what might have led up to it, and what it may lead to. And in this case another did know of it. The husband had reasoned with himself: a kiss of which nobody knows anything makes no rent in a wife’s virtue﻿—and behold! it is in everyone’s mouth already. And perhaps they don’t stop there. Perhaps while he, fond fool! imagined his honour in safe keeping, the world with a loud Ha, ha! has long been dragging it through the mire, and his ear is the very last to catch the insulting laugh. And that his mortal foe, too, should be at the bottom of it!

Night had fallen. The horses were tired out. Beldi had nowhere given them rest, nowhere changed them for fresh ones. He wanted to get home as quickly as possible. He wanted to meet face to face the woman who had so disgraced him, heaven only knew how much! But why be content to see a woman weep or die, when there was a man on whom vengeance could be taken? A man who had ever been his foe, from the time when they had been pages together at Prince Gabriel Bethlen’s court, and had now fastened on the most sensitive spot in his heart and ruthlessly torn it.

“Turn back,” he cried to the coachman, “and go in the direction of Klausenburg.”

The old servant shook his head; turned into a side-path, and so completely lost himself in the darkness of the night, that he was forced to confess to his master that he really did not know where he was.

Beldi’s rage and impatience knew no bounds. Looking about him, he perceived a small light burning at no great distance, and sulkily bade his coachman drive in that direction.

It was into the courtyard of a lonely country-house that they rolled at last, and Beldi recognized in the master of the house, who appeared at the barking of the large watchdogs, old Adam Gyergyai, one of his dearest friends, who, when he saw Beldi, rushed into his arms, and was beside himself with joy.

===Chinese===

一吻偿命

保罗·贝尔迪（Paul Beldi）从费海尔瓦尔（Fehervár）径直奔波多拉去：一路上，泰莱基那句话勾起的念头把他折磨个不休。

一吻本身，本是顶无害的东西。可倘若另一个人知道、或察觉了呢？那它便当真成了疑心的磁极，头脑绕着它，编织出一整座疑云猜雾的魔窟。我们开始想：它因何而起，又会引向何方。而这桩事，偏偏真有另一个人知道。做丈夫的曾自宽自解：一个无人知晓的吻，斯不破妻子的贞洁——瞧吧！它如今却已挂在了每个人的嘴上。兴许还不止于此。兴许当他这痴心的蠢人还自以为名节安然无恙时，世人早笑着一嗓子"哈，哈！"把他的名节拖进了泥里，而他的耳朵，竟是最后听见那羞辱笑声的地方。落井下石的，竟还是他的死敌！

夜幕已经落下。马匹累坏了。贝尔迪一路没让它们歇过，也没换过一匹。他只想尽快到家。他要面对面去见那个女人——天知道她辱没他到了什么地步！可是，既然有一个可以复仇的男人在，又何必满足于看一个女人哭泣或死去？那人从来就是他的对头——从两人同在加布里埃尔·贝特伦（Gabriel Bethlen）亲王宫中做侍童时起就是——如今又抓住他心上最痛的一处，狠狠撕了个开。

"掉头，"他朝车夫喊，"往克劳森堡方向走。"

老仆摇了摇头；转上一条岔道，便一头扎进夜色里，迷得不辨东西，只得向主人承认：他实在不知道这是到了哪儿。

贝尔迪的狂怒和焦躁没了边际。他四下一望，见不远处有一点小小的灯火，便没好气地吩咐车夫朝那儿赶。

车轮最后滚进的，是一座孤零零田庄的院子；犬吠声中出迎的庄主，贝尔迪认得——是他的至交老友亚当·盖尔盖伊（Adam Gyergyai）。老人一见贝尔迪，扑进他怀里，欢喜得不知如何是好。

===Original===

“God be with you!” said the good old man, covering his guest with kisses. “I will not ask what piece of good fortune has brought you to me.”

“To tell the truth, I’ve lost my way. I was on the road to Klausenburg. I must get there tonight; but I’ll rest my horses here for an hour or two if you’ll let me.”

“What pressing business is this you have on hand?”

“I must deliver a message,” replied Beldi evasively.

“If that be all, why so much hurry? Write it down, and one of my mounted servants shall immediately take it to its destination while you remain here.”

“You are right,” said Beldi, after some reflection; “it will be better to send a letter,” and with that he asked for writing materials, sat down, and wrote to Banfi.

The mere act of writing generally clears and calms the mind, so that it was in a fairly moderate tone that Beldi challenged Banfi to meet him at Szamos-Ujvar on an affair of honour. Beldi then sealed the letter and gave it to Gyergyai, requesting him to forward it at once.

“So you are writing to Banfi, my brother?” said the old man, looking at the address of the letter. “Why, you only parted from him a little time ago! What is all this between you?”

“Do you recollect the time, my father,” said Beldi, “when you saw Banfi and me fight together in the lists at the tournament held by Prince George Rakoczy?”

“Quite well! On that occasion you had both vanquished every other competitor, but could do nothing against each other.”

“You then said that you would very much like to see which of the two would beat the other if we set to it in earnest.”

“Yes; I well remember it.”

“Well, now you *shall* see!”

Gyergyai looked Beldi in the face.

“My brother, I know not what this letter contains, but I can guess your thoughts from your face. My father used to say that a letter written in wrath should never be sent off the same day, but should be put under one’s pillow and slept upon. The advice is not bad; follow it, and send off the letter tomorrow morning, for, to be candid with you, I won’t send it tonight.”

===Chinese===

"上帝与你同在！"善良的老人说着，吻了又吻他的客人。"你撞了什么好运到我这儿来，我一句也不问。"

"实话说，我迷了路。我本是要去克劳森堡的。今晚必须赶到；不过若蒙你应允，我想让马在这儿歇上一两个钟头。"

"你手头有什么火烧眉毛的事？"

"我得送个口信。"贝尔迪含糊地答。

"若只是这个，何必这么急？写下来，我派个骑马的仆人立刻送到地方去，你只管在这儿歇着。"

"也好。"贝尔迪想了想说，"还是送封信妥当。"说着他要来纸笔，坐下给班菲写信。

单是书写这个动作，通常便能使头脑清明、平静下来；所以贝尔迪约班菲到索莫什-乌伊瓦尔（Szamos-Ujvar）决斗、了结名誉之争的挑战书，措辞还算相当克制。写罢封好，交给盖尔盖伊，请他即刻发出。

"你这是写给班菲，我的兄弟？"老人望着信封上的地址说，"怎么，你们不是才分手不久吗！你们俩这到底是怎么回事？"

"老人家，你可记得，"贝尔迪说，"在乔治·拉科齐亲王的比武会上，你看见班菲和我在擂台上交手的那一回？"

"记得清楚！那一回，你们俩把别的对手全都挑落马下，彼此之间却分不出高下。"

"你当时说，很想看看我们俩若是真刀真枪地干起来，谁会赢谁。"

"是；我记得清楚。"

"好，如今你*就要*看到了！"

盖尔盖伊直望着贝尔迪的脸。

"我的兄弟，这信里写了什么我不知道，可从你脸上我猜得出你的心思。我父亲常说：盛怒之下写的信，当天万万不可发出，要压在枕头底下，睡它一觉再发。这主意不坏；听我的，明天一早再发——因为同你交个底：今晚我是不会替你送的。"

===Original===

Beldi followed the old man’s advice. He put the letter under his pillow, lay down, went to sleep, and dreamt that he was in the bosom of his family, saw his wife and children, and was very happy. It was only the rolling of his carriage into the courtyard next morning which woke him out of his slumbers. The first thing that occurred to him was his letter to Banfi. He broke the seal, read the letter through again, and was much ashamed that he had ever written such a letter.

“Where was your common sense, Beldi?” he asked himself, tore the letter to pieces, and threw it into the fire. “How the world would have laughed at me!” thought he. “An old fool, to take it into his head all at once to be jealous of the mother of his children!﻿—and for the sake of a kiss too given in drunkenness and rejected with indignation. What a weapon I should have put into Banfi’s hands, had I led him to suppose that I was jealous of my wife on his account.”

“Let us go to Bodola,” said he very gently to his coachman, and with that he took leave of his host.

“But how about that pressing letter of yours?” asked Gyergyai anxiously.

“I have already sent it﻿—up the chimney,” replied Beldi, smiling, and set out on his journey with feelings very different from those with which he had started.

So you see a man can be drunk without wine!

While still some distance from Bodola, he could see all the members of his family looking out for him on the castle terrace, and no sooner did they perceive his carriage, than they hastened down to greet him. He met them all in the park, wife and children; they threw themselves on his neck with cries of joy, and he kissed them all, one after another, over and over again; but his warmest embraces were for his darling wife, who smiled up at him with a radiant face, which he could not feast his eyes upon enough. It seemed to him as if her eyes were brighter, her features more enchanting, her lips sweeter than ever they had been.

“What a fool a man is, to be sure,” thought Beldi, “who, when his wife is out of sight, is capable of supposing everything bad of her, and when she stands before his eyes cannot make too much of her.”

In the abandonment of his joy he did not at first perceive that there was a strange face in the family circle﻿—a handsome, stately young Turk, with frank and noble features, not unlike an Hungarian.

===Chinese===

贝尔迪听了老人的话。他把信压在枕下，躺下便睡，梦见自己身在家人中间，见到妻子儿女，幸福得很。直到第二天早晨马车辚辚滚进院子，才把他从睡梦里惊醒。他头一件想起的，就是给班菲的那封信。他拆开封漆，从头再读一遍，顿时羞愧难当——竟写出过这样一封信。

"你的脑子哪儿去了，贝尔迪？"他问自己，随即把信撕成碎片，扔进火里。"世人该怎么怎么笑话我！"他想，"一个老糊涂，竟然一时鬼迷心穹，吃起自己儿女的母亲的醋来！——况且为的不过是一个酒后强来、当场就被愤然拒斥的吻。倘若我让他以为我疑心妻子同他有私，那等于往班菲手里塞了一件多好的兵器。"

"去波多拉吧。"他温声对车夫说，随后辞别了主人。

"可你那封十万火急的信呢？"盖尔盖伊担心地问。

"已经送出去了——从烟囱里。"贝尔迪微笑着答道，随即登程；这一路的心境，同来时已判若两人。

可见人不喝酒也能醉！

离波多拉还有好一段路，他已望见全家人都在城堡露台上张望；一认出他的马车，便急忙奔下来迎他。他在园林里同他们相遇——妻子和孩子们；他们欢呼着扑上他的脖颈，他把他们挨个儿亲了又亲，一遍又一遍；而最热烈的拥抱，留给了他亲爱的妻子：她仰起光彩焕发的脸朝他微笑，他怎么看也看不够。他只觉得她的眼睛比从前更亮，眉目比从前更动人，唇比从前更甜。

"人真是天生的傻瓜。"贝尔迪想，"妻子不在眼前时，什么坏事都疑得出；等她站到眼前，又怎么疼都疼不够。"

他纵情欢喜，起初竟没留意家里的圈子里有一张生面孔——一个俊美挺拔的土耳其青年，眉宇开朗高贵，不像土耳其人，倒像匈牙利人。

===Original===

“You do not even notice me, or perhaps you forget me,” said the youth, stepping in front of Beldi.

Beldi looked at him. The youth’s features were familiar to him, and yet he could not recall his name till his youngest daughter, Aranka, who was dangling on her father’s arm, remarked archly﻿—

“What! Not recognize Feriz Beg, papa! Why, I knew him at the first glance.”

Beldi at once held out his hand and heartily greeted the youth, whose manly features however wore a grave and serious look.

“My father sends me to you on an urgent errand,” said he, “and had you not come, I must have gone to seek you, for my message admits of no delay.”

Beldi was struck by the youth’s earnest tone, and on reaching the castle immediately took him aside into a private room, and there the young Beg handed him a parchment roll tied round with silken cord, and sealed with a yellow seal. Beldi broke the seal and read as follows﻿—

“The blessing and protection of heaven rest upon you and your family!﻿—Transylvania is in great danger. The Sultan is enraged at the war which Denis Banfi wages with the Pasha of Grosswardein. They say that this great noble is in league with the Emperor. See to it that the land chastises Banfi, the power to do so is still your own. But if the Prince cannot, or will not punish him, the Sultan has sworn to drive the pair of them out of the realm, and convert Transylvania into a Turkish Pachalic. The Pashas of Grosswardein and Temesvar, the Lord-Marchers, and the Tartar Khan have been ordered to hold themselves in readiness to invade Transylvania from all sides at a moment’s notice. Put a bit therefore in the mouth of this great lord, for death hangs over your heads on the film of a spider’s web.

“Your friend and brother,

“**Kucsuk Pasha**.”

Beldi’s face grew dark as he read this letter. So it was all in vain that he had driven Banfi’s name out of his head. This letter conjured up that odious form once more before his eyes.

He folded up the parchment and gave the grave youth a brief answer to take back with him﻿—

“Let your father know that we will take the necessary steps to avert the threatened evil, and thank him heartily for his warning.”

===Chinese===

"您眼里根本没有我，也许压根把我忘了。"青年说着，跨到贝尔迪面前。

贝尔迪端详着他。这张面孔分明眼熟，名字却一时想不起来；还是吊在他手臂上的小女儿阿兰卡（Aranka）狡黠地提点道——

"什么！认不出费里兹·贝伊（Feriz Beg）了，爸爸！我可是一眼就认出他了呢。"

贝尔迪立刻伸出手，同青年热络地见礼；只是那张英气勃勃的脸上，罩着一层凝重。

"家父有急事差我找您。"他说，"您若不回来，我就只得去寻您了，因为我要传的话刻不容缓。"

青年恳切的语调让贝尔迪心头一凛；一到城堡，他便把他拉进一间密室。小贝伊（beg）取出一卷以丝绦束着、盖黄蜡印的羊皮纸。贝尔迪拆开封印，读到如下文字——

"愿上天的祝福与庇佑，降临你和你的家人！——特兰西瓦尼亚（Transylvania）正处于极大的危险中。苏丹（Sultan）对德尼斯·班菲（Denis Banfi）与大瓦尔代帕夏的战事大为震怒。据说这位大贵族与皇帝勾结。务必让国家制裁班菲——这力量如今还在你们自己手里。倘若亲王不能、也不肯惩办他，苏丹已立誓把这一对人都逐出国门，把特兰西瓦尼亚改成土耳其的帕夏领地。大瓦尔代和蒂米什瓦尔（Temesvar）的两位帕夏、各边疆领主和鞑靼（Tartar）汗，都已奉命整装待发，一有号令便从四面侵入特兰西瓦尼亚。所以，快给这位大老爷的嘴上套上嚼子——死亡如今只隔着一层蛛丝，悬在你们头顶。

"你的朋友与兄弟，

"**库丘克帕夏（Kucsuk Pasha）**。"

读着这封信，贝尔迪的脸色阴沉下来。原来他把班菲的名字从脑中驱走，全是枉然。这封信又把那个可憎的身影，重新召到了他眼前。

他折起羊皮纸，给神情凝重的青年一句简短的回话，让他带回——

"禀告令尊：我们必将采取必要步骤，消弭这一威胁；并请代我衷心感谢他的预警。"

===Original===

Feriz Beg immediately quitted Bodola Castle. Beldi remained alone in his room, pacing to and fro in a brown study, and racking his brains to find a way out of the danger. He could find none. It was not to be expected that Banfi’s pride would yield to the Pasha, especially after a brilliant victory and in a just cause; and yet the welfare of the land required the sacrifice of the just cause.

Brooding thus, he did not notice that somebody was tapping at his door, who after thrice knocking and receiving no answer, opened it, and as Beldi suddenly came to himself and looked around him with a start, he perceived Michael Teleki standing before him. So amazed was Beldi by this apparition, that for the moment the power of speech forsook him.

“You appear surprised,” said Teleki, observing his amazement. “You are astonished that I should travel such a long way to see you, after parting from you only twenty-four hours ago. But great events have taken place in the meantime. Transylvania is threatened by a danger which must be averted as quickly as possible.”

“I know it,” replied Beldi, and putting his hand over the signature, he let Teleki read Kucsuk’s letter.

“Great heaven!” exclaimed the minister. “You know more than I did. But what I want to say on this matter is a secret which the very walls around us may not hear.”

“I understand,” replied Beldi, and immediately commanded his heydukes to admit no one into the vestibules; placed guards in front of the windows, and drew the curtains down to the ground. There now only remained a little tapestried door, at the back of the room, which led through a narrow corridor to his wife’s bedchamber, an arrangement very common, at that time, in the mansions of Hungarian magnates. By way of additional precaution Beldi closed this door also.

“Does your Excellency feel secure enough now?” asked Beldi.

“One thing more. Give me your word of honour that if what I am about to disclose does not meet with your approbation, you will at least keep it secret.”

“I promise,” returned Beldi, impatiently awaiting the dénouement of all this mystery.

Teleki thereupon drew forth a long strip of parchment, unfolded it, and held it before Beldi’s eyes, without however letting it out of his hands.

It was the league against Banfi, signed and sealed by the Prince.

===Chinese===

费里兹·贝伊随即离开了波多拉城堡。贝尔迪独自留在房里，苦思沉想，来回踱步，绞尽脑汁寻找脱险之路。他找不到。指望班菲的傲骨向帕夏低头，是办不到的——尤其是在大获全胜、理由堂堂正正之后；可国家的安危，偏偏又要求牺牲这份正当的事业。

正想得出神，他没觉察有人在敲门；那人敲了三遍无人应，便推门进来。贝尔迪蓦地回神，惊跳四顾——米哈伊·泰莱基（Michael Teleki）立在面前。这一现身惊得贝尔迪一时说不出话来。

"您似乎很惊讶。"泰莱基瞧着他的惊色说，"您奇怪，你我二十四小时前才分手，我竟赶这么远的路来见您。可这中间出了大事。特兰西瓦尼亚正面临一场危险，必须尽快化解。"

"我知道了。"贝尔迪答道，用手捂住落款，让泰莱基读了库丘克的信。

"我的天！"首相失声道，"您知道的比我还多。不过我要就此说的那件事，是一桩连四周墙壁都不许听的机密。"

"我明白。"贝尔迪答道，随即吩咐众海杜克（heyduke）不许任何人进前厅；又在窗前设了岗，把窗帘直垂到地。如今只剩屋后一扇小小的织锦包门，它经一条窄廊通到他妻子的卧房——在那年月，匈牙利大贵族的宅邸里这种格局司空见惯。为万全起见，贝尔迪把这扇门也关上了。

"大人这回觉得够严密了么？"贝尔迪问。

"还有一件事。请您以名誉为质：倘若我即将披露的事不合您的意，您至少也须守口如瓶。"

"我答应。"贝尔迪答道，急不可待地等着这一团玄虚的收场。

泰莱基于是抽出一长条羊皮纸，展开来，举到贝尔迪眼前——却始终不撒手。

那是一份针对班菲的同盟书，已有亲王的签名画押。

===Original===

The more Beldi read of this document, the blacker grew his looks, till at last, turning his face away, he pushed the document aside with an expression of deep disgust.

“Sir,” said he, “ ’tis a dirty piece of work!”

Teleki was prepared for some such answer, and summoned to his aid all the sophistry of which he was so perfect a master.

“Beldi!” cried he, “we must, for once, put aside all narrow-minded sentiment. Here it is a question of the end and not of the means. The means may seem bad, but we really have no other. Whenever a subject becomes so powerful in a state that the arm of the law is no longer able to bring him to justice, then I say he has only himself to blame if the state is compelled to conspire against him. He whom the axe of the executioner cannot reach, must fall beneath the dagger of the bravo. Denis Banfi, by despising the Prince’s commands and waging war on his own account, has placed himself outside the law. In such a case, where the ordinary tribunals become inoperative, we must of course have resort to secret tribunals. If anyone injures me, and the law can give me no remedy, I make use of my own weapons, and shoot him down wherever I meet him. If the country is injured by anyone whom it cannot punish, it must fall back upon the *jus ligatum*, and lay hands upon him whenever and wherever it can. The commonweal requires, the common danger compels such a step.”

“We are in the hands of God!” replied Beldi. “If ’tis His will to destroy the fatherland, we can only bow the head and die in defence of our freedom with a good conscience. But never ought we to lift our hands against the liberties we have inherited from our forefathers. Rather let us endure the wrongs which spring from those liberties, than lay the axe to the root of them ourselves! Rather let war and strife burst over the land, than conspire against the laws! That may cost the nation its blood; but this will destroy its very soul. I disapprove of this league, and, sir, I mean to oppose it!”

At these words Michael Teleki rose from his seat, sank down upon his knees before Beldi, raised his hands to heaven, and cried﻿—

===Chinese===

这份文书，贝尔迪越读脸色越黑，最后把脸扭向一旁，满脸嫌恶地把它推开了。

"先生，"他说，"这可是桩肮脏的勾当！"

泰莱基早备下了应对这类回答的说辞，把那套他炉火纯青的诡辩术全数搬了出来。

"贝尔迪！"他喊道，"这一次，我们必须把狭隘的感情搁在一旁。这里要紧的是目的，不是手段。手段看着或许龌龊，可我们实在没有别的。一个臣子在一国之内权势大到法律之臂再也无法将他绳之以法，那我说，国家被迫对他行阴谋，就全怪他自己。刽子手的斧头够不着的人，就得死在刺客的匕首下。德尼斯·班菲蔑视亲王之命、擅自开战，已把自己置于法律之外。这种情形，寻常法庭既已无能为力，我们自然只能诉诸秘密法庭。有谁伤了我，而法律给不了我救济，我就用自己的兵器，撞见他在哪儿就把他撲倒在哪儿。国家若受了某个它惩办不了的人的伤害，便只得求助于*结盟之法*（jus ligatum），随时随地能下手就下手。公益所需，公危所迫，不得不走出这一步。"

"我们都在上帝手里！"贝尔迪答道，"他若定要毁灭祖国，我们只能俯首受命，凭着无愧的良心，为捍卫自由而死。可我们万万不该向先祖传下的自由举起手来！与其亲手把斧头砍向它的根，不如忍受由它生出的种种不公！宁可让战火与纷争降临国土，也决不可阴谋对付法律！那样牺牲的，至多是民族的血；这样毁掉的，却是民族的灵魂。我不赞成这份同盟书，先生，而且我要反对它！"

听了这话，米哈伊·泰莱基从座上起身，扑通跪倒在贝尔迪面前，双手举天，喊道——

===Original===

“I swear by the living God, that as I hope for my own and my family’s protection and happiness here and for salvation hereafter, that what I now do, I do as your loyal friend, well knowing that all Banfi’s efforts aim at the ruin of your house, and I solemnly adjure you, as you love your life and the lives of your wife and children, to avert the impending danger by signing the league. I have now done all in my power to save you and my country, and that too at my own risk and peril. I have no other object. Before God I lie not!”

Beldi turned with calm dignity towards the minister, and said, in a tone of immovable conviction﻿—

“*Fiat justitia, pereat mundus!*”

---

A few moments after Teleki’s arrival at Bodola, a mounted heyduke had galloped into the courtyard; it was Andrew, Dame Apafi’s faithful old servant, who handed to Dame Beldi a letter from the Princess, adding that the message was doubly urgent, as he already perceived in the courtyard Teleki’s coachman, whom he ought to have forestalled.

Dame Beldi hastily opened the letter and read as follows﻿—

“Dear Sister﻿—

“Michael Teleki has set out for Bodola to see your husband. His aim is to secretly ruin Banfi by the hand of Beldi. The magnates have conspired together to break the law. Fortunately, every one of them has a wife, and in the hearts of our women the better feelings of human nature are not yet extinguished. I have charged each one of them to preserve their husbands from Teleki’s wiles; but ’tis to you that I chiefly look for help. Beldi is the most eminent of them all. If he joins the league, the rest will follow his example; but he is also the most honourable of men and the best of husbands. I count upon your firmness. Move heaven and earth!

“Your loving sister,

“**Anna Bornemissa**.”

On reading this letter, Dame Beldi almost swooned.

===Chinese===

"我凭永生的上帝起誓：我此刻的所作所为，正如我指望今生自家阖宅平安幸福、来世灵魂得救一样，是出自对你的一片忠忱——我深知班菲的一切图谋，都是要倾覆你的门庭；我郑重恳求你，既然你爱惜自己的性命、爱惜妻子儿女的性命，就签署这份同盟书，消弭这迫在眉睫的祸事。为拯救你、拯救我的国家，能做的我已全部做了，而且甘冒自家的风险。我别无他图。在上帝面前，我说的没有半句假话！"

贝尔迪带着平静的尊严转向首相，用一种不容动摇的确信口吻说——

"*Fiat justitia, pereat mundus!*（纵使天塌地陷，也要伸张正义！）"

---

泰莱基抵达波多拉后不多时，一名骑马的海杜克飞驰进院；来者是安德鲁（Andrew），阿帕菲夫人忠心的老仆。他把亲王夫人的一封信呈给贝尔迪夫人，又补上一句：这信十万火急、双倍的火急——因为他已认出院里泰莱基的车夫，那本是他该抢先赶在前头的。

贝尔迪夫人急忙拆信，读到如下文字——

"亲爱的妹妹——

"米哈伊·泰莱基已赴波多拉见你丈夫。他的目的是借贝尔迪之手，暗算班菲。大贵族们已串通一气要践踏法律。所幸他们人人有妻，而我们女人的心里，人性的善感尚未熄灭。我已嘱托她们每一个，各自拦住丈夫、莫中泰莱基的圈套；但我主要指望的是你。贝尔迪是他们中间最杰出的。他若入了盟，余人必群起效尤；可他也是顶讲信义的人、顶好的丈夫。我全指望你的坚定。上天入地也要拦住他！

"爱你的姐姐

"**安娜·博内米莎**。"

读罢此信，贝尔迪夫人几乎晕了过去。

===Original===

Teleki had already been closeted with her husband for more than half-an-hour, and the servants had brought word that everyone had been ordered away, even from the passages leading to the room. In an instant she divined everything. Terror seized her. Perhaps it was already too late! But what could she do? Suddenly, the secret corridor occurred to her, which led from her bedroom to her husband’s. Urged by fear, she rapidly traversed the corridor, reached the tapestried door, stood still before it with a beating heart, and listened. She could only hear Teleki, and he was speaking in an unusually excited voice, which rose almost to a scream. She looked through the keyhole, and beheld the minister on his knees before her husband with uplifted hands, endeavouring to move him by solemn oaths.

Such a sight made Dame Beldi perfectly frantic. What must it be that could make a man so proud and so exalted kneel down before Beldi? What is he swearing so vehemently? Suddenly Banfi’s name struck on her ear; she turned pale with horror, and at the same instant she heard Beldi say the words﻿—“*Fiat justitia, pereat mundus!*” Ignorant as she was of the Latin language, she at once jumped to the conclusion that her husband had yielded, and in her desperation pressed hard upon the door-latch, and finding it immovable, shook the door furiously, exclaiming wildly at the same time﻿—

“My husband! My beloved lord! Lord of my soul! Give no heed to Teleki’s words, for he would ruin you.”

Both the men started at this passionate cry, and Beldi rose from his seat, went to the door, opened it, and cried angrily to his wife﻿—

“Go to your work, woman! You have no business here.”

Then Dame Beldi lost her presence of mind altogether. Fear did not allow her to reflect. The idea that her husband was consenting to Teleki’s schemes rendered her incapable of grasping the situation; and she forgot that the most complaisant of husbands, rather than see his uxoriousness paraded before the world, will do violence to his better nature. So Dame Beldi rushed wildly into the room, sank down at her husband’s feet, convulsively clasped his knees, and cried in a voice of passionate remonstrance﻿—

“Sweet lord of my heart! I adjure you not to believe in that man. Don’t be led away. He would bring down innocent blood upon your head. You are too just and merciful to become a headsman.”

===Chinese===

泰莱基同她丈夫密谈已半个多钟头，仆人还传话说，人人都被打发开了，连通向那间屋的过道也不例外。霎时间她全明白了。恐惧攣住了她。也许已经迟了！可她能做什么？猛然间，她想起那条从她卧房通到丈夫房里的秘廊。恐惧催逼着她，她飞快穿过廊子，到了那扇织锦小门前，心怦怦跳着站定，侧耳细听。只听得见泰莱基的声音，语气激动异常，几乎成了尖叫。她凑近锁眼一望：首相正跪在她丈夫面前，双手高举，用庄严的誓言打动他。

这幅光景看得贝尔迪夫人六神无主。到底是什么事，能让一个这样高傲、这样显赫的人在贝尔迪面前跪下？他赌这样重的咒，为的是什么？忽然，班菲的名字撞进她耳中；她吓得脸色煞白，而同一瞬间，她听见贝尔迪说出那句——"*Fiat justitia, pereat mundus!*（纵使天塌地陷，也要伸张正义！）"她不谙拉丁文，登时断定丈夫已经屈服；绝望之下，她死命去压门闩，见纹丝不动，便狂怒地摇起门来，一边声嘶力竭地喊——

"我的丈夫！我的夫君！我的命根子！别听泰莱基的话，他要毁了你！"

两人都被这声嘶喊惊起。贝尔迪离座走到门边，拉开门，怒冲冲地朝妻子喝道——

"干你的活去，婆娘！这里没你的事。"

贝尔迪夫人这下彻底乱了方寸。恐惧不容她细想。认定丈夫正在应允泰莱基的计谋，她再也把握不住眼前的局面；她忘了一件事：天下最温存的丈夫，也不肯把自己惧内的样子当众示人，宁肯违逆自己的天性。于是贝尔迪夫人疯也似的冲进屋来，扑倒在丈夫脚边，痉挛般抱住他的双膝，用满腔哀恳的声音喊道——

"我心上的夫君！我求你，别信那个人。别让人牵着走。他要把无辜的血泼到你头上。你这样公正、这样仁慈，做不成刽子手的。"

===Original===

“Get up, woman! You are mad!”

“Oh! I know what I’m saying. I saw him kneel to you. He who believes in God, kneels not to man. He would ruin Denis Banfi through you. Woe betide us if you help him! For if Banfi be the first, you will assuredly be the second.”

When Teleki saw his secret design thus exposed, he grew wroth.

“If my wife were to treat me so,” cried he passionately, “I would tear her eyes out. If anyone came to me with a saving word of friendship on his tongue, I would thank him for it, and not allow my wife to lead me by the nose.”

Beldi turned furiously upon his wife and ordered her out.

“I’ll remain here even if you kill me, for ’tis a matter of life or death. When the peace of my family is at stake, I think ’tis time for me to speak. I beg, I implore you to hear me. I’ll not allow you to sacrifice Banfi.”

Beldi was already so ashamed of this onslaught on his marital authority that he was nearly beside himself; but when his wife began to plead for Banfi, he started back as if an adder had bitten him.

This did not escape Teleki, and with malicious innuendo he exclaimed﻿—

“It seems to me that wives forget *some things* much sooner than their husbands.”

Quick as lightning the dart pierced through Beldi’s soul. The recollection of that kiss came back to him. Pale and speechless, he seized his wife’s arm; her loud sobs only inflamed his jealousy, and dragging her to the tapestried door, he pushed her out and closed it behind her. There she remained, lying on the threshold, loudly cursing the Prince’s minister, and hammering at the closed door with her fists.

Beldi, pale as death, sat down at the table, gnashed his teeth, and whispered huskily﻿—

“Where’s the document?”

Teleki spread out the parchment roll before him on the table.

Beldi took up his pen without a word, and wrote his name in a bold hand beneath that of Michael Apafi.

A triumphant smile played around Teleki’s lips.

No sooner was the deed done than something in Beldi’s breast began to accuse him. Resting his hand on the document, he turned with a very grave face towards Teleki.

“I expressly stipulate,” he murmured, in a hollow voice, “that if Banfi be arrested, right and justice shall be done to him, according to the law of the land.”

“Quite so! Of course!” returned the Prince’s counsellor, making a snatch at the document.

Still Beldi would not let it go.

===Chinese===

"起来，婆娘！你疯了！"

"哦！我知道自己在说什么。我看见他给你下跪了。信上帝的人，不跪人。他要借你的手毁掉德尼斯·班菲。你若帮了他，我们就要遭殃！班菲若是头一个，你必是第二个。"

泰莱基见自己的密谋这样被戳穿，恼了。

"我的妻子若敢这样对我，"他怒气冲冲地喊，"我会把她的眼珠子挖出来。谁带着一句救人的好话来见我，我感激他还来不及，断不能容老婆牵着我的鼻子走。"

贝尔迪狂怒地转向妻子，喝令她出去。

"你就是杀了我，我也留在这儿，因为这是生死攸关的事。我全家的安宁悬在秤上，我想，该轮到我说话了。我求你，我恳求你听我说。我不许你牺牲班菲。"

丈夫的威严这样当众失守，贝尔迪已羞得几乎发狂；可等妻子开口为班菲求起情来，他猛地一缩，像被蝰蛇咬了一口。

这没能逃过泰莱基的眼睛，他含沙射影地刺了一句——

"依我看，做妻子的忘性，可比丈夫们大得多——忘掉*某些事*尤其快。"

那支毒箭闪电般穿透了贝尔迪的灵魂。那一吻的记忆回来了。他脸色煞白，一言不发，抓住妻子的手臂；她放声的呜咽只把他的妒火烧得更旺；他把她拖到织锦小门前，一把推出，反手把门关死。她就横在那门槛上，高声咒骂着亲王的首相，用双拳捶着紧闭的门。

贝尔迪面如死灰，在桌前坐下，咬着牙，声音沙哑地低语——

"文书在哪儿？"

泰莱基把那卷羊皮纸在他面前的桌上摊开。

贝尔迪一言不发地提起笔，在米哈伊·阿帕菲（Michael Apafi）的名字底下，挥笔签下了自己的名字。

一抹得胜的微笑在泰莱基唇边荡开。

字一落笔，贝尔迪胸中便有个声音开始控告他。他把手按在文书上，神色极为凝重地转向泰莱基。

"我特别言明，"他声音空洞地低语，"倘若班菲被捕，必须按照国法，还他一个公道与正义。"

"正是！当然！"亲王的重臣应道，伸手就去抓那份文书。

贝尔迪却仍不撒手。

===Original===

“Sir,” said he, “promise me that you will not secretly assassinate Banfi; but that when once he is arrested you will proceed against him before the proper Court of Justice, and in the usual, legitimate way. If you don’t guarantee me that, I’ll tear this parchment to pieces and throw it into the fire, together with my own and the Prince’s signatures.”

“I promise it to you on my word of honour,” replied the minister, inwardly smiling at the man who was so weak so long as he stood upright, and made such a brave show of firmness when he had already fallen.

That same day Teleki hastened with the subscribed league to Ladislaus Csaky, and from him to Haller, and from him to the Bethlens. As soon as they saw Beldi’s name, they signed the document without more ado, for all of them hated Banfi.

In every case the wives intervened. Terrible scenes took place. Nowhere did Teleki escape scot-free. But the league was successfully carried through, and that was, after all, the main thing.

And thus it was that Transylvania dug her own grave.

===Chinese===

"先生，"他说，"答应我：你们不许暗中刺杀班菲；一旦他被捕，必须交由正当的法院，循通常的合法程序对他起诉。你不给我这个保证，我就把这羊皮纸斯个粉碎，连同我自己的和亲王的名字，一齐扔进火里。"

"我以名誉向你担保。"首相答道，心里暗笑这个人：站着的时候那样软弱，栽倒之后，倒撑出这么一副硬骨头的架势。

当天，泰莱基便带着签好的同盟书赶去见拉斯洛·恰基（Ladislaus Csaky），又从他那里到哈勒家，再到贝特伦家。一见贝尔迪的名字，他们二话不说便签了字——他们个个恨班菲。

每到一家，夫人们都横加拦阻。可怕的场面一场接一场。哪一家也没让泰莱基全身而退。可同盟书终究办成了——说到底，这才是要紧的。

特兰西瓦尼亚，就这样亲手掘下了自己的坟墓。

## IX / 九
===Original===

Consort and Concubine

Ever since that painful scene at Bonczhida, Lady Banfi had not met her husband. Fate so willed it that Banfi was constantly away from home; scarcely had he come back from the Diet of Fehervár when he was called away to Somlyo, where his troops stood face to face with the Turks. During the few hours however that he remained at home, his wife had locked herself up from him; not even the domestics caught a glimpse of her face. She did not quit her chamber, and received no one.

One day both the spouses were invited to Roppad by a distant kinsman, one Gabriel Vitez, who knew nothing of their estrangement, to act as sponsors to his newborn son. To decline the invitation was impossible, and thus it came about that on the day in question, Lady Banfi coming from Bonczhida and her husband from Somlyo met together, to their mutual confusion, at the festive mansion of the Vitezes.

At the first meeting they instinctively shrank back from each other. They had both indeed longed for such a meeting, but pride had kept them apart, and thus while their affection rejoiced at, their pride revolted against this chance encounter. Of course they let nothing of all this appear openly. In the presence of their friends they had so to conduct themselves that nobody might suspect that this meeting was anything but an everyday occurrence.

At the end of the banquet, which lasted far into the night, Master Gabriel Vitez took care that all his guests should be lodged with the utmost convenience. Husbands and wives and all the young girls had separate quarters, and the young men were accommodated in the hunting saloon. For Banfi and his spouse the garden pavilion had been reserved, which, being at some distance from the noisy courtyard, promised to be the quietest resting-place of all. The host, with the most distinguished courtesy, accompanied them thither himself.

It was now a long time since they had slept together under the same roof.

Before so many acquaintances they could not declare their estrangement, and had been compelled to accept the nice quarters provided for them by their amiable host, who insisted, despite their protests, in showing them the way; jested pleasantly with them for a time, and only left them to themselves after wishing them good night some scores of times.

===Chinese===

正妻与宠姬

自邦奇达那场伤心的风波之后，班菲夫人再没见过丈夫。命运偏偏这样安排：班菲常年在外；刚从费海尔瓦尔（Fehervár）的议会回来，又被叫去索姆廖（Somlyo）——他的部队正在那里与土耳其人对峙。他在家待的那几个钟头里，妻子也闭门不见；连家仆都瞧不见她的脸。她不出房间，也不见任何人。

有一天，夫妻二人同时接到远亲加布里埃尔·维泰兹（Gabriel Vitez）的邀请——这位远亲对他们的失和一无所知——请他们去罗帕德（Roppad）给他新生的儿子当教父教母。这邀请推辞不得。于是到了那天，班菲夫人从邦奇达来，丈夫从索姆廖来，两人在维泰兹家喜庆的宅邸里撞了个对面，都有些手足无措。

乍一见面，两人都本能地各自缩了一下。这样的相见，两人其实都渴望已久，可骄傲把他们隔开；于是这场不期而遇，他们的深情为之欢喜，骄傲却为之抗拒。当然，这一切丝毫没有露到明面上。在亲友面前，他们的举止得做得滴水不漏，谁也别想看出这次会面不是寻常小事一桩。

宴会直到深夜方散。加布里埃尔·维泰兹老爷细心安排，务使宾客个个住得妥帖舒服。夫妻们、姑娘们各有各的住处，年轻小伙子们则安置在狩猎厅里。给班菲夫妇留的是花园里的一座凉阁，离闹哄哄的院子有段路，想必是全场最清静的歇处。主人以最殷勤的礼数，亲自陪他们前去。

他们已经很久很久没在同一片屋顶下过夜了。

当着这么多熟人的面，他们无法申明失和，只好领受这位和蔼主人备下的雅室；主人不顾推辞，执意引路，又陪他们说笑了一阵，道了不下二十遍晚安，这才把他们单独留下。

===Original===

The pavilion consisted of two small adjoining rooms, such cosy little cribs, with quite an air of home about them. In one of them a merry fire was crackling and flickering on the hearth. In the corner a tall solemn clock was softly ticking. The brocade curtains of the large tester-bed were half drawn back, revealing behind them a comfortable, snow-white, downy expanse, on which lay, side by side, *two* little pillows adorned with red ribbons.

In the other room, which was half lighted by the reflection of the fire, a couch was visible provided with a bearskin covering and a single stag-skin bolster. In all probability no one had ever thought that it would be occupied.

Banfi looked sadly at his wife. Now that he was no longer free to approach her, he saw what a heaven he had possessed in that noble and lovely being. She stood before him with downcast eyes, so sorrowful and yet so mild.

In her heart, too, many traitorous thoughts pleaded for her husband; wounded pride, that unbending judge, was already beginning to waver. In a noble breast it is not hate but grief that takes the place of love.

Banfi drew nearer to his wife, seized her hand, and pressed it in his own. He felt that her hand trembled, but he also felt that it did not return his pressure.

He went still further. He tenderly pressed her to him, and kissed her forehead, cheeks, and lips. She suffered his caresses but did not return them. But if only she had looked up into her husband’s eyes, she would have seen them glistening with two tears as sincere as ever repentant sinner shed.

Banfi, with a deep sigh, sat down in an armchair, still holding Margaret’s hand in his own; it needed but a single tender word from his wife, and he would have flung himself at her feet and wept like a remorseful child. Instead of that, Dame Banfi, with self-denying affectation, said to her husband﻿—

“Do you wish to remain in this room, and shall I go into the other?”

The icy tone of these words cut Banfi to the heart. His broad breast heaved a deep sigh, his eyes looked sorrowfully at Margaret’s joyless face﻿—to him a closed paradise. He rose gravely from his seat, pressed his wife’s hand to his lips, whispered her a scarcely audible good night, and tottered into the adjoining room, closing the door behind him.

===Chinese===

凉阁是两间相连的小屋，小小巧巧，暖意融融，很有家的味道。一间屋里，壁炉里欢快的炉火噼啪跳动。角落里，一座高大庄重的钟在轻轻滴答。大顶床的锦缎帷幔半开着，露出里面舒适雪白、蓬松柔软的一床铺盖，上头并排放着*两只*缀红丝带的小枕头。

另一间屋借着火光半明半暗，看得见一张卧榻，铺着熊皮，只搁了一枚鹿皮枕。十有八九，从没人想过它会派上用场。

班菲悲伤地望着妻子。如今他再不能随意亲近她了，才看清自己曾在这位高贵可爱的人儿身上拥有过怎样的天堂。她垂着眼站在他面前，那样哀伤，又那样温柔。

她心里，也有许多“叛徒”般的念头在替丈夫求情；那受伤的骄傲——铁面无私的法官——也已开始动摇。在高贵的胸怀里，接替爱的位置的，不是恨，而是悲伤。

班菲走近妻子，拉起她的手，握在自己手里。他觉出那只手在颤，可他也觉出，它没有回应他的紧握。

他得寸进尺。他温柔地把她搂进怀里，吻她的额头、脸颊和嘴唇。她承受着他的爱抚，却无动于衷。可她只要抬头看一眼丈夫的眼睛，就会看见那里面闪着两颗泪——像忏悔的罪人流下的泪那样真。

班菲长叹一声，跌坐在扶手椅里，仍握着玛格丽特的手；只需妻子一句软话，他就会扑倒在她脚下，像个知错的孩子一样大哭一场。谁知班菲夫人却强忍着心意，装出淡漠，对丈夫说——

“您想留在这间屋，让我到那间去吗？”

这句话冰冷的调子，直扎进班菲心里。他宽阔的胸膛深深一叹，眼睛哀哀地望着玛格丽特那张了无欢容的脸——对他来说，一座上了锁的乐园。他庄重地起身，把妻子的手按到唇边，低低道了一声几乎听不见的晚安，踉跄着走进隔壁房间，随手掩上了门。

===Original===

Dame Banfi set about disrobing, but on casting a glance at the lonely couch, a painful feeling overcame her. She threw herself sobbing on the pillows, and then, finding no rest for her soul there, she stood up again, drew a chair in front of the fire, sat down, and burying her face in her hands indulged in brooding, melancholy, dreamy thoughts.

And can there be any greater grief than when the heart fights against its own conviction; when a woman can no longer conceal from herself that the ideal of her love, him whom, after God, she loves the most, is after all only a common, ordinary mortal?﻿—that he whom she has loved so nobly deserves nothing but her contempt? And yet she cannot but love him! She feels she ought to hate him, yet she cannot bear the thought of being without him. She would fain die for him, and the opportunity of dying will not come.

A single unlocked door separates her from him. They are only a few steps apart. How small the distance, and yet how great! She can hear him sighing. He too cannot sleep while he is so near to her whom he has so deeply wounded. What bliss it would be to traverse those few steps, to nestle side by side, to gratify each other’s longings! But reconciliation is impossible; her heart yearns after it and recoils from it, loves and loathes at the same moment.

Oh! why can we not forget the Past? Why is it impossible to prevent Grief from grieving?

The lady fell a-thinking, a-dreaming.

It seemed to her as if she were talking to her husband in a vision﻿—

“You said yourself that we ought to part while we still loved each other, while our hearts would bleed at the rupture. Then why don’t you do it? Why do you sigh when you look at me? Why do you kiss me? Those sighs, those kisses are torture to me; they wound my heart. Let us part! It was your own wish.”

The fire had burnt very low in the grate; over the ruddy embers a pale, ever-dwindling flame was feebly flickering to and fro, like the last thought of an extinguished passion. All around the room was growing darker and darker; the light of the expiring embers barely lit up the form of the sorrowing lady who sat there, with her head buried in her hands, like a marble statue mourning over a tomb.

Suddenly, amid the silence of the night and of her own thoughts, it seemed to her as if whispering voices and stealthy footsteps were approaching the doors of the pavilion.

===Chinese===

班菲夫人动手卸装，可一瞥见那张孤零零的卧榻，一阵痛楚涌了上来。她扑在枕上呜咽；可灵魂在那里也寻不到安宁，她又站起来，把一把椅子拖到炉前坐下，双手掩面，任凭愁思绵绵、幽梦沉沉。

还有什么悲哀，大得过心同自己的信念交战？——当一个女人再也无法对自己隐瞒：她爱情的偶像、她在上帝之外最爱的人，终究不过一个平平常常的凡夫？——她爱得那样高贵的人，竟只配得她的轻蔑？可她偏偏不能不爱他！她觉得该恨他，却又受不了失去他的念头。她甘愿为他死，而死的机会偏偏不来。

一扇没上锁的门把她和他隔开。不过几步之遥。多近，又多远！她听得见他的叹息。离被他伤得那样深的人这么近，他同样睡不着。走完这几步、相偎相守、慰彼此的渴念，该是何等幸福！可和解是不可能的了；她的心向往它，又躲避它，同一刻里，又爱又恨。

哦！为什么我们忘不掉过去？为什么悲伤偏偏不能不悲伤？

夫人思前想后，昏昏欲梦。

恍惚间，她像是在幻象里同丈夫说话——

“你自己说过，我们该趁还相爱的时候分开，趁决裂还会叫心流血的时候分开。那你为什么不做？为什么望着我叹息？为什么吻我？那些叹息、那些吻，对我全是酷刑；它们扎着我的心。分开吧！那是你自己许的愿。”

炉里的火快烬了；通红的余火上，一缕惨白渐弱的火苗有气无力地摇摆，像一段熄灭的热情剩下的最后一个念头。四下里愈来愈暗；垂灭的余烬，勉强照出那位哀伤夫人的身影——她坐在那里，头埋在手里，像一座守墓哀悼的大理石像。

忽然，在夜的寂静和她自己思绪的寂静里，她仿佛听见窃窃的人语和蹑蹑的脚步，正朝凉阁的门逼近。

===Original===

Lady Banfi really did hear these sounds; but she was like one but half-awakened from his first sleep, who hears but heeds not, who knows what is going on about him without regarding it.

The whispering was now audible close beneath the windows, and now and then it seemed to her as if the smothered clash of arms was mingling with it. In her dreamy state the lady fancied she had got up and bolted the door; but this was a delusion, the door remained ajar.

Then someone pressed the latch, and the creaking sound made Lady Banfi dream that her husband had come to her, and was speaking to her in a tearful, supplicating voice. She felt the terrors of nightmare strong upon her as she came within the magnetic influence of that shape. “Let us part, Banfi!” she would have said, but the words died away on her lips. Then the dream-shape whispered to her﻿—“I am not Banfi, but the headsman!” and seized her hand.

At this cold touch Lady Banfi uttered a shriek and started up.

Two men stood before her with drawn swords. The lady looked into their faces with a shudder. Both were well known to her. One was Caspar Kornis, chief captain of the Maros district, the other John Daczo, chief captain of Csik, who now stood before her with menacing looks, and the points of their naked swords at her breast.

“Not a sound, my lady!” said Daczo grimly. “Where’s Banfi?”

The lady, thus scared out of her first sleep, was scarcely able to distinguish the objects around her: terror made her dumb.

Suddenly she observed through the open door that the passage was filled with armed men, whereupon her presence of mind seemed instantly and completely to return. She grasped at once the tremendous significance of the moment, and when Daczo, gnashing his teeth, again asked her where Banfi was, she bounded from her chair, ran to the door which separated her husband’s chamber from her own, turned the key quickly round, and screamed with all her might﻿—

“Banfi! Save yourself! They seek your life!”

Daczo ran forward to stop her mouth and snatch the key from her; but with singular presence of mind Lady Banfi had, in the meantime, thrown the key into the heart of the red-hot embers, and cried again﻿—

“Fly, Banfi! Your enemies are here!”

===Chinese===

班菲夫人确实听见了这些声响；可她像头一觉睡得半醒的人，听见了却不在意，知道身边在发生什么，却不当回事。

窃语声已经到了窗根底下，其间她还不时仿佛听见兵器的暗响。在迷离状态中，夫人恍惚以为自己起身闩了门；那只是错觉，门虚掩着。

接着有人按动了门闩；那吱呀一声，让班菲夫人梦见丈夫来了，正含泪哀求地同她说话。那形影的磁力罩住了她，梦魇的恐怖攫紧了她。“分开吧，班菲！”她想说，话却在唇边消散了。梦影对她耳语——“我不是班菲，是刽子手！”一把攥住了她的手。

这冰冷的触碰让班菲夫人尖叫一声，惊跳起来。

两个人持剑立在她面前。夫人颤抖着看他们的脸。两个她都认得：一个是马罗什（Maros）地区的都尉卡斯帕·科尔尼斯（Caspar Kornis），一个是奇克（Csik）的都尉约翰·达佐（John Daczo）；此刻他们面目狰狞地立在她跟前，出鞘的剑尖直指她的胸口。

“不许出声，夫人！”达佐阴狠地说，“班菲在哪儿？”

夫人从初睡中被吓起，眼前的物事都分辨不清：恐怖叫她哑了。

突然，她从敞开的门里看见过道里挤满了武装的人——她神智霎时全回来了。她一下子明白了这一刻的千钧分量；达佐咬牙切齿再问班菲在哪儿时，她从椅子上蹦起，冲到隔开丈夫房间与自己房间的那扇门前，飞快旋动钥匙锁了门，用尽全身力气尖叫——

“班菲！快逃！他们要你的命！”

达佐抢上来要捐她的嘴、夺钥匙；班菲夫人却以非凡的镇定，早把钥匙扔进了通红炭火的心窝，又叫道——

“快跑，班菲！你的仇人来了！”

===Original===

Daczo tried to pick the key out of the fire, and burnt his fingers very badly in the attempt, whereupon, still more furious, he rushed upon the lady sword in hand to cut her down, but Kornis held him back.

“Softly, sir! We have no orders to kill the woman, nor would it be worthy of us; let us try rather to burst open the door as quickly as possible,” and with that they both pressed their shoulders against the door, Daczo cursing and swearing, and calling upon all the devils in hell to help him, while Lady Banfi on her knees prayed God to allow her husband to escape.

---

Banfi had gone to sleep at the same time as his wife. He too had had a tormenting dream. He fancied he was in prison, and the moment he heard Margaret’s shriek, he sprang in terror from his couch, tore open the window of the pavilion, and without thinking what he was doing, leaped into the garden at a single bound. He looked hurriedly about him. The house was surrounded by armed Szeklers, and the rear of the garden was bounded by a broad ditch filled with greenish rainwater. Amongst the masses of infantry stood here and there a group of grooms, holding by the bridles the chargers from which their masters had just dismounted.

Banfi had very little time for reflection, nor did he need much. Under cover of the darkness, he rushed swiftly upon the nearest groom, gave him a buffet which brought the blood in streams from his nose and mouth, sprang upon one of the vacant horses, and struck the spurs into its flanks.

===Chinese===

达佐伸手往火里捞钥匙，指头烫得够呛；他愈发狂怒，提剑就要砍那夫人，被科尔尼斯拦住。

“轻点儿，先生！没人叫我们杀这女人，那也有失我们的身份；还是想法快些把门撞开。”说罢两人一齐用肩膀撞门；达佐咒天骂地，把地狱里的魔鬼统统喊来帮忙；班菲夫人跪在地上，求上帝放她丈夫一条生路。

---

班菲和妻子同时入睡。他也做了个受折磨的梦，梦见自己身陷囹圄；一听见玛格丽特的尖叫，他惊得从榻上弹起，扯开凉阁的窗，想也没想自己在干什么，一个箭步跳进了花园。

他慌忙四顾。房子被武装的塞克勒人（Szekler）围了，花园后头是一道宽沟，积着发绿的雨水。步兵群里，东一处西一处站着几伙马夫，牵着缰绳，拉住主人刚下的大马。

班菲容不得多想，也不需多想。借着夜色掩护，他疾扑向最近的一个马夫，一拳打得那人口鼻窜血，纵身跳上一匹空着的战马，马刺狠狠刺进马腹。

===Original===

The cry of the groom, who had fallen beneath the horse but still held on fast by the bridle, brought up to the spot a crowd of yelling Szeklers. It immediately occurred to Banfi to put his hands into his saddle-pouches, where pistols were sure to be found, and the moment he felt the handles, he as quick as light sent two shots among the crowd which was pressing upon him from all sides, and taking advantage of the consequent hubbub and confusion, spurred his horse fiercely, till it reared and plunged and flew away with him through the garden. The groom still stuck to it like a leech, and allowed himself to be dragged along the ground, till at last his head came into collision with the stump of a tree and he fell back unconscious. Banfi thereupon galloped towards the ditch, and leaped it at a single bold bound; his pursuers, not daring to follow him that way, were obliged to make a long detour to reach the gates, thus giving Banfi a start of several hundred paces. His steed too, scared by the noise of the pursuit, had become half frantic, and Banfi gave him his head, and away they went over stock and stone, up hill and down dale, without aim or purpose.

---

“Oh, accursed woman!” roared Daczo, threatening Lady Banfi with his fists, when he learnt that Banfi had made his escape. “ ’Tis all through you that Banfi has slipped through our fingers.”

“Oh, Almighty God! I thank Thee!” stammered Margaret, with hands upraised to heaven.

The Szeklers, enraged at having let the husband escape, swung their weapons and rushed upon the wife to murder her.

“Let her die! Her blood be upon her own head!” they roared, with bestial rage.

“Kill me! Death will be welcome to me!” cried Margaret, kneeling down before them. “To die for him was my only wish. God be with me!”

“Be off with you!” cried Kornis, suddenly intervening, beating down the weapons of the Szeklers with his sword, and covering the kneeling lady with his body. “Shame on you! Would you kill a woman? Ye are worse than the Pagan Tartars. If you’ve let Banfi escape, run after him.”

“We’ll kill her! We’ll kill her!” bellowed the Szeklers, and again they attempted to tear Kornis away from the lady.

“Eh! you damned beasts! Who commands here, I should like to know? Am I not your captain?”

===Chinese===

那马夫跌倒在马蹄下，仍死死拽着缰绳不放；他的嚎叫引来一群呐喊的塞克勒人。

班菲立刻想到去摸鞍囊——里面准有手枪；枪柄一入手，他眼疾手快朝四面逼近的人群就是两枪，趁着掀起的大乱，狠命加刺，那马人立暴跳，驮着他飞也似的穿过花园。那马夫还是蚂蟥似的不撒手，任凭在地上拖，直到脑袋撞上树桩，昏死过去。

班菲随即奔向那道沟，一个大胆的飞跃跨了过去；追兵不敢照走，只好绕远路赶大门，这一下让班菲先跑出去几百步。坐骑也被追赶的声浪惊得半疯；班菲索性松开缰，任它驮着自己越障跨石、翻山过谷，漫无目的地狂奔。

---

“哦，你这该咒的女人！”听说班菲逃了，达佐朝班菲夫人挥着拳头咆哮，“全坏在你手里，班菲才从我们指缝里溜了！”

“哦，全能的上帝！我感谢你！”玛格丽特双手向天，喃喃道。

塞克勒人放跑了丈夫，恼羞成怒，抡起兵器就扑向妻子要杀她。

“叫她死！她的血算在她自己头上！”他们兽性大发地吼。

“杀吧！死对我才是求之不得！”玛格丽特朝他们跪下，喊道，“为他而死，是我唯一的愿望。上帝与我同在！”

“都给我住手！”科尔尼斯突然插进来，用剑拨开塞克勒人的兵器，用身子护住跪着的夫人，“不要脸！你们要杀一个女人？你们比异教鞑靼人还狠。放跑了班菲，就追班菲去！”

“杀了她！杀了她！”塞克勒人吼着，又来撕扯科尔尼斯，要把他从夫人身边拖开。

“嘿！你们这些遭瘟的畜生！这里谁说了算，啊？我不是你们的都尉吗？”

===Original===

“No!” bluntly replied a stiff-necked, bullheaded Szekler, twitching his bulky shoulders to and fro. “Our captain is Nicholas Bethlen, and he is not here.”

“Then go and find him. But let me tell you that whoever does not instantly quit this room shall be beaten into a pulp.”

Still the Szeklers persisted in remaining, and there is no knowing what they might not have done, had not one of the hindermost suddenly exclaimed﻿—

“Let us go to Bonczhida!”

Thereupon all the others fell a-shouting﻿—“To Bonczhida! to Bonczhida!” and they withdrew, cursing horribly, and in the most chaotic confusion.

But Captain Kornis quietly put Lady Banfi into a carriage, and sent her to Bethlen Castle, which then belonged to Paul Beldi, hoping that Banfi would behave with a little more discretion when he heard that his wife was a prisoner.

---

Meanwhile, the Szekler rabble sent out against Banfi by order of the Prince had arrived at Bonczhida, and on showing the castellan the Prince’s mandate, the gates were opened to them without the slightest contradiction. Daczo only left a portion of his band there, whom he strictly charged to arrest Banfi the moment he appeared, then with the rest he went on to Örmenyes, where Banfi had another castle, to seek him there.
