The Szeklers left behind at Bonczhida no sooner perceived themselves captainless, than they proceeded to make themselves perfectly at home in the occupied castle. At first indeed they only jostled each other in the hall and vestibules, but presently they began to insist that the private apartments should also be thrown open to them.

The castellan hesitated. He declared that there was no necessity for such a step, and begged the noble gentlemen to keep within their legal rights, whereupon the before-mentioned broad-shouldered, bullheaded rogue stepped forth, twirled his blonde moustache, which consisted of about nine hairs, and thrusting his pockmarked face close under the castellan’s nose, exclaimed﻿—

“What do you mean by that? You are a conspirator! You have robber-bands concealed in those rooms. Open the doors instantly, or we’ll burn the house down!”

The castellan was very wroth, but he was also very frightened, so he threw open the rooms in order that the Szeklers might see with their own eyes that nobody was concealed there.

===Chinese===

“不是！”一个死心眼儿的牛脖子塞克勒人生硬地答，肥大的肩膀一耸一耸，“我们的都尉是尼古拉斯·贝特伦（Nicholas Bethlen），他不在这儿。”

“那就找他去。可我把话放这儿：谁不立刻滚出这间屋，就揍他个稀烂。”

塞克勒人还是赖着不走；谁知会不会闹出什么来，亏得后头有个人突然喊了一声——

“去邦奇达！”

于是众人都跟着喊——“去邦奇达！去邦奇达！”他们骂骂咧咧、乱乱哄哄地退了出去。

科尔尼斯都尉却悄悄把班菲夫人扶上一辆马车，送去当时归属保罗·贝尔迪（Paul Beldi）的贝特伦城堡（Bethlen Castle），指望班菲听说妻子被囚，行事会收敛些。

---

与此同时，奉亲王之命捉拿班菲的那群塞克勒乌合之众已到邦奇达；向城守一亮亲王手令，城门毫无二话地开了。达佐只留下一部分人在邦奇达，严令班菲一露面就拿下；自己带着其余人继续开往厄尔梅涅什（Örmenyes）——班菲在那里还有一座城堡——去那里搜他。

留在邦奇达的塞克勒人一见没了头目，立刻在这座占了的地堡里大大方方当起了主人。起初他们还只在大厅和门厅里挤来挤去，不久便非要内宅也向他们敞开不可。

城守迟疑。他声称没必要走到这一步，求诸位贵绅士守住法度。这时，前头说的那个宽肩膀的牛脖子无赖站了出来，捻着他那约莫九根毛的黄胡子，把一张麻子脸直杵到城守鼻子底下，嚷道——

“你这是什么意思？你是个同谋！你那几间屋里藏着匪帮。立刻开门，不然我们烧了这宅子！”

城守又怒又怕，只好把房间统统敞开，好叫塞克勒人亲眼看看里面没藏人。

===Original===

The Szeklers thereupon, with astonishing conscientiousness, thoroughly explored every hole and corner, even looking into places where no one would ever have thought of hiding anything. They looked under and inside all the beds. They pulled out all the cupboards. They took the grates out bodily to see what was behind them. They pitched all the books out of the bookcases, and, after ransacking every room, came at last to Lady Banfi’s bedchamber.

“Look! look! There sits Banfi!” cried the bullheaded ringleader, recoiling at first before a lifelike portrait of the Baron, but immediately afterwards rushing forward and gouging out one of its eyes with his spear. “And that pretty lady yonder is his wife, I suppose?” asked he, pointing to another portrait by the side of the first. “Ai, ai, ai! We were like to have killed her a little while ago, not knowing that she was so pretty. Let us be off, comrades! This room we must leave untouched, for it belongs to that pretty lady,” and with that he drove his comrades out, and wrote with a piece of charcoal on the white enamelled door, in letters each an ell long﻿—“**This is the pretty lady’s chamber.**”

“Why do you do that?” asked the castellan in some surprise.

“To prevent any fuddled blockhead from thrusting his nose in there, in case we all get drunk.”

“But where will you all get the drink from, pray?” asked the castellan, more and more amazed.

“Nay, gossip! we must certainly have a peep at the cellars also, to see if anybody is lurking there.”

“There you cannot go, and so I tell you once for all, unless you have brought petards with you under your coats of mail.”

“What! Just say that again! I should like to hear it once more. Do you know, gossip, to whom you are speaking? My name is Firi Firtos, and if you speak a single word more, I’ll chuck you over the house, so that you will fall to the ground in half-a-dozen pieces.”

“Why bandy words with him?” cried a voice from the crowd. “Let us pitch the fellow out of the window.”

===Chinese===

于是塞克勒人以惊人的认真，把每个窟窿每条缝都搜了个遍，连谁也想不到能藏东西的地方都看了。他们看遍所有床底床里，拉出所有橱柜，把炉格子整个拆下来看后面有什么。书架上的书全给他们扔了出来；一间间翻遍之后，终于来到班菲夫人的寝卧。

“看！看！班菲坐在那儿！”牛脖子头目喊道，起先被男爵那张栩栩如生的画像唬得倒退一步，随即又冲上去，用长矛剜掉了画像的一只眼睛。“那边那位俊娘子，大概就是他老婆了？”他指着旁边另一幅画像问。“哎呀，哎呀，哎呀！方才差点儿把她杀了，谁知道她这么俊。弟兄们，撤！这间屋动不得，它是那位俊娘子的。”说着把同伴们都轰了出去，又拿一块炭，在白珐琅门上写下尺半长一个字的一行字——“**这是俊娘子的闺房。**”

“你这是干什么？”城守有些惊讶地问。

“省得待会儿大伙都喝多了，哪个醉糊涂的蠢货把鼻子伸进去。”

“可请问，你们的酒从哪儿来？”城守愈发惊奇地问。

“嗐，老哥！酒窖咱们自然也得瞧瞧，看看里头猫着人没有。”

“那儿你们去不得，我把话一次性说绝——除非你们铠甲底下藏了攻城炮。”

“什么！再说一遍！我倒想再听一遍。老哥，你知道你在同谁说话？我叫菲里·菲尔托什（Firi Firtos）；你再多说一个字，我就把你从房顶上扔过去，摔你个六零七散！”

“同他磨什么嘴皮子！”人群里有人喊，“把这小子扔出窗外去。”

===Original===

The Szeklers did not wait to be told twice, but instantly raised the castellan into the air and threw him, despite his frantic struggles, out of the window. Luckily he fell on his feet, and took to his heels, to the great indignation of Firi Firtos, who seized all the cactus and hortensia plants that stood in the windows, and hurled them after him, pots and all, after which the whole mob rushed bellowing down to the cellars. Finding it impossible to open the large iron doors, they dragged forward huge casks, filled them with big stones, and sent them flying down the cellar steps, till at last the iron doors fell in with a tremendous crash.

The vast cellar was fitted with huge butts and barrels of every size and shape, and the Szeklers forthwith fell upon them and knocked the tops off with their morning-stars to see what was inside them. The costly wine poured out into the cellar. The Szeklers drank as only Szeklers can drink, and what they could not drink was simply wasted.

When they had all drunk as much as they could hold, the mob stormed upstairs again, and while another batch took their place below, they forced their way into the staterooms, rolled about on the costly divans and oriental carpets, hustled one another against the furniture and mirrors, and indulged in many other like pleasantries. Firi Firtos climbed on to a round ebony table in order to paint a moustache on the portrait of a medieval lady with a piece of charcoal, but someone else jerked the table from under him, and the merry wag fell crashing down into a glass chest containing the family treasures. Mad with rage, he immediately began pitching about everything which came to hand: gorgeous gold pocals, silver plates, enamelled snuffboxes, flew one after another at the heads of the Szeklers, who, entering into the joke, flung them all back at him with great spirit.

===Chinese===

塞克勒人说干就干，立刻把城守举到半空，任他拼命挣扎，从窗户扔了出去。幸亏他脚先着地，撒腿就跑。菲里·菲尔托什气得不行，抄起窗台上所有仙人掌和绣球花，连盆带花朝他砸去；随后整群人吼叫着冲下酒窖。

大铁门打不开，他们便拖来几只大桶，装满大石头，顺着窖梯轰隆隆砸下去，终于把铁门撞了个稀烂。

大窖里立着大大小小、各式各样的酒桶酒池；塞克勒人立刻扑上去，捙狼牙棒敲开桶盖，看里头装的什么。名贵的美酒哗哗淌进窖里。塞克勒人放开量喝，喝出只有塞克勒人喝得出的喝法；喝不下的，就白白糟蹋。

人人灌到嗓子眼了，这伙人又呼啦啦杀回楼上；下面换另一批接着灌，他们则闯进正房大厅，在贵重的长榻和东方地毯上打滚，互相往家具和镜子上撞，诸如此类的雅事干了不少。菲里·菲尔托什爬上一张乌木圆桌，要拿炭给一幅中古贵妇的画像添两撇胡子；不想别人从底下把桌子一抽，这位快活的活宝一头栽进装着传家珍宝的玻璃柜里。他怒得发疯，抓起什么就扔什么：华美的金杯、银盘、珐琅鼻烟壶，接二连三朝塞克勒人头上飞去；那些人也来了兴致，又精神抖擞地统统扔回来。

===Original===

This was the signal for a general devastation. The mania for destruction is contagious. It needs but one to begin it, and the mob, as if rejoicing at the sight, is never so ready as when there is something to be pulled, torn, or smashed to bits. In an instant every piece of furniture was broken up and every bit of tapestry torn down. Splendid costumes, costly, fur-trimmed pelisses, gala-mantles﻿—everything was torn to pieces. They ripped open the featherbeds, scattered the eiderdown out of the windows, and bellowed to those who stood below﻿—“It is snowing! it is snowing!” whereupon all the others came rushing up to tear and pull to pieces what still remained whole.

They pulled up the fragrant jasmines by the roots to make posies of them, and cut up into neckties the delicate tapestries which Lady Banfi had worked with her own hands. Stealing gave the Szeklers no pleasure, it was destruction for its own sake that they found so delightful. Thus they threw to the ground a rare and costly clock which needed winding only once a year, broke it up, distributed the wheels and chains as buckles for their shoes, and melted the silver keys into bullets, which they fired off into the air.

Here too it was edifying to see how Firi Firtos tried to get at the bottom of everything. He took down an antique urn and stuck it on his head upside down by way of a helmet. A clock chain he wound round his loins as a girdle, and he danced about hugging in his arms a huge statue of Gutenberg, declaring that it would make an excellent scarecrow for the Somlyo vineyards.

The fragments of the broken furniture they piled up on the hearth, and made a great fire of the priceless ebony, mahogany, and palisander woods. The conflagration of a whole village would not have been half so costly.

Over this fire they hung, on a silver chain, a Corinthian amphora of exquisite workmanship by way of a kettle, filled it with finely-chopped mutton, and sent Firi Firtos out for beans, salt, and onions. He brought them instead green coffee beans, white powdered sugar, and the most costly tulip, amaryllis, and hyacinth bulbs, all of which they threw pell-mell into the kettle, with the natural consequence that the mess, when finished, was very nearly the death of them all, and the end of it was that they pitched Firi Firtos neck and crop into the courtyard.

===Chinese===

这是全面洗劫的信号。破坏的疯狂是会传染的。只需一个起头，人群便像见了喜色，一有东西可拉、可撕、可砸个粉碎，就再没有那样踊跃的。眨眼间，件件家具砸了个碎，幅幅挂毯扯了个下。华丽的衣裳、镶毛边的贵重皮氅、礼服大氅——统统撕成条条。他们豁开羽绒褥，把鸭绒从窗口扬出去，朝底下的人大吼——“下雪了！下雪了！”于是其余的人又一窝蜂涌上来，把还算囫囵的东西也撕的撕、拽的拽。

他们把芳香的茉莉连根拔去扎花束，把班菲夫人亲手织的精美挂毯裁成领巾。偷，塞克勒人并不觉得有趣；为毁而毁，那才叫其乐无穷。于是他们把一架一年只需上一次弦的珍稀贵钟掼在地上，砸开，把齿轮链条分了当鞋扣，银钥匙化成子弹，朝天放了个痛快。

菲里·菲尔托什那种凡事都探个底的精神，也着实令人开眼。他摘下一只古董瓮，倒扣在头上当头盔；一条钟链缠在腰里当腰带；又抱着一尊高大的古腾堡（Gutenberg）雕像跳舞，声称拿它给索姆廖的葡萄园当稻草人再好不过。

砸烂的家具残骸堆上壁炉，无价的乌木、红木、花梨木烧起一大堆火。烧掉整座村子也不及这一把火值钱。

火上用银链吊起一只做工精美的科林斯双耳陶罐权当汤锅，罐里炖上剁碎的羊肉，又打发菲里·菲尔托什出去找豆子、盐和洋葱。他弄回来的却是生咖啡豆、白砂糖，和顶名贵的郁金香、孤挺花、风信子的球茎；众人生怕不齐似的，一股脑全倒进罐里。后果可想而知：那锅东西炖成之后，差点把大伙全都送走；末了，众人捐住菲里·菲尔托什，把他连人带脖子扔进了院子。

===Original===

The Szekler, mad with rage and unable to obtain any other satisfaction, rushed down to the cellars to drink himself dead drunk, but there all the hogsheads had already been staved in, and he waded in wine up to his middle. Looking about him, he perceived a door leading to a second cellar, broke it open with his axe, and was overjoyed to see by the light of the torch he held in his hand, a whole row of fresh casks. He immediately rushed upon the first of them, and knocking the top in, held the torch over it to see what was flowing out. It was *gunpowder*! Luckily for him he was drunk, otherwise he would certainly have sent the castle and everything it contained the shortest way to heaven. “That’s not good to drink!” thought he, and broke open the second cask; in that too there was powder, and in the third also, and he swore a terrible oath that if the fourth held the same thing he would hurl the torch into it holus bolus. In the fourth cask, however, there was honey, and shake it as much as he would, he could get nothing else out of it. At last he came upon a six-gallon cask, and, smelling the bung, inhaled a strong odour of spirits, which made him madder than ever, and seizing it by the spigot he raised it bodily from the ground and swallowed long draughts of the strong corn brandy, till over he fell backwards, cask and all. There he wallowed about in the streaming honey; struggled laboriously to his feet again, stumbled a few steps further on, fell down into the gunpowder; rolled backwards and forwards in it for some time, and finally, all candied as he was, scrambled into the courtyard, and there the honey-and-powder-bedaubed form fell prone into the heaps of eiderdown which covered the ground, and sprawled helplessly about till he was covered with plumage from the crown of his head to the soles of his jackboots, and in this plight the grotesquely hideous creature crawled upstairs on all fours in amongst his carousing companions. The man no longer resembled any known beast of the Old or New Worlds. He was black and white all over: white where he was not black, and black where he was not white. Perhaps he had some distant resemblance to a polar bear with a hide of feathers instead of hair, but his roaring was like the roaring of a hippopotamus.

===Chinese===

这塞克勒人一腔邪火没处出，冲下酒窖想灌个烂醉；可桶全给凿穿了，他一脚踩进去，酒没到腰。

四下一望，见有扇门通第二窖，捙斧劈开，举着火把一照，好一整排原封的新桶，喜得他心花怒放。他扑向头一桶，敲开顶盖，火把伸过去看流出来的是什么。是*火药*！全亏他醉了，不然定然送这座城堡和里面的万物走了去天堂的捷径。“这个不好喝！”他想，又凿开第二桶；也是火药；第三桶还是。他狠狠赌咒：第四桶要还是这玩意儿，就把火把整根扔进去。

不料第四桶里装的是蜜；任他怎么摇，再摇不出别的来。最后他撞见一只六加仑的小桶，凑着桶塞一闻，一股冲鼻的烧酒味，撩得他愈发疯了；他攥住桶嘴，整桶抱离地面，大口大口灌那烈性的粮食白兰地，直到仰面朝天，连人带桶翻倒。

他就在淌了一地的蜜里打滚；费力爬起来，踉跄又走几步，一头栽进火药堆；前后滚了好一阵，最后，浑身挂满糖霜似的蜜壳，手脚并用爬进院子；那蜜和火药糊成的躯体，扑通趴进满地的鸭绒堆，无助地扑腾，直到从头到脚、连长统靴底都挂满了羽绒。就这样，这奇形怪状的怪物四肢着地，爬上楼，钻回他那些正喝得痛快的伙伴中间。

这人已经不像新旧大陆任何一种已知的兽类。他通身黑白：不黑的地方白，不白的地方黑。硬要说，倒有几分像一头长羽毛不长毛的北极熊；只是那吼声，像河马。

===Original===

It is therefore not surprising that when the Szeklers beheld this strange monster crawling towards them on all fours and bellowing loudly, they should take to their heels in terror, scatter to all points of the compass, and leave the flesh-filled kettle in the lurch. Most of them took the shortest but most dangerous way out of the window, exclaiming﻿—“That is Banfi’s devil! Here comes Banfi’s devil!”

The Szekler, perceiving the success of his involuntary masquerade, sent after the fugitives a still more ghastly howl, took the amphora down from the chain, sat down with it in the middle of the parquetted floor, thrust both hands into it at once like a demon of the woods, and gobbled and roared alternately.

And these savage scenes took place in the very same chamber where, only a few days before, the delicate form of Dame Banfi had appeared among her jasmines and mimosas like a melancholy shade from fairyland which only listens with its soul and speaks with its eyes.

---

Meanwhile Denis Banfi, after breaking through the ambush laid for him at Koppad, began, as the noise of the pursuit gradually died away, to look about him in the star-bright night, and picked his way so carefully through woods and over stubble-fields that, at dawn of day, he saw before him the towers of Klausenburg.

Once rid of the terrors of pursuit, anger and revenge began to rage within him. He thought at first that this night attack was simply an audacious conspiracy of his private enemies concocted without the knowledge of the Prince, on the principle that an accomplished act is more easily justifiable than an act that has still to be accomplished. But the attempt had not succeeded, and the escaped lion had both the will and the power to turn upon his pursuers and teach them respect for the laws.

In the plain before the town Banfi’s troops were just going through their morning exercises when their leader came galloping up to them, pale, agitated, unarmed, and without either hat or mantle. His captains hastened towards him, aghast and curious.

“I’ve just escaped from a murderous assault,” said Banfi, with a hoarse voice and a heaving breast; “my enemies have treacherously fallen upon me. I have escaped them, but my wife is in their hands. I recognized the voices of Daczo and Kornis among my pursuers.”

“Yes, and Daczo’s name is embroidered on this saddlecloth,” said Michael Angel.

===Chinese===

因此毫不奇怪：塞克勒人一见这怪物四肢着地、大声吼着朝自己爬来，无不吓得抱头鼠窜，四散八方，连那锅炖肉都顾不得了。多数人拣了最短却最险的路——跳窗，一边喊——“那是班菲的魔鬼！班菲的魔鬼来了！”

那塞克勒人见自己这场无心插柳的化装舞会大获全胜，朝逃散的人追发一声更凄厉的嚎，把双耳罐从银链上摘下，抱到镶木地板正中坐下，双手一齐插进罐里，活像林中恶魔，一口肉一口吼，交替不歇。

而这一场场野蛮的闹剧，恰恰上演在那间屋里——几天前，班菲夫人纤秀的身影还置身在她的茉莉和含羞草之间，宛如仙界一位忧郁的精魂，只用灵魂倾听，只用眼睛说话。

---

与此同时，德尼斯·班菲（Denis Banfi）冲破罗帕德为他设下的埋伏，待追兵的声浪渐渐消歇，便在星光灿亮的夜里辨认方向，穿树林、过茬地，走得那样小心，以致天色破晓时，克劳森堡的塔楼已在他眼前。

追捕的恐惧一去，愤怒和报复就在他胸中翻腾起来。起初他以为，这场夜袭不过是私敌们背着亲王搞的胆大包天的阴谋——奉行的是“生米煮成熟饭，事后好辩解”的信条。可这一击没有得手，逃出来的雄狮既有心也有力，回头咬向追兵，教他们懂得敬畏法律。

城外平原上，班菲的部队正在出早操，忽见主帅飞马而来——面色苍白，心绪激荡，赤手空拳，帽子大氅都没有。队长们又惊又疑，连忙迎上去。

“我刚从一场谋杀里逃出来，”班菲嗓音嘶哑，胸膛起伏，“我的仇人背信弃义地扑了我。我脱了身，可我妻子落在他们手里。追我的人里，我听出了达佐和科尔尼斯的嗓音。”

“不错，这块鞍鞯上还绣着达佐的名字呢。”米哈伊·安吉尔说。

===Original===

Banfi appeared much disturbed. His face was dark and troubled, as if neither the future nor the past was quite clear to him.

“I don’t understand it at all,” said he to his captains. “If the attack was by the Prince’s command, I ought to have been served beforehand with a writ, a citation, or, at the very least, a notice of judgment. If however it be only an act of private vengeance, my band is more than sufficient to reach these honest Szeklers. In any case, you will remain under arms before the town while I go up to my castle. In a few hours I shall know whither we have to turn.”

Thereupon Banfi rode into the town, accompanied by Michael Angel. As he turned the corner of his palace, he was obliged to pass over the ground where the house of Dame Saint Pauli had formerly stood. All that remained of it now was a large stone, and Banfi, chancing to look in that direction, saw the mistress of the vanished house sitting on that single stone, and evidently awaiting him. He turned impatiently away, but she arose, curtseyed low, and cried derisively﻿—

“Good morning, your Excellency! Good morning!”

Banfi haughtily rode on without a word. At the palace gate the castellan of Bonczhida awaited him, who, after escaping from the violence of the Szeklers, had discreetly kept his evil tidings secret, and now told his lord, in a hurried whisper, that his castle had been turned upside down, and the Szeklers were making merry there to their hearts’ content.

Banfi answered not a syllable, but he sent for his armour and his charger, and calmly got ready to depart.

“Your lordship would do well to hasten,” said the castellan; “by this time the Szeklers must have penetrated into the state apartments.”

“It is well,” replied Banfi, walking up and down the room with folded arms.

“No, my lord; it is not well. They have smashed to pieces everything in the rooms, torn the carpets to shreds, divided among them the curiosities, flooded the cellars with wine, and even made away with the horses.”

“It does not matter,” replied the magnate hoarsely. What cared he at that moment for his costliest treasures, his wine, his horses?

“They have done still worse, my lord. They forced their way into her ladyship’s bedroom, set up the bust of her ladyship as a target, and mutilated it horribly amidst peals of laughter.”

===Chinese===

班菲显得六神无主。他脸色阴沉烦乱，仿佛无论前路还是来路，在他眼里都已模糊不清。

“我全然摸不着头脑，”他对队长们说，“这袭击若是奉亲王之命，事先总该有传票、传唤，至少也该有一纸判决通知送到我手上。若只是私仇报复，我这一旅人马收拾这伙‘老实’塞克勒人绰绰有余。无论如何，你们持械在城外待命，我上城堡去一趟。几个钟头之内，我就知道咱们该往哪儿开。”

于是班菲在米哈伊·安吉尔陪同下进城。转过府邸的街角，他必须经过圣保利夫人宅子从前所在的那块地。如今只剩一块大石头。班菲无意间朝那边一瞥，看见那座消失了宅子的女主人正坐在那块孤石上，分明是在等他。

他不耐烦地扭过头去；她却站起身，深深一福，讥诮地喊——

“早安，大人！早安！”

班菲傲然策马而过，一言不发。府门前，邦奇达的城守正候着他——他从塞克勒人手里逃出后，乖觉地把凶信括到现在，此刻才急急地附耳禀告老爷：城堡已被翻了个底朝天，塞克勒人正在里面大闹尽兴。

班菲一个字的回话也没有，只叫人取甲、牵马，静静收拾动身。

“老爷还是快些好，”城守说，“这会儿塞克勒人怕是已经打进正厅了。”

“知道了。”班菲答道，抱臂在屋里踱来踱去。

“不，老爷；不好。他们把屋里的东西砸了个精光，地毯斯成碎片，古玩分赃分光，酒窖灌满了酒，连马都牵走了。”

“不打紧。”大贵族（magnate）哑着嗓子答。此刻他哪还顾得上什么至宝、美酒、骏马？

“还有更糟的，老爷。他们闯进了夫人的卧房，把夫人的半身像立起来当靶子，哄笑声中糟践得不成样子。”

===Original===

“What! My wife’s bust?” cried Banfi, putting his hand to his sword. “My wife’s bust did you say?” repeated he with sparkling eyes. “Ha!” he roared, and tearing his sword from its sheath, raised his face to heaven with an expression which no one had ever seen there before. It was like the face of a furious tiger chained down by force, with bloodshot eyes, thick starting veins in the forehead, and lips thirsting after blood. “God be gracious and merciful to them!” cried he, with a terrible voice, threw himself upon his horse, and hastened to his host.

“My friends!” cried he, ere yet he had had time to marshal their ranks. “A marauding swarm of hornets has fallen upon my castle and plundered it. They have smashed everything in my rooms, emptied my stables, stolen or destroyed my family treasures. All that troubles me little. Let the half-starved wretches eat and drink their fill! Let them keep what they have got! Let them rob, burn, and ravage if they will, poor devils! I am still the master of many mansions, and can pay off this beggarly Szekler crew out of one pocket. But they have defaced the image of my wife!﻿—my wife I say! Therefore will I take vengeance upon them, a fearful vengeance. Follow me! The trees of the orchards of Bonczhida have not borne fruit for a long time. We will now hang fruit upon them ourselves!”

The enthusiastic shouts of the squadrons proved that the host was ready to follow Banfi whithersoever he might choose to lead it. The captains marshalled their divisions, and the second flourish of trumpets had already sounded, when a company of twelve horsemen suddenly appeared in front of Banfi’s host. In the foremost of this company they recognized the Prince’s herald, a broad-shouldered man of gigantic stature, who boldly rode up to Banfi and his staff, and raising his escutcheoned bâton, cried﻿—“Halt!”

“Use your eyes! We *are* halting!” retorted Michael Angel.

“In the name of his Highness, the Prince, I cite you, Denis Banfi, to appear within three days before the Privy Council at Karoly-Fehervár, there to defend yourself as best you may against the charges brought against you. Till then your consort remains in our hands as a hostage for your good behaviour.”

“We *are* coming,” retorted Michael Angel; “don’t you see that we are already about to start? We only wanted to know whither, and now we know it.”

===Chinese===

“什么！我妻子的像？”班菲喝道，手按剑柄。“你说的是我妻子的像？”他眼里灼灼放光，又问了一遍。“哈！”他大吼一声，咻地拔剑出鞘，仰面朝天，脸上是一种谁也没见过的神情。那像一头被强行锁住的怒虎的脸：眼睛血红，额上青筋暴绽，嘴唇渴着血。

“上帝宽恕他们，怜悯他们吧！”他用骇人的声音喊道，翻身上马，奔向他的部队。

“弟兄们！”他还没来得及整队，就喊了起来，“一伙流窜的马蜂扑到我城堡里劫掠。他们砸了我屋中的一切，掏空了我的马厩，偷的偷、毁的毁我的传家之宝。这些都算不得什么。让那群半饿死的可怜虫吃饱喝足！让他们留着拿走的！他们要抢要烧要糟蹋，由他们去，可怜虫们！我名下还有的是宅子，收拾这帮叫花子塞克勒人，一个口袋的财也够了。可他们毁了我妻子的像！——我说的是我妻子！所以我要报仇，狠狠地报仇。跟我来！邦奇达果园里的树，好久没结过果子了。咱们这就亲手去给它们挂果！”

各队爆出震天的呐喊——全军愿意跟随班菲，他指哪儿打哪儿。队长们正部署各部，第二通号已经吹响，忽有一队十二骑出现在班菲全军面前。当先一位，众人认出是亲王的传令官，一个肩膀宽阔、身躯魁伟的汉子；他大胆驰到班菲及其幕僚跟前，举起饰着纹章的权杖，喝道——“止步！”

“睁眼看看！我们*本来*就止着步！”米哈伊·安吉尔顶了回去。

“奉亲王殿下之名，传你德尼斯·班菲，三日内到卡罗利-费海尔瓦尔（Karoly-Fehervár）的枢密院（Privy Council）听审，就所控各款，自行为自己辩护。在此之前，你的妻子留在我们手中，作为你安分守己的人质。”

“我们*正要去*，”米哈伊·安吉尔又顶了一句，“没瞧见我们都要开拔了吗？就差不知道上哪儿——现在知道了。”

===Original===

“Silence, captain!” cried Banfi; “one must not jest with the Prince’s ambassador.”

The herald next turned to the captains.

“This citation does not concern you. I have a very different message to deliver to you in the Prince’s name.”

“You had better keep your message to yourself, or I’ll speak a word in your ears which will make them tingle,” jeered one of the captains, aiming at the herald with his pistol.

“Down with your weapons,” exclaimed Banfi; “let him proclaim the Prince’s mandate. Give him room that he may speak freely.”

The herald rose in his stirrups, and looking along the ranks cried aloud﻿—

“The Prince forbids you from henceforth to obey Banfi! Whoever takes up weapons for him is a traitor!”

“You’re a traitor yourself,” roared Michael Angel, and the next moment the crowd fell furiously upon the herald, with loud cries of “Kill him! kill him!” A hundred blades flashed simultaneously over his head.

“Hold!” cried Banfi in a voice of thunder, covering the herald with his body; “this man’s person is sacred and inviolable. To your places! Sheathe your swords! I﻿—your leader﻿—command it!”

“Eljen! eljen!” roared the brigades, and at the word of command they fell back into their places and stood there like an iron wall.

“You will not be very angry with me,” said Banfi to the herald, who had suddenly turned deadly pale, “you will not be very angry with me, I hope, for making them obey me this once? Go back to the Prince and tell him that in three days I will appear before him.”

“And tell him that we will be there too,” cried the captains in chorus.

The herald and his suite withdrew. Banfi moodily bent his head.

The third flourish of trumpets had already sounded, and the banners were all unfurled; but Banfi still continued staring blankly, darkly, dumbly before him.

“Draw your sword, my lord!” cried Angel; “place yourself at our head, and let us start. First to Bonczhida and then to Fehervár.”

“What do you say?” said Banfi, with a start. “What is it?”

“I say that if the law of the sword is to try you, the sword must also be your defence.”

“And such a process is generally called *civil war*!”

“We have not kindled it.”

“Nor will we fan it. ’Tis no longer, I see, a struggle against my personal enemies, but against the Prince, and he is the head of the land.”

===Chinese===

“住口，队长！”班菲喝道，“对亲王的使臣不得放肆。”

传令官随即转向众队长。

“这道传票与你们无干。我奉亲王之名，另有话传给你们。”

“你那话还是自己留着吧，不然我朝你耳朵里送一句，叫它烧得慌。”一名队长讥笑着，拿手枪瞄住传令官。

“放下武器，”班菲喝道，“让他宣读亲王的敕令。给他腾出地方，让他把话说畅。”

传令官立在马镫上，目光扫过队列，高声宣道——

“亲王敕令：自今而后，尔等不得再听命于班菲！谁为他拿起武器，谁就是叛逆！”

“你才是叛逆！”米哈伊·安吉尔怒吼。下一刻，人群狂怒地扑向传令官，“杀了他！杀了他！”喊声四起，百剑齐挥，在他头顶闪光。

“住手！”班菲雷鸣般大喝，用身子护住传令官，“此人神圣不可侵犯。各归其位！剑入鞘！我——你们的主帅——命令你们！”

“万岁！万岁！（Eljen!）”各旅怒吼。一声令下，他们退回原位，站定如一堵铁墙。

“你不会太见怪吧，”班菲对传令官说——他早已面无人色，“我希望，你不会太见怪我让他们服从了我这一次？回去禀告亲王：三天之内，我必到案。”

“也告诉他，我们也会到场。”队长们齐声喊。

传令官一行退走了。班菲闷闷地垂下头。

第三通号已经吹响，旌旗尽展；班菲却依旧直愣愣、阴沉沉、一言不发地望着前方。

“拔剑吧，大人！”安吉尔喊，“领着我们，开拔！先取邦奇达，再赴费海尔瓦尔。”

“你说什么？”班菲一惊，“什么？”

“我说，既然要用刀剑之法审你，刀剑也该做你的盾。”

“这样的官司，通常叫做*内战*！”

“火不是我们点的。”

“我们也决不扇它。我明白了，这已经不再是同我私敌的争斗，而是同亲王——而他，是这个国家的元首。”

===Original===

“And are not you its right arm? If they choose to light up the flames of civil war, we will not allow it to be quenched in your blood.”

“And why should my blood flow at all? Have I committed any capital offence? Can I even be charged with such a thing?”

“You are powerful, and that is a sufficient reason for killing you.”

“I care not. I’ll go, and what is more, alone. My wife is in their hands. They have the power to make me feel their wrath in the most painful way, and if there were no other reason for appearing, it is my knightly duty to release her.”

“You can save both her and yourself much more efficaciously by force of arms.”

“I have nothing to fear. I have done nothing for which I need blush in the sight of justice, and if they plot privily against me, are not you here? Summon hither my Somlyo troops as well, and only intervene if they practise foul play.”

“Oh, my lord! that army is good for nothing which is abandoned by its leader. Today it would go through fire and water for you, and is even ready to proclaim you Prince; but tomorrow, when it hears that you have appeared before the court, it will disperse and deny you.”

“They need know nothing of my resolution. I’ll immediately take coach and go to Fehervár. Tell the troops I’ve gone to Somlyo to collect my other forces, and keep them under arms till you hear from me.”

With that Banfi rode off to Klausenburg, and Michael Angel irritably stuck his sword into its sheath and told the troops that they might rest if they felt tired.

---

An hour later Banfi was rolling in a carriage-and-four towards Torda, on his way to Fehervár; a mounted servant led a spare horse after him by the bridle.

The further he withdrew from the seat of his power, the more anxious he became. His soul wavered. He began to see phantoms at every step. Only his pride prevented him from turning back again.

===Chinese===

“可你不正是这国家的右臂？他们既然存心点燃内战之火，我们就不能容它用你的血来浇灭。”

“凭什么就该流我的血？我犯过死罪么？这样的罪名，安得上我头上么？”

“你太有权势——要杀你，这一个理由就够了。”

“我不管。我去，而且是一个人去。我妻子在他们手里。他们有本事用最狠的法子叫我领教他们的怒气；就算再没有别的缘故，救她出来，也是我骑士的本分。”

“凭武力救她救你，都干脆得多。”

“我没什么可怕的。我没做过一件在法理面前脸红的事。他们若暗算我——你们不都在这儿？把我的索姆廖部队也调来；他们使阴的，你们再动手不迟。”

“唉，大人！被主帅撇下的军队，一钱不值。今天它肯为您赴汤蹈火，甚至愿意拥立您为亲王；可明天一听说您自己出庭去了，它就会散伙，翻脸不认您。”

“我用不着让他们知道我的决定。我立刻乘车去费海尔瓦尔。对部队就说我去索姆廖调集另一路人马；叫他们持械待命，等我的信。”

说罢，班菲纵马去了克劳森堡；米哈伊·安吉尔摃着火把剑捅回鞘里，对部队说：累了就歇着吧。

---

一个钟头后，班菲乘着四驾马车一路奔托尔达（Torda），取道去费海尔瓦尔；一名骑马的仆人牵着一匹副马跟在车后。

离他的权势之地越远，他心里越发慌。灵魂摇摇晃晃。他开始步步见鬼。全凭骄傲撑着，才没有掉头回去。

===Original===

Everything now wore a different aspect. He could read in the looks and salutations of all whom he met what they thought of him. A smile was a sign of compassion; a mere nod, a token of ill-will. He stopped to speak to everyone, even to very slight acquaintances, even to those whom he had hitherto looked down upon or had never regarded at all. He even condescended to question them. In the hour of misfortune it is wonderful how a man recollects all his acquaintances. At such a time he who once haughtily rejected the hand of friendship is ready to meet his very enemy halfway.

Suddenly he perceived an open carriage coming towards him from Torda, and in it sat a man wrapped up in a grey cloak, in whom, as he passed, Banfi recognized Martin Kuncz, the Unitarian bishop; he called to him to stop for a moment. The bishop, not hearing him for the clatter of the wheels, simply doffed his hat and drove on. Banfi thought he did it on purpose, and took it for a very bad omen. He who ordinarily treated all danger so lightly, now recoiled before the veriest bugbears. He stopped his carriage, and taking horse bade his coachman drive on to Torda and await him there. In the meantime he galloped after the bishop’s carriage, whereupon the bishop, catching sight of him, stopped and awaited the magnate, who cried to him from a distance﻿—

“So you will not answer when I speak to you, eh?”

“I am at your lordship’s command. I did not know that you wished to speak to me.”

“You know my situation, I suppose? What do you think of it? What ought I to do?”

“In such a case, my lord, it is as difficult to give advice as to take it.”

“I have resolved to appear to the citation.”

“Really, my lord?”

“I have nothing to fear. I feel that my cause is just.”

“No doubt; but it does not follow that you will get justice because your cause is just. In this world anything is possible.”

Banfi understood the allusion. He had formerly said the very same words to the bishop, and now he had not even sufficient strength of mind to leave him and go on his way defiantly; on the contrary, he dallied with him for some time longer.

“The Prince indeed is my enemy; but the Princess has always defended me, and I have every confidence in her Highness.”

“Yes; but unfortunately the Prince has quarrelled with his consort. They say that he even forbids her to enter his apartments.”

===Chinese===

如今一切都换了一副面孔。所遇之人的眼神和招呼，他都能读出对自己的看法：一笑是怜悯，一点头是恶意。

他逢人就停车搭话，连泛泛之交也不放过，连他一向瞧不起、从没正眼看过的也不放过，甚至屈尊向他们问长问短。人在倒霉的时候，忽然把三亲六故全都想起来，真是奇妙。这种时候，连当初傲然推开友谊之手的他，也肯迎着自己的仇家走上一半路了。

忽然，他望见一辆敞篷马车从托尔达迎面而来，车里坐着一个裹灰斗篷的人；两车交会时，班菲认出是一位论派主教马丁·孔茨（Martin Kuncz），便唤他稍停一停。车轮辚辚，主教没听见，只脱帽致意，径直过去了。班菲认定他是故意的，心里当作了极坏的兆头。

平常把一切凶险都不当回事的他，如今连最虚幻的稻草人也要退避三舍。他叫停马车，跨上马，吩咐车夫先赶去托尔达等他。他自己拍马追赶主教的马车。主教望见他，停下车等这位大贵族，只听他从远处喊——

“我同你说话，你竟不搭理，嗯？”

“听凭大人差遣。我不知道您有话对我说。”

“我的处境，你大概听说了？你怎么看？我该怎么办？”

“这种情形，大人，出主意难，听主意也一样难。”

“我已决意应传出庭。”

“当真，大人？”

“我无所畏惧。我知道我的事业是正义的。”

“无疑；可是事业正义，不等于就能讨到公道。在这个世上，什么事都可能。”

班菲听懂了这弦外之音——从前他自己对主教说过一模一样的话。此刻他甚至连拨马就走、扬长而去的定力都没有了，反倒又同主教磨蹭了好一阵。

“亲王固然是我的对头；可王妃一向护着我，我对殿下信得很。”

“是；可惜亲王同王妃闹翻了。听说，他连她的面都不许进他的寝宫。”

===Original===

This answer seemed to quite confound Banfi; but he had still one hope left.

“I don’t believe they’d dare to do me mischief, for they know that at Klausenburg and Somlyo I have armies in battle array which can call them to account at any moment.”

“Oh, my lord, it is difficult to direct an army from the walls of a prison, and you know very well that a live dog is stronger than a dead lion.”

These words seemed to produce a great change in Banfi. For a time he moodily rode by Kuncz’s carriage; then, after a long pause, he replied in a very low voice﻿—“You are right,” gave his horse the spur, and rode back to Klausenburg with the firm resolve of not allowing himself to be enticed from his stronghold.

On reaching the spot where scarcely six hours before he had restrained the enthusiastic ardour of his troops, he was much surprised to find a band of gypsies apparently searching for something on the ground.

“What are you doing here?” cried he, as he came up to them.

At this question their leader came forward, and recognizing Banfi, humbly doffed his cap.

“Verily, your Excellency, the gypsies have come hither to collect the cartridges which the brave and noble gentlemen have scattered about here.”

“But where then are the gentlemen?”

“Gone, your Excellency.”

“But why, and whither?”

“The moment they heard your lordship had quitted Klausenburg﻿—whew!﻿—they dispersed in all directions.”

“And Michael Angel?”

“He was the first to depart.”

Banfi felt sick and dizzy. The tears rushed to his eyes. To be so abandoned by everyone, by Fate, by his fellow-men, and even by his own self-confidence! What now remained of all his former might? Whither should he turn? What should he devise? Every way was closed against him. Neither with the sword of justice nor with the sword of battle could he fight. There was no hope and no refuge.

===Chinese===

这个回答把班菲彻底砸懵了；可他还剩一线指望。

“我不信他们敢下我的手。他们知道，克劳森堡和索姆廖，我有整装列阵的大军，随时能找他们算账。”

“唉，大人，隔着监狱的墙，调兵是难的；活狗强过死狮，这个道理您最明白不过。”

这几句话仿佛让班菲大变。他闷闷地伴着孔茨的马车走了一程；沉默良久，才低声答了句——“你说得对”——随即催马回头，直奔克劳森堡，铁了心不再让人把自己诱出老巢。

回到不到六个钟头前他按住全军热血的地方，他大为诧异地发现，一伙吉卜赛人正在地上像找什么东西。

“你们在这儿干什么？”他走近了喊。

听了这问，领头人上前，认出是班菲，谦卑地摘了帽。

“回大人的话，吉卜赛人到这儿来，是捡那些勇敢高贵的老爷们撒了一地的子弹壳。”

“那老爷们呢？”

“走光了，大人。”

“怎么走的？去哪儿了？”

“一听说大人您离了克劳森堡——呼！——四下里就散了个干净。”

“米哈伊·安吉尔呢？”

“他头一个走的。”

班菲只觉一阵昏眩，眼泪直涌上来。被所有人抛弃——被命运、被同类、甚至被自己的自信抛弃！他昔日的威权还剩下什么？该转向何方？该作何打算？条条路都对死了他。法理之剑不能仗，兵戈之剑也不能仗。没有指望，没有退路。

===Original===

His horse carried him whither it would. The magnate sat upon it with a darkened face, staring blankly at the clouds or on the ground. The earth, the sky, and his own heart﻿—everything within him and around him was dark and desolate. Hitherto his soul had been so full of pride that there was no room for anything else, and now all his pride was gone, and had left a hideous blank behind it. On, on he went; but it was his horse that chose the road. Vast forests lay before him, and he thought﻿—What lies beyond those forests? Lofty hills. And what beyond the hills? Still higher hills. And what then? The snowy peaks. And nowhere was there any refuge or shelter for him! So at the very first stroke everyone had fallen away from him, and he who only the day before had ruled over the half of Transylvania, and held fortresses at his disposal, cannot even find a hut to shelter him from the night. Or shall he give himself up to the derision of his enemies, and not even have the poor satisfaction of meeting death with front erect and a smiling countenance? Shall he perish ignobly like a hunted beast? He fell a-thinking. If die he must, he would at least die like a man. But how?

Gradually a thought began to dawn in his benighted soul, and with that thought the colour returned to his cheeks. Slowly he raised his head, and this secret thought ripening into a quick resolution, it was as though a voice within him cried﻿—“Yes! Thither! thither!” His eyes began to sparkle, he turned his horse’s head towards the forest, and disappeared beneath the thick foliage.

---

The tempest is raging. The storm snaps the trees. The rain patters down, and the swollen torrents roar. From time to time fitful lightning flashes illumine the whole region, and snowy mountain peaks grow dark and the black sky gleams white﻿—and again the sky darkens and the snowy peaks shine forth.

The scanty patches of brushwood clinging to the bald rocks are rudely torn and shaken by the hurricane, and the distant pine forests roar like the last trump. Every beast crouches trembling in its den and listens to the storm.

Lofty, inaccessibly steep rocks shut out the horizon, and far, far down in the vale below, like a toiling ant, we see a horseman struggling through the pathless wilderness.

God be merciful to him in such a night in such a place!

It is the Devil’s Garden!

---

===Chinese===

马驮着他信步而行。大贵族坐在鞍上，一脸晦暗，茫然望着云，或望着地。大地、天空，还有他自己的心——身内身外，一切都昏黑荒凉。从前他的灵魂被骄傲塞得满满当当，容不下别的东西；如今骄傲荡然无存，只留下一个可怕的空洞。

走啊，走啊；选路的却是他的马。茫茫林海铺在眼前，他想——林子那边是什么？高山。山那边呢？更高的山。再往后呢？雪峰。而处处都没有他的藏身之所！头一个回合，人人心涣然散尽；昨天还统治着半个特兰西瓦尼亚、堡寨任他调遣的他，今夜竟寻不见一间遮身的小屋。难道就这样把自己交出去受仇人嘲弄，连昂着头、含笑赴死这点可怜的体面都没有？难道像一头被围猎的野兽，死得不明不白？他陷入沉思。死是必然的了，那也要死得像个人。可是怎么个死法？

渐渐地，一个念头在他昏暗的灵魂里透出光来；随着这念头，血色回到他脸上。他缓缓抬起头，那隐秘的念头倏然酿成决断，仿佛心底有个声音在喊——“对！去那儿！去那儿！”他眼里重新有了光，拨转马头朝森林而去，消失在浓密的枝叶之下。

---

暴风雨大作。狂风折断树木。骤雨噼啪砸下，暴涨的山涧咆哮。闪电时时照亮整个地区：雪峰暗下去，黑天亮起来——随后天又黑下去，雪峰又亮起来。

秃岩上零星的灌木，被飓风连斯带晃；远处的松林吼如末日的号角。百兽战栗着伏在穴中，听风暴施威。

高峻不可攀的巉岩遮断天际；远远的、远远的谷底，我们看见一点如辛劳蚂蚁般的骑影，在无路的荒野里挣扎前行。

这样的夜，这样的地方，愿上帝怜悯他！

那是魔鬼花园！

---

===Original===

A gorgeous oriental chamber opens out before us. Round about the walls gleam hundreds of torches; but the ceiling is so lofty that it is invisible, the light of the torches never reaches it. Two rows of columns support the gigantic architrave, slender columns with capitals in the shape of beasts’ heads, as we are wont to see them in ancient Persian temples. Splendid curtains fill up the interstices of the columns. Moorish arabesques adorn the walls; the arched portals are ablaze with gold and malachite. In the centre of the room a lofty red velvet couch rests on four gold griffins with amethyst eyes. In front of the couch is a little ivory table, supported by intertwining silver snakes, and beside the table a golden censer exhales light-blue fragrant clouds of ambergris and aloes. On the couch reclines a sylphlike girl with languishing and yet ardent eyes. A string of pearls, dependent from her neck, draws her light tunic up to her bosom. Her slender form is girdled round the hips by a gorgeous oriental shawl. Her black locks are held together by a golden fillet, which encircles her brows, and the huge diamond clasp of this fillet flashes its myriad blinding rays amidst her dark tresses, like a rainbow condensed into a star gleaming through darkest night.

The girl is alone. Everything around her is motionless. We seem to be in an enchanted fairy palace. Nowhere a sound, a movement.

Who would ever have thought of finding such a magic chamber in the bowels of the earth, six hundred feet within the solid rock, on the surface of which the storm is worrying the hardy shrubs and trees?

It is the crypt of the Devil’s Garden, and the woman, sylph or demon, who inhabits it is Azrael.

How can this woman live here so lonely, so far from everything human?

And yet, why not? She is a whole world, a hell, to herself. Within the resounding walls of the populous harem she felt herself lonely, and she peoples this vast vault with the creations of her own wild fancy. Here she shapes the future, forms endless plans, dreams of battles, of intoxicating love, of more than earthly might, of new realms of which she is the Queen, the Sun surrounded by her starry train.

===Chinese===

一座华丽的东方厅堂在我们面前展开。四壁火炬数百，光焰灼灼；可穹顶高不可见，火光永远够不到它。两列细柱撑起巨大的额枋，柱头雕成兽首，一如古波斯神庙中所见。柱间垂满华美的帷幔。摩尔（Moorish）风格的阿拉伯花纹装点着四壁；拱门金碧辉煌，嵌着孔雀石。厅堂正中，一张高高的红丝绒长榻，架在四只镶紫水晶眼的金狮鹫上。榻前是一张象牙小几，几足是银蛇交缠；几旁一只金香炉，吐出龙涎与沉香的浅蓝香雾。榻上斜倚着一个精灵般苗条的少女，眼波慵倦而炽热。一串珍珠从颈间垂下，把轻薄的束腰外衣拢向胸前。她纤细的身腰，由一条华丽的东方披巾围束在胯上。她的黑发拢在一条束发的金带里，金带环着眉际；带上那枚巨大的钻石搭扣，在乌黑的发间迸射出千万道耀眼的光，像一道彩虹凝成一颗星，在至黑的夜里闪烁。

少女独自一人。周遭万籁俱寂。我们仿佛置身施了魔法的仙宫。处处无声，无动。

谁能想到，在地的腹中、六百尺的岩层之内，竟藏着这样一间魔室——而在岩面之上，风暴正肆虐着坚韧的灌木与林木？

这是魔鬼花园的地宫；住在里面的那个女人——是仙是魔——名叫阿兹拉尔（Azrael）。

这个女人怎能在这里活得下去，如此孤独，离一切人间烟火如此遥远？

可又为何不能？她自己就是一个完整的世界，一座自足的地狱。在人口稠密的后宫那四壁回声之内，她尚且觉得孤独；而在这里，她用自己狂野想象造出的人物，住满这空阔的穹窖。在这里，她塑造未来，编织无穷的计划；梦见战争，梦见醉人的爱，梦见超越人间的权能，梦见一个个新的王国——在那里她是女王，是众星环绕的太阳。

===Original===

Suddenly a light trampling is heard overhead, as if someone were riding over the vaulted roof. Azrael arises and listens. The sound of footsteps is audible in the corridors, and presently three familiar, measured knocks are heard at the doors.

“ ’Tis he!” she whispers; springs from her couch, hastens to the door, draws back the heavy bolts, tears the door violently open, and falls into the arms of him who enters.

“At last! at last!” she murmurs, twining her arms round the man’s neck and pressing her cheeks to his lips.

The man is Denis Banfi.

Sad, speechless, broken as he never was before, he does not even greet the girl as he enters. He seems to freeze, all his limbs are trembling. He has left his tiger-skin outside, but the drenching rain has soaked him through and through.

“Thou art wet to the skin,” says the girl. “Quick! warm thyself. Thou hast come from afar. Thou dost need repose,” and dragging Banfi to her couch, she took off his dolman, covered him with her own costly ermine mantle, placed under his feet soft velvet cushions, which she first warmed over the steaming censer, and pressing the man’s frozen hands to her throbbing bosom, warmed them there.

Yet Banfi remained dumb. Misfortune seemed to be written on his forehead. A far less practised eye, a far less penetrating genius than Azrael’s, could have seen at a glance that he was no longer the haughty magnate he had been, but a fallen viceroy, whose fall was all the greater because he had stood so high; who had come to her, not because he had forsaken everyone, but because everyone had forsaken him; whom not pleasure but despair had brought to this place.

“I have been waiting for thee!” cried the girl, burying her head in Banfi’s bosom, while he played involuntarily with her rich tresses. “To me thy absence is an eternity, thy presence but a fleeting moment.”

Not for all the world would Azrael have let Banfi perceive that she had observed the change in him. She pushed a little round stool in front of the couch, took up her mandolin, and began to sing with a voice of thrilling sweetness one of those improvisations which the ardent imagination of the East brings spontaneously to the lips, striking the while with her fingers wild, fantastic chords.

===Chinese===

忽然，头顶传来轻轻的马蹄声，仿佛有人骑马走过拱顶。阿兹拉尔起身倾听。走廊里响起脚步声；紧接着，门上响起熟悉的三下、不轻不重的叩击。

“是他！”她低语，从榻上一跃而起，奔到门边，抽开沉重的门闩，猛地拉开门，扑进进来的人的怀抱。

“你可来了！可来了！”她喃喃着，双臂缠上那人的脖颈，把自己的脸颊贴向他的唇。

那人是德尼斯·班菲。

他神情惨淡，一言不发，落魄得前所未有；进门时，连招呼也没同姑娘打。他像冻僵了，四肢都在抖。虎皮氅留在了外头，倾盆的雨把他浇了个透心湿。

“你浑身都湿透了，”姑娘说，“快！暖一暖。你从远处来，你该歇歇了。”她把班菲拽到榻上，替他脱下多尔曼（dolman），盖上自己那件贵重的貂皮氅；又把软丝绒坐垫先在腾着热气的香炉上烘暖，垫在他脚下；再把男人冻僵的手按到自己突突跳动的胸口，为他焐暖。

班菲依然一言不发。厄运仿佛写在他额头上。远不如阿兹拉尔那样阅人无数、慧眼如炬的眼睛，也能一眼看出：他不再是那傲岸的大贵族了，而是一位垮了的封疆之主——正因从前站得那么高，跌得才这么重；他到她这里来，不是因为他抛下了所有人，而是因为所有人抛下了他；把他带到此地来的，不是欢乐，是绝望。

“我一直在等你！”姑娘喊道，把头埋进班菲怀里；他不由自主地拨弄着她浓密的鬈发。“对我来说，你不在，是一整个永恒；你在，不过是飞逝的一瞬。”

阿兹拉尔绝不肯让班菲察觉：他的变化，她全看在眼里。她把一张小圆凳推到榻前，取过曼陀林，用一声声令人心颤的甜嗓，唱起东方炽热的想象随口涌出的那种即兴曲，手指同时弹出狂野奇幻的和弦。

===Original===

“If thou hast joy, share it with thy beloved, and thou wilt have so much the more. If thou hast grief, share it with thy beloved, and thou wilt have so much the less.”

Banfi looked at the odalisque with beetling brows.

But Azrael struck fresh chords and began another song﻿—

“False is the world and all that is therein! Every day the sun forsakes the sky. Every day the sea forsakes her shores. Every year the swallow forsakes her nest. But the maiden who loves never forsakes her beloved.”

Still Banfi remained silent. There he sat with staring, bloodshot eyes, his head resting on his elbows, like a poor, mortally-wounded lion.

And again the odalisque sang﻿—

“If choice were thine, which wouldst thou choose﻿—love with hell, or heaven without love?”

Banfi stretched out his arms towards Azrael, and as the odalisque, casting away her mandolin, bent down to kiss his hand, he drew her to his breast, and the odalisque, softly stroking Banfi’s forehead, said﻿—

“What mean these wrinkles on thy noble brow, which I have never seen there before? Vainly do I charm them away with my kisses; they come back again and again. Wait!﻿—I’ll cover them with this diadem. So!﻿—how well a kingly crown becomes thy brow!”

Banfi uttered an inarticulate cry, tore the diadem from his head, and hurled it far away, while with the other hand he roughly repulsed the girl. Every line of his face proclaimed his agony of mind. The odalisque looked into his face and could read there everything which had happened.

This passionate outburst, however, aroused Banfi from out of his dull despondency. He sprang from the couch, resumed with an effort his usual proud, devil-may-care look, and raising the girl into the air cried, with bitter, scornful mirth﻿—

“Bring me wine! Today I’ll make merry! Over our heads the storm is howling﻿—let it howl! We’ll laugh at it, eh! my pretty wench? Today is ours! On this one day we’ll heap together everything which can bring bliss and mad delight, so as to leave nothing for the morrow. Wine and kisses and music﻿—and hellfire!”

The girl skipped away like a chamois, and came back like a Hebe with a large silver salver covered with gold goblets.

“No, not the golden pocals!” cried Banfi. “They won’t break when we dash them against the wall. Serve the wine in Venetian crystals.”

===Chinese===

“你有欢乐，分给心上人，欢乐就更多；你有愁苦，分给心上人，愁苦就更少。”

班菲紧锁双眉，望着那姬妾。

阿兹拉尔却又拨出新调，唱起另一支歌——

“世界虚伪，世间万物皆虚伪！太阳天天抛开天空。大海天天抛开堤岸。燕子年年抛开旧巢。唯有热恋的少女，永不离弃她的爱人。”

班菲依旧沉默。他坐在那里，双眼圆睁、布满血丝，头支在臂肘上，像一头可怜的、受了致命伤的狮子。

姬妾又唱——

“倘若由你挑，你挑哪样——伴着地狱的爱，还是没有爱的天堂？”

班菲朝阿兹拉尔张开双臂；姬妾丢开曼陀林，俯身吻他的手，他把她揽进怀里。她轻抚着班菲的额头，说——

“你高贵的额头上这些皱纹，是从哪儿来的？我从没在那儿见过。我用吻把它们抚平，全不中用；它们一回来再回来。等等！——我拿这顶冠给你盖上。瞧！——王冠戴在你额上多合适！”

班菲发出一声不成腔的喊，把冠冕从头上扯下，远远掼了出去，另一只手粗暴地推开姑娘。他脸上的每一道纹路都写着心头的痛楚。姬妾望着他的脸，把发生的一切都读了出来。

然而这感情的爆发倒把班菲从麻木的颓唐里激醒了。他从榻上一跃而起，勉强找回平日那副骄傲的、满不在乎的神气，把姑娘高举到半空，带着苦涩讥诮的欢快喊道——

“拿酒来！今天我要痛快！头顶上风暴在吼——让它吼去！咱们笑它一笑，嗯！我的俏娘们？今天是咱们的！就这一天，咱们把一切能带来极乐狂喜的东西都堆到一块儿，一点不给明天剩下。美酒、亲吻、音乐——再加地狱之火！”

姑娘岩羚羊似的蹦开了，回来时像位青春女神，捧着一大银盘，盘里满是金杯。

“不，不要金杯！”班菲喊，“往墙上砸它们不碎。酒要斟在威尼斯水晶杯里。”

===Original===

The odalisque obediently brought forth the gorgeously-coloured and gilded Venetian glasses, then so much in vogue, and pushed a broad, short-legged table close to the couch.

“Come, embrace me!” cried Banfi, drawing the girl to his bosom, and gazing into her abysmal black eyes.

“My love is an endless sea,” whispered the girl, her hands resting on Banfi’s shoulder.

“My desire is as hell itself, which drinks to the very dregs!” cried Banfi, embracing the odalisque and pressing a burning kiss on her lips, as if he would have drunk in her very soul.

With that he seized the first glass that came to hand; the wine sparkled in the torchlight. Azrael’s kisses had not yet softened his heart. With bitter scorn he raised the glass, and cried﻿—

“I drink to my friends.”

He drained it to the last drop, and hurled it contemptuously against the wall, so that it was shivered to pieces. Immediately afterwards he seized a second glass﻿—

“I drink to my enemies.”

With a wild peal of laughter he hurled the second glass into the air. In its flight it almost reached the ceiling, but it fell back again on the couch and did not break.

“See, it mocks me and will not break!” exclaimed Banfi, with sparkling eyes.

Azrael sprang up, seized the glass, and crushed it beneath her foot.

In Banfi’s heart the flames of three passions began to mingle﻿—wrath, intoxication, and frantic love.

He raised the third glass to his lips, and while the girl held his body fast embraced, Banfi exclaimed, with flushed face and strident voice﻿—

“I drink to Transylvania.”

He drained the glass, but when he took it from his lips, the smile had frozen on his face, and instead of dashing the glass against the wall, he placed it gently on the table. A cold shudder ran through him at his own words﻿—“I drink to Transylvania.”

He did not remove his hand from the glass, and would shyly have put it aside in a safe place, when the crystal, without any visible cause, suddenly burst in pieces, filling the magnate’s hand with a million fragments.

The diamond ring on his finger had scratched the glass, which, as all badly-cooled crystals are wont to do, shivered instantly at the contact, scattering its sparkling fragments in every direction like a Bologna flask.

Banfi shrank shuddering back at this phenomenon and hid his face in Azrael’s bosom, as if he had seen a portentous enchantment.

===Chinese===

姬妾顺从地捧出那些彩色绚丽、描金错彩的威尼斯玻璃杯——那是当时最时兴的玩意——又把一张宽大的矮脚桌推到榻边。

“来，抱我！”班菲喊，把姑娘拉到怀里，直望进她那双深不见底的黑眼睛。

“我的爱是一片没有尽头的海。”姑娘耳语，双手搭在班菲肩上。

“我的欲是地狱，一滴残渣也不剩地喝干！”班菲喊，搂住姬妾，在她唇上印下燃烧的一吻，仿佛要把她的魂魄也吸进腹中。

说罢他抓起手边的头一杯；酒在火把光里冒泡闪光。阿兹拉尔的吻还没有焐软他的心。他带着苦涩的讥诮举杯，喊道——

“我为我朋友们干杯。”

他一饮而尽，把杯子鄙夷地朝墙上掼去，摔了个粉碎。紧接着又抓起第二杯——

“我为我的仇敌干杯。”

他狂笑一声，把第二杯抛上半空。杯子飞得几乎碰着穹顶，却又落回榻上，没有碎。

“瞧，它嘲弄我，偏不碎！”班菲目光灼灼地喊。

阿兹拉尔跳起来，抓过杯子，一脚踩了个粉碎。

班菲心里，三股情焰开始交织——狂怒、酒意，和疯魔的爱。

他举起第三杯凑到唇边；姑娘紧紧搂着他的身子。班菲涨红着脸，声音尖利地喊——

“我为特兰西瓦尼亚干杯。”

他干了那杯；可杯子离唇时，笑容已在他脸上冻住。他没有把杯子往墙上砸，而是轻轻放到了桌上。自己的那句话让他打了个寒噤——“我为特兰西瓦尼亚干杯。”

他的手没有离开杯子，还想小心翼翼把它挪到稳当的地方，不想那水晶毫无来由地骤然炸裂，大贵族满手全是细碎的碍子。

是他指上的钻戒划了杯壁；淬火不佳的水晶素来如此，一碰即碎，像博洛尼亚烧瓶似的向四面迸溅出闪光的碎片。

见这异象，班菲打战着缩了回去，把脸埋进阿兹拉尔怀里，仿佛撞见了凶兆妖术。

===Original===

The girl, however, impetuously seized her glass and cried exultantly﻿—

“I drink to our love.”

Her voice broke the spell of Banfi’s sobering horror and plunged him into frenzied joy. He caught the slim, supple body of the odalisque in his arms, and pressed her to him with the strength of a boa-constrictor: she was almost stifled in his embrace.

“I know not what you have given me to drink,” stammered Banfi, “but I have lost my head. I am beside myself for love.”

“Then take heed that thou dost not faint. Long hast thou let me languish, and I swore that when next thou camest, to murder thee in thy sleep, so that thou mightest never forsake me more.”

“Oh, do it, do it,” whispered he, and drawing his dagger from his girdle and stretching himself at full length upon the couch, he laid bare his breast with one hand and gave the girl the dagger with the other.

Azrael, with demoniacal ferocity, grasped the dagger by its beryl handle, and threw herself like an armed Fury upon Banfi, who looked at her with a frenzied smile as the sharp edge of the dagger grazed his breast. Then the weapon fell from the hand of the odalisque, and the madly-distended eyes and lips resumed their languishing smile.

“Kill me rather than forsake me,” stammered the girl, embracing Banfi.

“We’ll die together, eh?”

“Yes, yes!”

“Jest not, Azrael. I am ready to do what I say.”

“And I am ready to die,” replied the girl. “Come, I’ll show thee something,”﻿—and with that, drawing aside the carpet, she lifted up a trapdoor, beneath which was visible through the gloom a deeper, lower room, supported by short, stout, arched columns, close beside which a number of large barrels had been placed.

“Yes,” said Banfi, “I know. In that cellar I have hidden the gunpowder which I saved after John Kemeny’s fall.”

“Look at this long nitrous linstock,” said Azrael, drawing up the end of a thick cotton coil out of the cellar; “the barrels are connected with it, and many a time when thou hast been with me have I had the end of this lunt under the cushions of my couch, and held in my couch the torch which was to have kindled it whilst thou wert sleeping with thy head upon my breast, and I lay and listened calmly for the explosion which was to send us both to heaven or to hell.”

“And you were afraid to do it?”

“Not for myself. But I reflected that thou wert not thine own but thy country’s.”

===Chinese===

姑娘却一把抢过自己那杯，扬扬得意地喊——

“我为我们的爱干杯。”

她的声音破开了班菲那渐渐醒来的恐惧，又把他掷进疯狂的欢乐。他把姬妾纤细柔软的身子抄进臂弯，用蟒一般的力气箍紧她：她几乎被勒得窒息。

“不知道你给我灌了什么，”班菲结结巴巴地说，“我的魂丢了。爱得我没了命。”

“那可小心别晕过去。你让我空守了这么久；我发过誓：你再来时，趁你睡着把你杀了，叫你永远没法再抛下我。”

“哦，动手吧，动手吧，”他低语，从腰间抽出匕首，直挺挺舒展开身子躺在榻上，一只手敞开胸膛，另一只手把匕首递给姑娘。

阿兹拉尔带着魔性的凶戾攥住绿玉柄的匕首，像一位持械的复仇女神扑向班菲；刀锋擦着他的胸膛，他却疯狂地含笑望着她。随后，武器从姬妾手中滑落，那双暴突的眼睛和嘴唇，又恢复了慵倦的笑。

“宁可杀了我，别抛下我。”姑娘搂着班菲，喃喃地说。

“咱们死在一块儿，嗯？”

“对，对！”

“不是玩笑，阿兹拉尔。我说到做到。”

“我也死得起。”姑娘答道，“来，给你看样东西。”——说着，她掀开地毯，抬起一扇活板门；门下昏暗里望得见一间更深更矮的地窖，由粗短的拱柱撑着，柱边挨挨放着一排大桶。

“知道，”班菲说，“那窖里藏着我从约翰·凯迈尼（John Kemeny）败亡后保下来的火药。”

“瞧这根长长的火药引线，”阿兹拉尔说着，从窖里拽上一截粗棉芯的头，“桶都连在它上头。多少回你陪着我，这引线的头就掖在我榻上的坐垫底下；点它的火把，也收在我的榻上——趁你把头睡在我胸口，我静静躺着，等那一声把我们俩一同送上天、或者送下地狱的爆响。”

“你没敢下手？”

“不是为我。只是我想，你不是你自己的，是你国家的。”

===Original===

“I belong to no one now.”

“Thy mind was so full of lofty plans. Destiny chose thee to be a Prince among men, a hero among the kings of the earth whose name should fill the pages of history.”

“All that is over now,” cried Banfi, with drunken self-forgetfulness. “I am nobody and nothing. The vault beneath this floor is all that belongs to me. In the world without I am a fugitive and a vagabond.”

“Ha!” hissed Azrael. “Then thy enemies have triumphed over thee?”

“My curse be upon their heads! I had compassion upon them, so I have perished.”

“Is Csaky also among thy persecutors?”

“Yes; he is my most pitiless pursuer.”

“And have all thy faithful friends deserted thee?”

“The fallen has no faithful friends.”

“Thou mightst hire mercenaries and begin the struggle anew. Thou art rich enough.”

“My wealth has gone.”

“Thou mightst beg for help from foreign lands.”

“That would be treason against my country. I have fallen and know what awaits me. I must die. But my enemies shall not triumph at my death as at a festival, or laugh aloud to see me go pale and downcast to my doom. I will die alone.”

“By Allah, thou shalt not die alone! Come, let us fill our glasses. Accursed be the world! we’ll speak of it no more. Come, stifle thy soul in the delirium of joy, and when thy drooping head sinks down upon my breast, I will light the end of this lunt. Thou shalt dream of bliss, of paradise, of kisses ravished and returned; the twofold throbbing of our hearts shall beat the minutes; here below, the stillness of death; there above, the howling of the tempest and of thy foes; and then an earthquaking thunder, rending and scattering the rocks, shall proclaim to heaven and hell that none shall ever find Denis Banfi here on earth again!”

“Azrael, thou art a devil, and I love thee!” cried Banfi, and he clasped the girl in his arms as if she had been a little child.

---

An hour has passed, and the room has grown dark. The torches are expiring. In the huge vaulted chamber no other light is visible but the red vapour streaming from the orifices of the censer, which gleams like a many-eyed monster, and the burning end of the linstock, lit by Banfi in the midst of his mad orgy, crawling slowly along the room like a fiery serpent.

Naught is to be heard in the deep silence but the sighs of two lovers, and the throbbing of two hearts.

Banfi slept long.

===Chinese===

“如今我谁也不属了。”

“你心里曾装满何等宏图。命运拣中你，要你做万人中的亲王、地上君王中的英雄，叫你的名字写满史册。”

“全完了，”班菲带着醉后的忘乎所以喊道，“我什么也不是，什么都没有。这地板底下的地窖，就是我全部的家当。外头的那个世界里，我是个逃犯、流浪汉。”

“哈！”阿兹拉尔嘶声道，“这么说，你的仇人把你踩倒了？”

“咒他们头上去！我心慈了他们一念，就毁了自己。”

“恰基也在害你的人里头？”

“在；数他追我最狠。”

“你那些忠心的朋友，也都散了？”

“落难的人没有忠心的朋友。”

“你可以雇兵买马，重整旗鼓。你的家财尽够。”

“家财没了。”

“你可以向异邦求援。”

“那是叛国。我败了，知道等着我的是什么。我必死。可我的死不能让仇人当节日庆，不能让他们看着我面无人色、垂头丧气地走上绝路而放声大笑。我要独自死。”

“凭真主起誓，你休想独自死！来，把杯子斟满。世界该咒！咱们再不提它。来，把你的灵魂闷死在极乐的迷狂里；等你低垂的头沉到我胸口，我就点着这引线的头。你要梦见至福、天堂、抢来的吻和还回的吻；我们两颗心的双重搏动替我们数着分秒；底下这边，是死一般的寂静；上头那边，是风暴和你的仇敌的咆哮；然后——一声撼天动地的巨响，岩石崩裂四散，向天堂和地狱一同宣告：这世上谁也别想再找到德尼斯·班菲！”

“阿兹拉尔，你是个魔鬼，我爱你！”班菲喊，把姑娘搂进怀里，像搂一个小小的孩子。

---

一个钟头过去了，屋里暗下来。火把将烬。巨大的拱顶厅堂里，唯一可见的光，只剩香炉孔窍里流出的红雾，像一头多眼的怪物幽幽发亮；还有班菲在狂欢极乐中亲手点着的引线头，像一条火蛇，在屋里缓缓爬行。

深深的寂静里，只听得见一对爱人的叹息，和两颗心的搏动。

班菲睡了很久。

===Original===

Suddenly he awoke. Pitch-black darkness surrounded him. It was some time before his reeling brain could realize where he was, or why he was there. He felt an icy wind streaming through the room, but it was only after a long interval that he grasped the fact that a door was open somewhere, and that the cold night air was rushing in from outside.

Gradually the scenes of the bygone night and the vows of death came back to his mind, and he felt that he still lived. “The girl has certainly repented of her wish to die,” thought he, and he began to grope about for her. The couch was empty.

“Azrael! Azrael!” he cried repeatedly; but there was no answer.

At last he tottered to his feet, and snatching some embers from the hearth, lit a torch. The solitary, feeble light did not penetrate far, but as far as it extended Azrael was nowhere to be seen.

The first thing he perceived was the linstock cut in two by a pair of shears.

“Coward soul!” he growled, and, pierced through and through by the air, would have put on his mantle, when a roll of parchment fell at his feet, and picking it up he recognized Azrael’s handwriting, and read as follows﻿—

“My lord, you read not hearts aright. We give our love for our own sakes, but we do not give ourselves for love’s sake. You have frittered away your power, and, deserted by all the world, think to find me faithful who loved your power and that only: I am his who has inherited that power. He who is in the ascendant I adore, but I hate and despise the fallen. Corsar Beg’s fate should have warned you that one day you too might fare like him﻿ ﻿…”

Banfi could not read it to the end. His face grew dark with shame. “To sink so low as this! This wretched slavish soul even while embracing me was devising treachery! And I to wish to spend my last moments in the arms of such a monster﻿—” At that moment he *loathed* himself.

“Cowardice and infamy! A man who has lived as I have lived, to desire such a death! He who has always been wont to meet his foes face to face, to hide himself from them in his last moments!﻿—to hide himself in the arms of a slave! Shame upon him!

===Chinese===

他忽然惊醒。四下漆黑。发晕的脑子过了好一会儿，才弄明白自己身在何处、为何在此。他觉出一股冰冷的风穿堂而过；又过了许久，他才醒悟：不知哪里的门开了，外面的夜寒正灌进来。

昨夜的情景和同死的盟誓，渐渐回到他脑海里；他发觉自己还活着。“姑娘一定是回心转意，不想死了。”他想，便伸手四处摸她。榻是空的。

“阿兹拉尔！阿兹拉尔！”他一声声喊；无人应答。

他终于踉跄起身，从炉膛里夹起余烬，点着一支火把。孤独微弱的光照不多远；可就光所及处，到处都没有阿兹拉尔的影子。

他头一眼看见的，是那根引线——已叫一把剪刀铰成了两段。

“胆小鬼的灵魂！”他咆哮。寒气把他浸了个透；他正要披上大氅，一卷羊皮纸落到脚边。他拾起来，认出是阿兹拉尔的笔迹，读道——

“大人，你读不懂人心。我们把爱给出去，是为我们自己；却不会把自己为爱交出去。你把权势散尽了，又被全世界抛弃，就想在我身上找忠实——可我爱的，从来只是你的权势：如今那权势归了谁，我就是谁的。得势的，我崇拜；失势的，我恨，我鄙夷。科尔萨尔贝伊的下场本该提醒你：你也会有他那样的一天……”

班菲读不到底。羞愧使他的脸黑了下来。“竟堕落到这般地步！这奴才胚子的灵魂，抱着我的时候就在算计背叛！而我，竟想把最后的时光消磨在这样一个怪物的怀抱里——”那一刻，他*憎厌*起自己来。

“怯懦！无耻！一个活得像我这样的人，竟想求这样的死！向来惯于同仇人当面相见的他，临末了竟要躲着他们！——躲进一个女奴的怀抱！可耻啊！”

===Original===

“This lesson has done me good. It was meet that I who could forget a wife who sacrificed herself to deliver me out of the hands of my enemies, should fall into the power of a harlot who would have betrayed me to them. Yet even now it is not too late. My life is forfeit, but at least I can save my honour. None shall be able to boast that he has betrayed me. My enemies shall never say that I hid myself from them and they found me out. I’ll appear before them boldly, as I ought to have done at first.”

Full of this resolution, Banfi went straightway into the secret courtyard, where he had left his horse. He was surprised to find it no longer there. The odalisque had taken it away with her.

He smiled disdainfully.

“What matters it, so long as she has not stolen me also.”

He returned into the rocky chamber, rekindled the lunt, came out, and closing the iron door behind him made his way along the banks of the cold Szamos.

Towards midday he sat down on the bank to rest, and he had scarcely been there a quarter of an hour, when he heard the trampling of horses, and looking up﻿—the bushes completely concealed him﻿—beheld Ladislaus Csaky and Azrael on horseback, side by side, at the head of an armed band. The girl seemed to be pointing out something to Csaky on the rocks above, and the worthy gentleman was beside himself for joy.

Banfi smiled scornfully.

“Poor Tartars!”

As soon as the band had passed by, Banfi continued his journey. He had not gone far when he came upon a poor peasant cleaving wood.

“Dost know whither that armed band has gone?” he asked.

“Yes, sir. They have gone to capture Denis Banfi, on whose head a great price has been set.”

“How much?”

“If a noble capture him he will receive an estate, if a peasant, two hundred ducats.”

“Little enough, but enough for you, I dare say. I am Denis Banfi.”

The peasant took off his cap.

“Does my lord wish to be led anywhither?”

“Lead me to the place where they will pay you two hundred ducats.”

---

A quarter of an hour afterwards a tremendous explosion resounded through the mountains, which shook the earth for half-a-mile around. The enchanted garden of the Gradina Dracului had collapsed into an inaccessible chaos.

Csaky had fortunately lingered on the road, or he and his company would have perished utterly.

===Chinese===

“这一课教训得好。我能忘了那样舍身救我出仇人之手的妻子，就活该落到一个要把由卖给他们的娼妇手里。可如今也还不迟。命是抵定了，荣誉总还救得回来。谁也别想夸口说他出卖了我。我的仇人永远别说不上，是我躲着他们、被他们搜出来的。我要堂堂正正走到他们面前——一开始本就该这样。”

怀着这个决断，班菲径直走进那座秘院——马留在那儿。却惊见马已不在。姬妾连马一并带走了。

他轻蔑地一笑。

“那又何妨，只要她没把我也偷走。”

他回到岩室，重新点燃引线，走出来，掩上铁门，沿着冰冷的索莫什河岸上路了。

近晌午，他在岸边坐下歇脚；不到一刻钟，忽听得马蹄声，抬头一看——灌木把他遮得严实——只见拉斯洛·恰基（Ladislaus Csaky）和阿兹拉尔并辔而行，领着一队武装人马。姑娘似乎正指给恰基看上方岩壁上的什么，那位体面老爷喜得魂都没了。

班菲冷冷一笑。

“可怜的鞑靼人！”

那队人马一过去，班菲便接着赶路。没走多远，遇上一个正在劈柴的穷苦农夫。

“知道那队兵去哪儿了吗？”他问。

“知道，老爷。他们捉德尼斯·班菲去了——他头上悬了大赏。”

“多少？”

“贵族捉着他，赏一座庄园；庄稼人捉着，赏二百达卡。”

“不算多，不过给你想必够了。我就是德尼斯·班菲。”

农夫摘下了帽子。

“大人要小的领上哪儿去吗？”

“领我去他们付你二百达卡的地方。”

---

一刻钟后，一声惊天动地的爆炸响彻群山，方圆半英里大地摇撼。格拉迪纳·德拉库卢伊那座魔园，塌成了一片人迹难通的乱石。

幸亏恰基在路上耽搁了一步，不然他和他的手下都得全军葬送。

===Original===

On returning, he found Banfi already under arrest, and was thus deprived of the glory of having captured his foe with his own hand. He immediately hastened to accost him, and, with exquisite malice, brought with him the odalisque, who looked at Banfi as if she had never seen him before.

Banfi, however, since his voluntary surrender, had quite resumed his former sangfroid, and looking contemptuously over his shoulder at Csaky, said﻿—

“So your Excellency means in future to wear my cast-off clothes, eh?”

At this bitter jest Azrael hissed like a snake upon whose tail one has suddenly trodden, whilst Csaky blushed up to his ears and tried hard to smile.

“Does your Excellency desire any favour from me?” asked Csaky presently, with insulting commiseration.

“None from *your* Excellency. I came here of my own free will, and have been arrested I know not why. My wife, therefore, can now be set free.”

“So at last we begin to whine for our wife, eh?”

“On the contrary. So far from wishing to meet her, I desire that as soon as I am put in prison she should be let go.”

“It shall be as you desire, my lord!” replied Csaky, with ironical benevolence.

Banfi requited him with a look of the most withering contempt, and turning to the jailers entered into conversation with them: the magnates he no longer regarded.

---

When Teleki heard of the capture of Banfi, he ordered him to be sent at once to Bethlen Castle, to make the world believe that the anti-Banfi faction was headed, not by him, but by Beldi, to whom the castle belonged.

On his way thither, the captive magnate learnt that his consort had already been released, and thus relieved of his one remaining anxiety, cared little for the rest.

On reaching Bethlen Castle he was received by the Rev. Stephen Pataky, Rector of Klausenburg, to whom he cried jocosely﻿—

“So they’ve appointed you my father confessor, eh?”

Pataky wept bitterly, but Banfi only smiled.

The jailer conducted Banfi up the steps with every demonstration of respect.

Banfi turned round to him.

“I hope you will let Reverend Master Pataky remain with me all the time?” said he.

Pataky was understood to say through his sobs﻿—

“Truly your Excellency will find far better company awaiting you than any my poor self can offer.”

===Chinese===

回来时，他发现班菲已经被捕，亲手擒敌的光荣就此落了空。他连忙抢上前去搭话，还心怀歹毒的精巧，把那姬妾也带来了——她望着班菲，像从没见过这个人。

而班菲自投罗网之后，早已完全恢复了往日的镇定；他轻蔑地回头瞥了恰基一眼，说——

“这么说，大人往后打算穿我穿剩的衣裳了，嗯？”

这句毒话出口，阿兹拉尔像一条猝然被人踩了尾巴的蛇，嘶嘶地叫；恰基红到耳根，还强撑着笑。

“大人可有什么要托我办的？”恰基过了一会儿问，怜悯得咄咄逼人。

“*你*大人跟前，一无所求。我是自己甘愿来的，被抓得莫名其妙。如此，我妻子可以放了。”

“到底开始哭起老婆来了，嗯？”

“恰恰相反。我不但不想见她，还巴望我一进监狱，立刻放她走。”

“如你所愿，大人！”恰基答道，恩赐得阴阳怪气。

班菲用一眼最能把人冻僵的轻蔑回敬了他，随即转过身去同狱卒攀谈：贵族们，他已不再放在眼里。

---

泰莱基听说班菲被擒，即刻下令把他送往贝特伦城堡——好让世人以为，倒班菲一党的首领不是他，而是城堡的主人贝尔迪。

被囚的大贵族在路上听说妻子已经获释，心头最后一桩牵挂落地，其余的事便都无所谓了。

到了贝特伦城堡，迎接他的是克劳森堡的司铎帕塔基·伊什特万神父（Rev. Stephen Pataky）；他朝神父打趣道——

“他们派你当我的听告解神父了，嗯？”

帕塔基哭得伤心，班菲只是微笑。

狱卒恭恭敬敬地把班菲引上台阶。

班菲回过头来。

“望你让帕塔基神父大人一直陪着我？”他说。

帕塔基哽咽着说了句什么，人们听出是——

“说真的，大人，等着您的同伴，远胜过鄙人这点微末的陪伴。”

===Original===

Banfi, not knowing what to say to this, only shrugged his shoulders and hastened towards the door of his prison, but remained standing on the threshold transfixed with astonishment. In the room was a lady in deep mourning, who turned very pale on perceiving him, and clung to the table unable to utter a word.

Banfi felt all his blood rush to his heart. The next moment he darted impetuously forward and cried﻿—

“My wife! Margaret!”

The lady threw herself upon her husband’s breast and sobbed aloud.

“What! have they not released you?” inquired Banfi anxiously.

“I would not be released,” answered Margaret. “How *could* I forsake you in your prison?”

The tears came to Banfi’s eyes. Speechless he sank to the ground, and covered her hands with glowing kisses.

“While we were what the world calls happy we might avoid each other,” said Margaret, with a choking voice, “but misfortune has brought us together again,” and she bowed her head to kiss her husband’s forehead.

Banfi fell senseless at her feet. It was more than even his strong soul could bear.

===Chinese===

班菲不知怎么接这话，只耸了耸肩，快步走向牢门——却在门槛上钉住了，惊得目瞪口呆。屋里立着一位浑身重孝的夫人，一看见他，脸色煞白，扶住桌子，一句话也说不出。

班菲觉出全身的血一齐涌向心口。下一刻，他一头抢上前去，喊道——

“我的妻子！玛格丽特！”

夫人扑到丈夫怀里，放声大哭。

“怎么！他们没放你？”班菲急切地问。

“我不肯被放，”玛格丽特答道，“你身在牢里，我*怎么*能丢下你？”

泪水涌上班菲的眼眶。他一言不发地跪倒在地，把她一双手盖满了滚烫的吻。

“从前世人说我们幸福的时候，我们倒可以互相躲避，”玛格丽特哽咽着说，“如今是厄运让我们重聚了。”她低下头，去吻丈夫的额头。

班菲在她脚边昏了过去。这连他坚强的灵魂也承受不起。

## X / 十
===Original===

The Sentence

The Diet, hastily summoned to Fehervár, strongly disapproved of the secret proceedings against Banfi. Paul Beldi was the first to declare that even if Banfi could be arrested by means of a league, a Diet was the only tribunal which could try him, and insisted that he should have every opportunity of defending himself.

The Prince came to the Diet with red eyes, an aching head, and a very irritable temper﻿—the usual witnesses of a drunken debauch.

Teleki, finding the Diet beyond his control, got Apafi to dissolve it, by persuading him that if Banfi were brought before it he would escape altogether, and even turn the two-edged sword of justice against the Prince himself.

In the Privy Council itself, Kozma Horvath’s opposition to the extrajudicial prosecution was all in vain. The league drew up thirty-seven articles of accusation against Banfi, and the magnate was impeached.

Most of these articles were so utterly frivolous as to need no reply. Banfi’s real offence was his pretension to the throne, and this they dared not bring forward at all.

Banfi manfully replied on every count. In vain. Defend himself as he might, his adversaries knew only too well how much they had offended him: they could not afford to let him live.

The matter came to the vote.

Banfi was condemned to death.

On the day when this took place, no one could get at the Prince except the members of the league, who were constantly going in and out of Apafi’s apartments with hasty steps and eager faces.

Towards evening they succeeded in bringing the besotted Prince to sign the sentence. It was no longer possible to recognize in the spectre-haunted drunkard the mild and gentle Prince, who had had a tear for the sorrows of the meanest of his servants.

Saddled horses and long rows of carriages had been standing before the castle gates since midday. Suddenly Ladislaus Csaky came very hastily out of the castle with a document hidden in the folds of his pelisse, and calling for his horse, mounted, nodded significantly to the other gentlemen who had followed him out, and galloped away. The other gentlemen thereupon leapt into their carriages, or on to their horses, with as much expedition as if someone was pursuing them, and exchanging hurried whispers, decamped so swiftly that in a few moments the Prince was left entirely alone.

===Chinese===

判决

匆匆召集到费海尔瓦尔（Fehervár）的议会（Diet），强烈反对对班菲的密室经营。保罗·贝尔迪（Paul Beldi）第一个申明：纵然可以借同盟之力逮捕班菲，能审讯他的法庭也只有议会一个；并坚持必须给他一切为自己辩护的机会。

亲王到议会时双眼通红、头疼欲裂、脾气暴躁——都是酒后纵饮的老见证。

泰莱基见议会不听摆布，便撺掇阿帕菲把它解散，说动他的理由是：班菲一旦被押到议会面前，必会全身而脱，甚至会掉转正义这柄双刃剑，刺向亲王本人。

在枢密院（Privy Council）内部，科兹马·霍尔瓦特（Kozma Horvath）反对法外追诉，也是枉然。同盟给班菲拟了三十七条罪状，这位大贵族（magnate）遭到弹劾。

这些条目大多轻薄得根本不值一驳。班菲真正的罪名，是觊觎王座——而这一条，他们根本不敢拿出来。

班菲逐条做了堂堂正正的答辩。徒劳。他怎样自辩都无济于事：他的对头们心里太清楚他们得罪他有多深——留他不得。

表决的时候到了。

班菲被判处死刑。

判决落定的那一天，除了同盟中的人，谁也见不着亲王一面；这些人脚步匆匆、神色急切，在阿帕菲的居所里进进出出。

挨到傍晚，他们终于哄得那烂醉的亲王在判决书上签了字。在这具鬼影缠身的酒鬼身上，再也认不出那位连最卑微的仆人受了苦都要陪着落泪的温厚亲王了。

从正午起，鞍马和长长一溜马车就候在城堡门外。忽然，拉斯洛·恰基（Ladislaus Csaky）行色匆匆地出了城堡，一份文书藏在他的皮裘褶子里；他唤过自己的马，翻身上鞍，朝跟出来的众老爷意味深长地一点头，纵马而去。其余老爷们随即钻进各自的马车、或跃上马背，那急切劲儿倒像背后有人追赶似的；交换了几句仓促的耳语，便一哄而散——不多时，亲王身边便走得干干净净。

===Original===

Teleki was the last who quitted him. The Prince accompanied the minister to the very end of the antechamber. Black care was written in his face. He would hardly let Teleki go.

Teleki coldly withdrew his hand from the Prince’s grasp.

“You have no need to brood over it, sir. It is not a question of the life of a man, but of the welfare of a state. If my own neck had stood in the way, I would have said, Hew it off! I say the same when it is another’s.”

With that he took his leave.

Apafi could not remain in his room. He was obliged to go out into the fresh open air. Inside something seemed to choke him, the air was so oppressive﻿—or was it his own conscience? He went into the garden. The cool night air soothed his throbbing head; the sight of the starry heaven did good to his darkened soul. Leaning over the balcony, he looked amazedly out into the quiet night, as if he expected a star larger than all the rest to fall from heaven, or someone miles and miles away to call him by name.

Suddenly a scream fell on his ear.

He looked around with a shudder, and terror made him speechless﻿—before him stood his consort, whom his counsellors had kept away from him for weeks.

The moment the last magnate had departed, her own faithful servants told her that the Prince had signed the death-warrant, and the terrified woman, breaking through the castle guards, rushed after Apafi, found him in the garden, seized him roughly, and shrieking rather than speaking in her agitation, exclaimed﻿—

“Oh, accursed, accursed wretch! Thou hast shed innocent blood!”

Apafi tried to avoid his wife. He feared her.

“What do you want with me?” he asked in a hollow voice. “What do you mean?”

“You have signed Banfi’s death-warrant.”

“I!” cried Apafi feebly, trying to catch hold of his wife’s hand.

“Away with that hand, monster! It is stained with my kinsman’s blood.”

“Then you don’t consent to it?” stammered the abject creature. “Neither did I, but the magnates constrained me.”

The Princess smote her hands together, and looked at her consort despairingly.

“You have brought blood on our family! You have brought a curse on the land and on me! Oh, why did I not let you perish in the hands of the Tartars? Where you are concerned virtue itself becomes a sin.”

Apafi was crushed. Alone with his wife, he was something less than a man.

===Chinese===

泰莱基是最后一个离开的。亲王把这位首相一直送到前厅尽头。他脸上刻着黑色的忧惧，几乎不肯放泰莱基走。

泰莱基冷冷地把自己的手从亲王的把握里抽了回来。

"殿下用不着为此郁结于心。这不是一条人命的事，是一个国家安危的事。倘若挡道的是我自己的脖子，我也会说：砍了它！换了别人的，我照样说。"

说罢，他告辞而去。

阿帕菲在自己的房里待不住。他不得不到外面透透新鲜空气。屋里仿佛有什么东西掐着他的喉咙，空气闷得叫人透不过气——或者，掐着他的是他自己的良心？他走进花园。清凉的夜气抚慰他突突直跳的头颅；星空的景象慰藉他晦暗的灵魂。他凭倚着阳台，惊怔地望向静谧的夜，仿佛在等一颗比所有星辰都大的星从天上坠落，或是等什么远在千里之外的人唤他的名字。

忽然，一声尖叫落进他的耳朵。

他战栗着回过头，恐惧弄得他说不出话——面前立着他的夫人，他的重臣们已把她隔开他好几个星期了。

最后一位大贵族一走，她自家的忠仆便告诉她：亲王签下了死刑令。吓坏了的妇人推开城堡卫队，追着阿帕菲奔来，在花园里找到他，一把狠狠攣住他，激动得与其说是说话不如说是尖叫，喊道——

"哦，你这该诅咒、该诅咒的东西！你流了无辜者的血！"

阿帕菲想躲开妻子。他怕她。

"你要我怎么样？"他声音空洞地问，"你这话什么意思？"

"你签了班菲的死刑令。"

"我！"阿帕菲有气无力地喊，想抓住妻子的手。

"拿开那只手，恶魔！它沾着我亲人的血。"

"那么你不同意？"这可怜虫结结巴巴地说，"我也不同意，是大贵族们逼我的。"

亲王夫人双掌一拍，绝望地望着丈夫。

"你把血债引进了我们的家门！你把诅咒加给了国土、加给了我！哦，当年我为什么不在鞑靼人手里由着你死？牵涉到你，连德行本身都成了罪过。"

阿帕菲被击垮了。单独同妻子在一起时，他连个男人都算不上。

===Original===

“I did not wish to kill him,” he blurted out, “nor do I now; and if you wish it, I’ll reprieve him. Here, take my signet-ring. Send a horseman after Csaky to Bethlen Castle. Reprieve your cousin and leave me in peace.”

“What ho, there! Who is without?” shrieked the Princess.

The domestic servants came pouring in, headed by the pantler.

“Take four of the Prince’s swiftest horses with you,” cried Anna, as she wrote out the pardon with her own hand and made her husband sign and seal it. “Take this letter and hasten to Bethlen Castle. If one of the horses falls under you, take the others. Stop not an instant on the road! A man’s life is in your hands!”

The grooms led forward the swift horses; the pantler swung himself into the saddle, and, leading the other three horses by the bridles, galloped away.

The Princess impatiently followed him with her eyes till he was out of sight, and then went up to her room again; but unable to rest there long, she came down once more, sent for her faithful old servant Andrew, and giving him an old piece of green velvet,55 set him on horseback and sent him after the pantler.

“If the Prince’s reprieve arrives too late, this will be a cerecloth wherein to wrap the murdered man.”

---

The same hour, perhaps at the selfsame moment, Paul Beldi called his chief groom, bade him mount his swiftest horse, ride to Bethlen Castle, and inform the castellan there that he would cut his head off if the slightest harm happened to Banfi at Bethlen. He too dared not face his wife at that moment.

---

The same hour, perhaps at the selfsame moment, Michael Teleki pressed the hand of his future son-in-law Tököli, and whispered in his ear, “We are a step nearer.” And beneath the pressure of the youth’s iron hand, the engagement ring which knitted him to Teleki’s daughter snapped in two, and Teleki took it as an omen56 that, one day, the hand of this youth would be stronger than his own.

---

That night all Transylvania was greatly disturbed. Farkas Bethlen could not sleep in his bed all night. Stephen Apor was so unwell that he had to send for his confessor, and Kornis lost himself so completely on his way home that he was forced to sleep in his carriage.

===Chinese===

"我并不想杀他，"他脱口而出，"现在也不想；你若愿意，我这就赦他。喏，拿我的印戒去。派个骑手去追恰基，赶到贝特伦城堡（Bethlen Castle）。赦了你的那位亲人，也让我清静清静。"

"来人哪！外面是谁？"亲王夫人尖声喊。

仆役们涌了进来，为首的是司膳官。

"带上亲王最快的四匹马。"安娜喊道，一面亲手写好赦令，让丈夫签名用印。"带上这封信，火速赶往贝特伦城堡。一匹马累倒了就换其余的。路上片刻不停！一条人命就在你们手里！"

马夫们牵出快马；司膳官翻身上鞍，手里牵着另外三匹马的缰绳，飞驰而去。

亲王夫人焦灼地目送他远去，直到人影不见，才回楼上房里；可在那儿也安坐不了多久，又走下来，唤来忠心的老仆安德鲁（Andrew），给他一块旧的绿天鹅绒55，打发他上马，随司膳官之后追去。

"倘若亲王的赦令到得太迟，这块布就拿来作裹尸布（cerecloth），裹那位被杀的人。"

---

同一个时辰，或许正是同一刻，保罗·贝尔迪唤来他的马夫头目，命他骑上最快的马奔赴贝特伦城堡，告知那里的城堡总管：班菲在贝特伦若损伤一根毫毛，就砍下他的脑袋。这一刻，他也不敢面对自己的妻子。

---

同一个时辰，或许正是同一刻，米哈伊·泰莱基（Michael Teleki）握住他未来女婿托克利的手，对他耳语道："我们又近了一步。"在少年铁钳般的手劲下，把他同泰莱基之女（Teleki's daughter）联结在一起的订婚戒指"咔"地断成两截；泰莱基把它当作一个兆头56：有朝一日，这少年的手会比他自己的手更有力。

---

那一夜，整个特兰西瓦尼亚（Transylvania）人心惶惶。法尔卡什·贝特伦（Farkas Bethlen）彻夜难眠。伊什特万·阿波尔（Stephen Apor）难受得不得不请忏悔神父来；科尔尼斯（Kornis）回家路上竟走迷了道，只得在马车里过夜。

===Original===

And what was going on in heaven? Towards midnight a storm arose, the like of which the oldest men could not call to mind. The lightning set forests and castles on fire; the falling clouds drove the rivers out of their beds. The alarm bells resounded everywhere. God sat in judgment over the land that night. The whole population was sleepless.

---

Only the reconciled consorts slept calmly.

With one arm under her husband’s head and the other embracing him, the pale and fragile lady fell asleep. At times she wept in her dreams, and her tears fell on the pillow. She was dreaming of her happy bridal days, and of that sweet moment when she had laid her first and only child in her husband’s arms, and she pressed him more closely to her, while he lay sleeping there so calmly, at enmity with the world, but reconciled to himself and to the better-half of his soul. Happiness, which had fled him in his palace, sought him out in his dungeon.

The night lamp cast its pale rays on the sleeping forms.

---

Through that terrible night, four horsemen, scarcely a thousand paces apart, are galloping at full speed towards Bethlen Castle. During the lightning flashes they sometimes catch a glimpse of each other, and then each of them digs his spurs more deeply into his horse’s sides.

The first horseman reaches the castle gate and winds the signal horn. The drawbridge sinks groaning down; the horseman springs into the courtyard and places a letter in the hands of the flurried castellan. It is Paul Beldi’s messenger.

The horseman who next arrives at the castle orders the gates to be opened in the name of the Prince. He hands the castellan a second letter. It is Ladislaus Csaky.

The castellan grows pale as he reads this letter.

“My lord,” says he, “I have just received a message from Paul Beldi, threatening us with death in case any harm befalls the prisoner.”

“You have your choice,” answered Csaky. “If you obey me, Beldi may perhaps cut off your head tomorrow; but if you don’t obey me, I’ll cut off your head myself this instant.”

The trembling castellan bowed submission.

“Up with the drawbridge!” commanded Csaky. “None must enter this castle without my permission. Whoever acts against my orders is a dead man!”

---

===Chinese===

天上又在做什么呢？将近午夜，起了一场风暴，其猛烈连最年长的老人也记不起先例。闪电点燃了森林和城堡；倾泻的乌云把江河赶出了河床。报警的钟声四处回荡。那一夜，上帝坐堂，审判这片土地。举国无人入眠。

---

只有重归于好的那对夫妇安睡着。

苍白孱弱的夫人一条手臂枕着丈夫的头，另一条搂着他，睡着了。梦里她时时哭泣，泪水落在枕上。她正梦见自己幸福的新婚岁月，梦见把头一个也是唯一的孩子放进丈夫怀抱的那一甜蜜时刻；她把他搂得更紧。他躺在那里睡得那样安稳——与全世界为敌，却同自己、同他灵魂的另一半和解了。在他的宫殿里躲开他的幸福，到他的囚牢里寻他来了。

夜灯把苍白的光投在这对睡影上。

---

穿过那可怕的黑夜，四名骑手前后相隔不过千步，正全速飞驰，赶往贝特伦城堡。闪电亮起的一瞬，他们偶尔瞥见彼此的身影，于是各自把马刺更深地磕进马的胁腹。

第一个骑手赶到城堡门前，吹响号角。吊桥呻吟着放下；骑手跃进院子，把一封信塞进慌了手脚的城堡总管手里。这是保罗·贝尔迪的信使。

第二个赶到城下的骑手以亲王之命叫开城门。他递给城堡总管第二封信。来者正是拉斯洛·恰基本人。

总管读着信，脸都白了。

"大人，"他说，"我方才接到保罗·贝尔迪的信，威胁说囚犯若有闪失，就要我们的命。"

"你自己挑吧。"恰基答道，"听我的，贝尔迪兴许明天砍你的头；不听我的，我现在就亲手砍了你的头。"

发抖的总管躬身从命。

"吊桥拉起来！"恰基下令，"未经我允许，谁也不许进这座城堡。违我令者，死！"

---

===Original===

The spouses lay tranquilly sleeping in each other’s arms. A minute later the door creaked on its hinges, and the Rev. Stephen Pataky, tearful and terrified, entered the dungeon. His heart died within him when he saw the consorts sleeping so calmly side by side.

He stepped up to Banfi to rouse him. As he touched his hand, Banfi awoke, and perceiving Pataky, who could not speak for emotion, tried to disengage his head from his wife’s encircling arm without awakening her. At that very moment Lady Banfi opened her eyes. Pataky, wishing to conceal the fatal message from her, addressed Banfi in the Latin tongue﻿—

“*Surge Domine! sententia lethalis adest!*”57

Lady Banfi, terrified by these mysterious words, the meaning of which Pataky’s face so ill concealed, asked in mortal fear what was the matter.

“Nothing, my darling! nothing!” said Banfi, embracing her with a tender smile. “A pressing message which I must attend to at once. I’ll be back again soon! Lie down and sleep gently!”

With these words he persuaded his wife to fall back upon her pillow, kissed her repeatedly with great tenderness, and soothed her caressingly between each kiss﻿—“My soul! my delight! my love! my heaven!”

The wife little suspected that this was the parting kiss of a man about to meet his doom; Banfi looked at her so smilingly, feigning a joyful countenance as he stood on the threshold of death.

Then the castle horn again sounded. The Princess’s first messenger had arrived, and demanded admittance in her Highness’s name.

Csaky rushed hastily upstairs, and just as Banfi, after half reassuring his consort, was about to quit her, suddenly burst open the door, and cried﻿—

“Why so long a leave-taking? Get ready! The sentence stays for execution!”

Lady Banfi with a piercing scream rose from her couch, and stretching out both her arms towards Banfi, gazed speechlessly at him for a moment, then, clutching at her heart, fell back dead upon her pillow with wide-open eyes.

Banfi looked at his enemy with the bitterness of death, his streaming eyes hurled more curses at him than any lip could have uttered.

“Base, cowardly wretch!” he moaned, “was it then part of your mandate to murder my wife also?”

Csaky turned his head away, and said in a hoarse voice﻿—

“Hasten! the time is short!”

===Chinese===

夫妇俩还安静地相拥睡着。一分钟后，门轴吱呀一响，帕塔基·伊什特万神父（Rev. Stephen Pataky）泪眼汪汪、满脸惊惶地走进囚室。看见这对夫妇如此安详地并头而眠，他的心凉了半截。

他走上前唤醒班菲。手一碰，班菲就醒了；他看见激动得说不出话的帕塔基，便想在不惊醒妻子的前提下，把头从她环抱的手臂里轻轻抽出来。恰在此刻，班菲夫人睁开了眼睛。帕塔基想对她瞒下那致命的口信，便用拉丁语对班菲说——

"*Surge Domine! sententia lethalis adest!*（起来吧，大人！死判已至！）57"

这几句神秘的话，帕塔基的脸又遮掩不住其含义，班菲夫人吓得魂不附体，惊恐万状地问出了什么事。

"没什么，亲爱的！没什么！"班菲带着温柔的笑拥住她，"一桩急事，我得马上去料理。很快就回来！躺下，安心睡吧！"

他就这样哄得妻子躺回枕上，一次又一次温存地吻她，在每个吻的间隙爱抚地宽慰她——"我的灵魂！我的欢喜！我的爱！我的天！"

妻子哪里疑心，这竟是一个赴死之人的诀别之吻；站在死亡门槛上的班菲望着她，笑得那样欢，装出一副喜洋洋的面容。

这时，城堡的号角又响了。亲王夫人的第一路信使已到，以殿下之名要求入城。

恰基急奔上楼；班菲刚把妻子安抚了个大概、正要离开她，恰基猛地撞开门，喊道——

"道什么别这么半天？准备！判决即行执行！"

班菲夫人发出一声刺耳的尖叫，从榻上坐起，朝班菲伸出双臂，无言地凝望了他片刻，随即抓住心口，睁着双眼，死去在她枕上。

班菲用死一般的怨毒望着他的仇人；他涕泪横流的双眼，甩给对方的咒骂，比任何嘴唇能说出的都多。

"卑鄙的懦夫！"他呻吟道，"你的差事里，还包括谋害我的妻子么？"

恰基把头扭向一边，哑着嗓子说——

"快点！时间不多了！"

===Original===

“Short for me, but it shall be long for you! For a time is coming when you will curse the day of your birth, and will not be able to die as calmly as I do!﻿—Leave me!﻿—I would fain pray; but I cannot call upon my God while you are nigh!”

Csaky, overcome despite himself, quitted the room.

Banfi laid his hand on his forehead and prayed.

Outside the heavens were thundering.

“O God! who dost thunder on high, take my blood as a sacrifice for my sins, but let not a drop of it fall on the heads of those who shed it! Suffer not my native land to pay the price of my blood! Guard this poor land from every ill! Visit not this people in Thy anger, but be their refuge and their sure defence in the evil day! Forgive my enemies my death, as I forgive them!”

The thunder roared terribly. God was wroth that day. He would not hearken to such a prayer.

“Is your Excellency ready?” inquired Csaky impatiently, whilst the Princess’s messengers hammered furiously at the gates, and demanded instant admission.

Banfi stepped up to his lifeless consort and kissed her cold, pale face for the last time; then, turning calmly to Csaky, he said﻿—

“Yes; I am ready now!”

---

A quarter of an hour later Csaky admitted the messengers.

“What do you bring?” he asked the pantler.

“The Prince’s pardon for the prisoner.”

“You are too late!﻿—And you?”

“A cerecloth for the corpse!”

“You have brought it very opportunely.”

The highest head of the Transylvanian nobility had already fallen in the dust.

---

The tragedy ends with the hero’s death.

The tide of history brings other shapes and other leaders to the surface. The fate, the fashion, and the history of Transylvania are no longer the same.58 The sword-stroke which slew Banfi cut short an epoch only half begun. The body of that dominating form reposes in the crypt of the church at Bethlen, and no one has inherited his spirit.

But the chronicles say that whenever danger threatens Transylvania, the blood of the buried patriot flows from his simple tomb, a terror to the people, and a wonder to the world.

===Chinese===

"对我不多，对你却长着呢！总有一天，你会咒骂自己出世的那个日子，而且到那时，你想死得像我这样平静，也办不到！——出去！——我要祈祷；可你在这儿，我没法呼唤我的上帝！"

恰基不由自主地被慑住了，退出房去。

班菲把手放在额头，祈祷起来。

外面，天雷正轰鸣。

"哦，上帝！你在高天之上打雷。收下我的血，作我罪愆的祭品吧，只是别让它溅落在杀人者的头顶！别让我的祖国偿还我血的代价！护佑这可怜的国土免遭一切灾殃！不要在震怒中降罚这方百姓，而在那凶险之日，做他们的避难所和坚固的屏障！饶恕我的仇人害死我，如同我饶恕他们！"

雷声轰然怒吼。那一天，上帝震怒了。他不肯垂听这样的祈祷。

"大人预备好了么？"恰基不耐烦地问；这时，亲王夫人的信使们正疯狂捶打城门，要求立即放行。

班菲走到没了气息的妻子跟前，最后一次吻了她冰冷苍白的脸；然后平静地转向恰基，说——

"好了；我这就准备停当！"

---

一刻钟后，恰基放信使们进来了。

"你们带来的是什么？"他问司膳官。

"亲王给囚犯的赦令。"

"你来迟了！——你呢？"

"给死者的一块裹尸布！"

"你倒来得正是时候。"

特兰西瓦尼亚贵族中最高贵的头颅，已经委于尘土。

---

悲剧以英雄之死落幕。

历史的潮水把别的身影、别的首领推上浪尖。特兰西瓦尼亚的命运、风尚与历史，从此换了模样58。斩落班菲的那一剑，砍断了一个才进行到一半的时代。那雄杰的躯骸安卧在贝特伦教堂的地下墓室里，而他的精神，再无一人承继。

可编年史记载：每当危险威胁特兰西瓦尼亚，那长眠爱国者的血，便从他朴素的墓中流出——于本邦是惊惶，于世界是奇观。
